Q. Tell me about your roles and responsibilities in your project? Have you created framework from scratch? Explain architecture

=> Based on your resume, for an Automation Engineer interview, I would answer this in a way that highlights your framework development, Python automation, Selenium/Playwright, PUMBA/SIMBA, API automation, and embedded/connected-services experience.

Interview Answer

“In my current role as a Software Automation & Development Engineer at Forvia Faurecia, my responsibilities are a combination of automation development, software integration testing, and framework development.

I work mainly on embedded and connected systems, where I perform SIT, sanity, regression, integration, and system-level testing. I have worked on projects such as McLaren, ESS+, and Technobricks, and my automation responsibilities include setting up the test environment, executing test cases, validating results, and generating logs and reports.

From the automation perspective, I have worked extensively with Python, Selenium, Playwright, TestNG, POM, REST Assured, and BDD. I have also worked with internal simulation frameworks such as PUMBA and SIMBA, where I automated the build, execution, and management of test cases.

Yes, I have developed automation frameworks and modules from scratch. One example is the Connected Services automation framework. I was involved in designing the framework structure, setting up the test environment, implementing reusable automation components, executing test scenarios, and validating the results. I also worked on automating certificate creation and testing, which helped make the process more reliable and scalable.

At a high level, I follow a layered architecture. The test layer contains the actual test cases and business scenarios. Below that, I have reusable automation or page/component layers, which contain common operations and application interactions. Then I have a utility layer for things such as configuration, logging, JSON handling, file operations, and common reusable functions. For API or system communication, I keep separate modules for API calls, socket communication, or hardware interaction.

I also focus heavily on logging and debugging. For one of my automation solutions, I developed an advanced logging mechanism that captured synchronized serial and video logs during test execution, which required multithreading and I/O handling.

I have also developed REST API automation for defect creation, including authentication, JSON processing, and error handling, and Selenium-based automation for workflows on the Leshan server.

Overall, my responsibility is not just writing test scripts. I focus on building reusable, maintainable, scalable automation frameworks, integrating them with the test environment, analyzing failures, and improving execution efficiency.”**

Q. Which design pattern you have used in framework ?
=> For your resume, the best and safest answer is Page Object Model (POM), especially because your skills explicitly mention Selenium WebDriver, TestNG, and POM Framework.

Interview Answer

“The main design pattern I have used in my automation framework is the Page Object Model, or POM.

The main purpose of POM is to separate the test logic from the application UI interaction logic. I create a separate class for each page or component, where I keep the locators and methods related to that page.

For example, for a Login page, I would have a LoginPage class containing the username, password, and login button locators, along with methods such as enterUsername(), enterPassword(), and clickLogin().

Then my test class only focuses on the business flow, such as login with valid credentials, instead of directly interacting with locators.

This makes the framework reusable, maintainable, and easier to update. For example, if the login button locator changes, I only need to update it in the LoginPage class rather than modifying multiple test cases.

Along with POM, I have also used reusable utility components and a layered framework structure for configuration, logging, API handling, test execution, and reporting.”**

If they ask: “Why did you choose POM?”

Give these 5 points:

Maintainability – UI changes are handled in one place.
Reusability – Page methods can be reused across multiple tests.
Separation of concerns – Test logic and UI interaction are separated.
Readability – Tests look more like business scenarios.
Scalability – Easier to add new pages and test cases.
Very likely follow-up

Interviewer: “Apart from POM, which other design patterns have you used?”

For your profile, I would answer carefully:

“POM is the primary design pattern I have directly used in my Selenium automation. In addition, I have applied framework-level concepts such as reusable utility classes, separation of concerns, and common driver/configuration management. I would not claim a specific GoF pattern unless I have actually implemented it in the project.”

That last sentence is important—don't name Singleton, Factory, Strategy, etc. just because they are common interview answers. If the interviewer asks specifically about Singleton or Factory, I can prepare the answer based on your actual framework experience.

==================================================================================================

Q : Explain how do you pick which test cases to automate ?
==>  “I don’t automate every test case. I first identify the test cases where automation provides maximum value and is stable enough to maintain. I usually consider the following factors:

1. Repetitive test cases – If a test needs to be executed frequently, such as login, checkout, or regression scenarios, I prefer automation.

2. Regression test cases – Test cases that need to be executed after every build or release are good candidates for automation.

3. Stable functionality – I prioritize features whose requirements and UI are relatively stable. Automating a constantly changing feature can create unnecessary maintenance.

4.Data-driven scenarios – If the same functionality needs to be tested with multiple sets of data, automation is very useful. For example, testing login with different valid and invalid credentials.

5. Complex workflows – Long end-to-end scenarios involving multiple steps are good candidates because manual execution is time-consuming and error-prone.

6. High-risk or business-critical functionality – For example, payment, checkout, order creation, or authentication flows.

7. Cross-browser or cross-platform testing – If the same test needs to run on Chrome, Firefox, and Edge, automation can save significant time.

8. Tests that are difficult to execute manually – For example, scenarios requiring large amounts of data or repeated execution.

On the other hand, I generally avoid automating one-time tests, frequently changing features, exploratory testing, usability testing, and scenarios where automation effort is greater than the benefit.

So my approach is basically: prioritize tests based on frequency, stability, business risk, execution effort, and ROI. The goal is not maximum automation coverage, but maximum useful automation coverage.”

Short interview version

“I select test cases for automation based on ROI. I prioritize repetitive regression tests, stable and business-critical functionality, data-driven scenarios, complex workflows, and cross-browser tests. I usually avoid one-time, exploratory, usability, and frequently changing tests. My goal is to automate tests that are executed frequently, are stable, time-consuming manually, and provide high business value.”

#==================================================================================================
# Q :  In TestNg how will you ensure that screenshots will be taken only for fail test cases.

==> In TestNG, I use an ITestListener and override the onTestFailure() method. Since onTestFailure() is called only when a test case fails, I capture the screenshot there. This prevents screenshots from being taken for passed or skipped tests

public class TestListener implements ITestListener {

    @Override
    public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {

        WebDriver driver = DriverManager.getDriver();

        if (driver != null) {

            TakesScreenshot screenshot =
                    (TakesScreenshot) driver;

            File source = screenshot.getScreenshotAs(
                    OutputType.FILE);

            String testName = result.getName();

            File destination = new File(
                    "screenshots/" + testName + ".png");

            try {
                Files.copy(
                        source.toPath(),
                        destination.toPath(),
                        StandardCopyOption.REPLACE_EXISTING
                );

                System.out.println(
                        "Screenshot captured for failed test: "
                        + testName);

            } catch (IOException e) {
                e.printStackTrace();
            }
        }
    }
}

Then I register the listener:
@Listeners(TestListener.class)
public class LoginTest {
    
    @Test
    public void validLoginTest() {
        // test steps
    }
}

Or I can register it through testng.xml:
<listeners>
    <listener class-name="listeners.TestListener"/>
</listeners>

** Important point** :
I would not put screenshot logic inside @AfterMethod without checking the result, because @AfterMethod runs after every test.

If I use @AfterMethod, I can explicitly check the result:
@AfterMethod
public void tearDown(ITestResult result) {

    if (result.getStatus() == ITestResult.FAILURE) {
        captureScreenshot(result.getName());
    }

    DriverManager.quitDriver();
}
#----------------------------------------#
*** In a Cucumber + TestNG framework ***
#----------------------------------------#
Since you're using Cucumber with TestNG, the equivalent approach is usually the Cucumber @After hook

@After
public void tearDown(Scenario scenario) {

    if (scenario.isFailed()) {

        byte[] screenshot = ((TakesScreenshot) DriverManager.getDriver())
               			 .getScreenshotAs(OutputType.BYTES);

        scenario.attach(screenshot, "image/png", "Failure Screenshot" );
    }

    DriverManager.quitDriver();
}

#----------------------------------------#
# ** Best interview answer **
#----------------------------------------#

	For pure TestNG, I use ITestListener and capture the screenshot inside onTestFailure(), because that callback is invoked only when the test fails. If I'm using @AfterMethod, I check ITestResult.FAILURE before capturing. In my Cucumber-TestNG framework, I can similarly use an @After hook with scenario.isFailed(). This ensures screenshots are generated only for failed scenarios and avoids unnecessary screenshot files for passed tests.

# Q : Suppose you have 200 test case and 49 test failed , how would you collect the data of only failed test cases in testNg. Best way ?

==> ITestListener + onTestFailure() as the best approach.

If I have 200 TestNG test cases and 49 fail, I don't scan all 200 tests after execution. I use TestNG's ITestListener. The onTestFailure(ITestResult result) callback is triggered only for failed test cases, so I can collect the test name, class, exception, parameters, execution time, and screenshot information for those 49 failures.

import org.testng.ITestListener;
import org.testng.ITestResult;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;

public class TestListener implements ITestListener {

    private static final List<ITestResult> failedTests = new ArrayList<>();

    @Override
    public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {

        failedTests.add(result);

        System.out.println( "Failed Test: " + result.getMethod().getMethodName());

        System.out.println( "Class: " + result.getTestClass().getName());

        System.out.println( "Exception: " + result.getThrowable().getMessage());
    }

    public static List<ITestResult> getFailedTests() {
        return failedTests;
    }
}

Register it:
@Listeners(TestListener.class)
public class LoginTest {
    
    @Test
    public void validLogin() {
        // test
    }
}
Or in testng.xml:

<listeners>
    <listener class-name="listeners.TestListener"/>
</listeners>

#===============================================
If I need a simple list of failed test names

I would maintain a List<String>:

private static final List<String> failedTestNames = new ArrayList<>();

@Override
public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {

    String testName = result.getMethod().getMethodName();

    failedTestNames.add(testName);
}

After execution:

System.out.println("Total Failed Tests: "
        + failedTestNames.size());

failedTestNames.forEach(System.out::println);

#------------------------------------------------------#
But for a real framework, I would collect more information

Instead of only storing the test name, I would create a failure object/model:

public class FailureDetails {

    private String testName;
    private String className;
    private String exception;
    private String screenshotPath;

    // constructor, getters, setters
}

Then inside onTestFailure():

@Override
public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {

    FailureDetails failure = new FailureDetails(
        result.getMethod().getMethodName(),
        result.getTestClass().getName(),
        result.getThrowable() != null
            ? result.getThrowable().getMessage()
            : "Unknown error",
        captureScreenshot(result)
    );

    failedTestDetails.add(failure);
}
#--------------------------------------------------#
# Very important interview point
#---------------------------------------------------#
If the interviewer asks:

“Why ITestListener instead of checking everything in @AfterMethod?”

I would say:

“@AfterMethod runs after every test, so I would have to check the result status for all 200 tests. With ITestListener.onTestFailure(), TestNG directly notifies me when a test fails. Therefore, I can capture only the 49 failed tests without processing the successful tests unnecessarily.”

And if they ask “How would you use this in your Selenium framework?”, I would say:

“I would use ITestListener to collect failed test metadata, capture the Selenium screenshot, log the exception, attach the information to the Extent report, and optionally generate a failed-test summary or feed those failed tests into a retry mechanism.”

# Q : What is the difference between “/” and “//” in XPath?

==> Single Slash “/” – A single slash is used to create an XPath with an absolute path i.e. the XPath would be created to start selection from the document node/start node.  For example, if we have an XML document with a root element called “form,” each form is represented by a component called “content.”  The XPath expression “/form/content” would select all the immediate child elements called “content” of the “form” element. An example is given below 

1. driver.findElement(By.xpath("/html/body/form/content"));

Double Slash “//” – Double slash is used to create an XPath with a relative path i.e. the XPath would be created to start selection from anywhere within the document. For example, the below example will select any element in the document which has an attribute named “id” with the specified value “email”.

1. driver.findElement(By.xpath("//*[@id = 'email']")

#===============================================================================================================================
# Q :  Explain the different exceptions in Selenium WebDriver
Exceptions in Selenium are similar to exceptions in other programming languages. The most common exceptions in Selenium are:
==> 

1. TimeoutException: This exception is thrown when a command performing an operation does not complete in the expected time.

2. NoSuchElementException: This exception is thrown when an element with given attributes is not found on the web page. Suppose webdriver is trying to click on XPath – “//*[@id=’yDmH0d’]/div/div[2]/div[2]/form/content” which doesn’t exist on that particular web page, then the below exception is displayed – org.openqa.selenium.NoSuchElementException.

3. ElementNotVisibleException: This exception is thrown when the element is present in DOM (Document Object Model), but not visible on the web page.

4. StaleElementException: This exception is thrown when the element is either deleted or no longer attached to the DOM. This exception occurs, when Selenium navigates to a different page, comes back to the same old page, and performs operations on the old page. Technically, it occurs when the element defined in the Selenium code is not found in the cache memory and the Selenium code is trying to locate it. 

5. NoSuchFrameException: iframe is the HTML web page inside another HTML web page. In order to work with the Web Elements on any iframe, we have to first switch to the iframe in Selenium and then locate the respective web elements inside the iframe. NoSuchFrameException WebDriver Exception occurs when the driver in the code is unable to find the frame on the web page to switch. i.e. when the driver is switching to an invalid or non-existing iframe.

6. NoAlertPresentException: In order to work with Alert pop-ups, we have to first switch to Alert and then perform operations on Alert like reading the messages on the Alerts or Accepting the alert by pressing ‘OK’ button on the alert, etc. NoAlertPresentException WebDriver Exception occurs when the web driver is unable to find the Alert on the web page to switch. i.e. when the driver is switching to an invalid or non-existing Alert pop-up.

7. NoSuchWindowException:  In order to work with the Web Elements on any Browser Pop-up Window, we have to first switch to the pop-up in Selenium and then locate the respective web elements inside the pop-up window. NoSuchWindowException WebDriver Exception occurs when the web driver is unable to find the pop-up window on the web page to switch. i.e. when the driver is switching to an invalid or non-existing pop-up window.

8. ElementNotSelectableException: This exception comes under the InvalidElementStateException class The ElementNotSelectableException indicates that the web element is present on the web page but cannot be selected.

9. SessionNotFoundException: This Exception will occur when the driver is trying to perform operations on the Web Application after the Browser is closed

#=============================================================================================================
# Q : What is maven surefire plugin ?

==> Maven Surefire Plugin is a Maven plugin used to execute unit and integration tests during the Maven build lifecycle, typically in the test phase. In a Selenium TestNG project, it is commonly used to run TestNG test classes and configure how the tests are executed.

command : mvn test
Maven reaches the test phase, and the Surefire plugin executes the tests.

The flow is roughly:

mvn test
   ↓
Maven test phase
   ↓
Maven Surefire Plugin
   ↓
TestNG / JUnit
   ↓
Test execution
   ↓
Test reports

In pom.xml:

<plugin>
    <groupId>org.apache.maven.plugins</groupId>
    <artifactId>maven-surefire-plugin</artifactId>
    <version>3.5.3</version>

    <configuration>
        <suiteXmlFiles>
            <suiteXmlFile>testng.xml</suiteXmlFile>
        </suiteXmlFiles>
    </configuration>
</plugin>

command : mvn clean test
Surefire picks up testng.xml and executes the tests defined there.
#---------------------------------------------------#
# What can we configure using Surefire?
Some common configurations are:

1. TestNG suite

<suiteXmlFiles>
    <suiteXmlFile>testng.xml</suiteXmlFile>
</suiteXmlFiles>

2. Parallel execution

<parallel>methods</parallel>
<threadCount>4</threadCount>

3. Include/exclude tests

We can configure which test classes or methods should be executed.

4. System properties

For example:

mvn test -Dbrowser=chrome -Denvironment=qa

Then the framework can read those values.

5. Failure behavior

Surefire also controls how Maven handles test failures and generates test execution reports.

Surefire reports

After execution, Surefire typically generates reports under:

target/
 └── surefire-reports/
      ├── TEST-*.xml
      └── *.txt

These reports contain information such as:

Number of tests executed
Passed tests
Failed tests
Skipped tests
Failure/error details
Execution time

Surefire vs Failsafe — common interview question
Surefire :	
1. Primarily used for test execution in the test phase	
2. Commonly executes unit/test-level tests	
3. mvn test	
4. Default naming commonly includes *Test.java, Test*.java, etc.	

Failsafe :
1. Primarily used for integration tests
2. Commonly executes integration tests
3. Usually mvn verify
4. Commonly uses *IT.java, IT*.java, etc.

#----------------------------------------------#
⭐ Strong interview answer

“Maven Surefire Plugin integrates the test framework with Maven. In my Selenium TestNG framework, when I execute mvn clean test, Surefire is responsible for launching the TestNG tests, and I can configure things like the TestNG suite XML, parallel execution, test inclusion/exclusion, system properties, and test reporting. The execution results are generally generated under target/surefire-reports.”

One important distinction: Surefire is not the TestNG framework itself. TestNG provides the testing features such as @Test, assertions, listeners, and data providers; Surefire is the Maven plugin that launches and integrates those tests into the Maven build lifecycle.

#=========================================================================================================================================
# Q : Write code to find Broken Links in Selenium
==> 	To find broken links, I first collect all <a> elements, extract their href attributes, and then send HTTP requests to those URLs. If the response status code is 400 or greater, I consider the link broken.
import java.net.HttpURLConnection;
import java.net.URI;
import java.util.List;

import org.openqa.selenium.By;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebElement;

public class BrokenLinksTest {

    public static void findBrokenLinks(WebDriver driver) {

        List<WebElement> links =  driver.findElements(By.tagName("a"));

        System.out.println("Total links found: " + links.size());

        for (WebElement link : links) {

            String href = link.getAttribute("href");

            // Skip links without href
            if (href == null || href.isBlank()) {
                continue;
            }

            // Skip non-HTTP links
            if (!href.startsWith("http://") && !href.startsWith("https://")) {
                continue;
            }

            try {
                HttpURLConnection connection = (HttpURLConnection) URI
                                .create(href)
                                .toURL()
                                .openConnection();

                connection.setRequestMethod("HEAD");
                connection.setConnectTimeout(5000);
                connection.setReadTimeout(5000);

                connection.connect();

                int responseCode = connection.getResponseCode();

                if (responseCode >= 400) {
                    System.out.println("BROKEN LINK: " + href+ " | Status Code: "+ responseCode);                                                                               
                
		} else {
                    System.out.println("VALID LINK: " + href+ " | Status Code: "+ responseCode);                                            
                }

                connection.disconnect();

            } catch (Exception e) {
                System.out.println("BROKEN/UNREACHABLE LINK: " + href + " | Error: "  + e.getMessage());
            }
        }
    }
}

@Test
public void verifyBrokenLinks() {

    driver.get("https://www.saucedemo.com/");

    BrokenLinksTest.findBrokenLinks(driver);
}

#----------------------------------------#
# Important interview points
#----------------------------------------#

1. Why <a> tag?
	For hyperlinks, I locate the <a> elements and read their href attribute.”

2. Why HTTP connection instead of clicking every link?
	
	I don't click every link because clicking can navigate away from the current page and makes the test slower. I can directly validate the URL using an HTTP request.”

3. What is a broken link?

Typically:

200–399 → generally reachable/redirected
400–499 → client-side error
500–599 → server-side error

So I generally flag:

if (responseCode >= 400)

4. What about href being null?

“I skip null or empty href values because there is no URL to validate.”

⭐ Strong interview answer

“I find all anchor elements using driver.findElements(By.tagName("a")), extract their href, skip null or non-HTTP URLs, and send an HTTP HEAD request to each URL. I check the response status code, and if it is 400 or above, I report it as broken. I also handle connection exceptions because an unreachable URL can fail without returning an HTTP status.”

One practical caveat: some servers don't support HEAD correctly and may return 405 Method Not Allowed even though GET works. In a production framework, I would handle that case by falling back to a GET request before declaring the link broken.

# Q : You have 10 links how will you print title of each link using selenium. Give Optimal approach 
==> If I have 10 links and I need to print the title of the page each link opens, I would first collect all the href values, then open each URL in the same driver and read driver.getTitle(). I would avoid clicking the links because clicking can change the page state and makes the test unnecessarily dependent on browser navigation.

List<WebElement> links = driver.findElements(By.tagName("a"));

for (WebElement link : links) {

    String href = link.getAttribute("href");

    if (href == null || href.isBlank()) {
        continue;
    }

    driver.get(href);

    String title = driver.getTitle();

    System.out.println("URL   : " + href);
    System.out.println("Title : " + title);
}

If I need exactly 10 links

List<WebElement> links = driver.findElements(By.tagName("a"));

int count = Math.min(10, links.size());

for (int i = 0; i < count; i++) {

    String href = links.get(i).getAttribute("href");

    if (href == null || href.isBlank()) {
        continue;
    }

    driver.get(href);

    System.out.println("Link " + (i + 1) + " | Title: " + driver.getTitle() );
}

⭐ But there's an important interview consideration

If the requirement is only to print the title of each linked page, I would say:

“For a small number of links, using the same WebDriver instance and driver.get() is simple and optimal. However, if I want to preserve the original page and avoid repeated browser navigation, I can open each URL in a new tab/window, retrieve the title, and close that tab.”

String originalWindow = driver.getWindowHandle();

List<WebElement> links =
        driver.findElements(By.tagName("a"));

for (int i = 0; i < Math.min(10, links.size()); i++) {

    String href = links.get(i).getAttribute("href");

    if (href == null || href.isBlank()) {
        continue;
    }

    driver.switchTo().newWindow(WindowType.TAB);
    driver.get(href);

    System.out.println(
        "Link " + (i + 1) +
        " | Title: " + driver.getTitle()
    );

    driver.close();
    driver.switchTo().window(originalWindow);
}

Interview one-liner

“I would extract the href values rather than click the links, navigate to each URL using the same WebDriver session, call getTitle(), print the result, and handle null/empty URLs. For only 10 links, this keeps the implementation simple and avoids unnecessary element interactions.”

#======================================================================================================================================
# Q : What is the difference between driver.findElement() and driver.findElements() commands?
==>
FindElement – This method locates the first web element on the current web page matching the criteria mentioned as the parameter.  
If the web element is not found, it will throw an exception – NoSuchElementException. 
1 . driver.findElement(By.xpath("Xpath location"));

FindElements – This method locates all the web elements on the current web page matching the criteria mentioned as parameters. 
If not found any WebElement on the current page as per the given element locator mechanism, it will return the empty list.     
1 : findElements (By arg0):List<WebElement

#======================================================================================================================================
# Q : In what cases we need to use implicit / explicit wait

==> I use implicit and explicit waits for synchronization between Selenium and the application. The key difference is that implicit wait is a global wait for finding elements, while explicit wait is a targeted wait for a specific condition.
1. Implicit Wait — When would I use it?

I use an implicit wait when I want Selenium to wait for an element to be present in the DOM whenever Selenium tries to locate it.

driver.manage().timeouts().implicitlyWait(Duration.ofSeconds(10));

For example:

driver.findElement(By.id("user-name")).sendKeys("standard_user");

If the element isn't immediately available, Selenium waits up to 10 seconds while trying to locate it.

Use case:

Simple applications
Consistent application loading behavior
When I want a basic global synchronization mechanism

But in a real automation framework, I generally prefer explicit waits because they give much better control.

2. Explicit Wait — When would I use it?

I use an explicit wait when I need to wait for a specific condition.

For example, if a button becomes clickable after some processing:

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(
        driver,
        Duration.ofSeconds(15)
);

WebElement loginButton = wait.until(
        ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(
                By.id("login-button")
        )
);

loginButton.click();

Other common conditions are:

ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfElementLocated(locator)

ExpectedConditions.presenceOfElementLocated(locator)

ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(locator)

ExpectedConditions.invisibilityOfElementLocated(locator)

ExpectedConditions.urlContains("inventory")

ExpectedConditions.titleContains("Swag Labs")
Real Selenium example

Suppose after clicking Login, the Inventory page takes a few seconds to load.

I wouldn't use:

Thread.sleep(5000);

Instead:

wait.until(
    ExpectedConditions.urlContains("inventory.html")
);

This is better because Selenium continues as soon as the condition is satisfied instead of always waiting the full five seconds

⭐ Important interview point

If the interviewer asks:

“Can we use implicit and explicit waits together?”

I would say:

“Technically, yes, but I generally avoid mixing them because their interaction can make the actual wait behavior difficult to predict and can increase synchronization time. In a framework, I prefer explicit waits or a centralized wait utility for better control.”

What about Fluent Wait?

I would also mention:

“For more customized synchronization, I can use FluentWait, where I can define the timeout, polling interval, and exceptions to ignore.”
Example:

Wait<WebDriver> wait = new FluentWait<>(driver)
        .withTimeout(Duration.ofSeconds(20))
        .pollingEvery(Duration.ofSeconds(2))
        .ignoring(NoSuchElementException.class);
#--------------------------------------#
Best interview answer

“I use implicit wait when I need a simple global wait for element lookup, but for a production Selenium framework I prefer explicit waits because applications are dynamic and different elements may require different conditions. For example, I may wait for an element to be visible, clickable, a loader to disappear, or the URL to change. I avoid Thread.sleep() because it introduces fixed delays and makes execution slower and less reliable.”

#======================================================================================================================================
# Q : How to you handle multiple frames ? how would you switch to parent frame.

==> When working with multiple iframes, I first switch from the main document to the required frame using switchTo().frame(). If there are nested frames, I switch frame by frame. To move from a child frame back to its immediate parent, I use parentFrame(). If I want to return completely to the main page, I use defaultContent().

1. Switch to a frame

By index:

driver.switchTo().frame(0);

By name or ID:

driver.switchTo().frame("paymentFrame");

By WebElement:

WebElement frame = driver.findElement(
        By.id("paymentFrame")
);

driver.switchTo().frame(frame);
2. Handling nested/multiple frames

Suppose the structure is:

Main Page
   |
   └── Frame A
         |
         └── Frame B
               |
               └── Input

I need to switch sequentially:

driver.switchTo().frame("frameA");

driver.switchTo().frame("frameB");

driver.findElement(By.id("cardNumber"))
      .sendKeys("123456789");

I cannot directly switch to Frame B from the main page if Frame B is nested inside Frame A.

3. Switch to parent frame

If I am currently inside Frame B:

driver.switchTo().parentFrame();

Now I am back inside Frame A.

For example:

driver.switchTo().frame("frameA");
driver.switchTo().frame("frameB");

// Work inside Frame B

driver.switchTo().parentFrame();

// Now I am inside Frame A
4. Switch back to main document

If I want to completely come out of all frames:

driver.switchTo().defaultContent();

Example:

driver.switchTo().frame("frameA");
driver.switchTo().frame("frameB");

// Work inside Frame B

driver.switchTo().defaultContent();

// Back to the main page

⭐ Important difference

parentFrame() → moves one level up.
defaultContent() → moves directly to the main document.

Practical framework approach

In a framework, I prefer using an explicit wait when the frame loads:

WebDriverWait wait =
        new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(15));

wait.until(
    ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt(
        By.id("paymentFrame")
    )
);

This is useful when the iframe is dynamically loaded.

Strong interview answer

“For multiple or nested frames, I switch sequentially using switchTo().frame(). Once I'm inside a child frame, parentFrame() takes me back one level to the immediate parent frame, whereas defaultContent() takes me all the way back to the main document. For dynamically loaded frames, I prefer frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt() with an explicit wait.”

#======================================================================================================================================
# Q : Difference between driver.get() vs driver.navigate().to() ?
==> Both driver.get() and driver.navigate().to() are used to navigate to a URL, and for a normal URL navigation they behave very similarly. The main difference is that navigate() also provides browser navigation operations such as back, forward, and refresh.”

# driver.get() :
1. Directly navigates to a URL
2. Simple and commonly used for opening a page
3. Returns void
4. Does not provide back(), forward(), refresh() through get()
5. Commonly used for initial page navigation
	
# driver.navigate().to() :
1. Navigates to a URL through the Navigation interface
2. Useful when working with browser navigation
3. Also returns void
4. Provides back(), forward(), refresh()
5. Commonly used when navigation operations are required

driver.navigate().to()
driver.navigate().back();
driver.navigate().forward();
driver.navigate().refresh();

Important interview point

A common misconception is:

“get() waits but navigate().to() doesn't.”

I would not give that as a blanket answer. Both are navigation commands and Selenium's page-load behavior is governed by the WebDriver/page-load strategy and browser behavior. The practical distinction is that navigate() exposes the broader navigation API.

⭐ Strong interview answer

“driver.get() and driver.navigate().to() can both be used to open a URL. I normally use get() when I simply need to navigate to a page. I use navigate() when I also need browser navigation operations such as back, forward, or refresh. So the major difference is not that one is inherently faster or waits while the other doesn't; it's that navigate() provides a complete navigation interface.”

#======================================================================================================================================
# Q :  How to handle Location popup in selenium ?

==>	“In Selenium, handling a location permission popup depends on whether it is a browser-level permission popup or a JavaScript alert. Selenium cannot directly interact with native browser permission popups using normal WebDriver methods. I handle them by configuring browser preferences before launching the browser

1. Handle Location Popup in Chrome

We can use ChromeOptions to automatically allow or block location access.
Allow location permission:
import org.openqa.selenium.WebDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.chrome.ChromeDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.chrome.ChromeOptions;

import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.Map;

public class LocationPopupExample {

    public static void main(String[] args) {

        ChromeOptions options = new ChromeOptions();

        Map<String, Object> prefs = new HashMap<>();
        prefs.put("profile.default_content_setting_values.geolocation", 1);

        options.setExperimentalOption("prefs", prefs);

        WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver(options);

        driver.get("https://example.com");

        // Perform test steps

        driver.quit();
    }
}
2. Handle Location Popup in Firefox

For Firefox, use FirefoxOptions and configure geolocation permission preferences.
import org.openqa.selenium.WebDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.firefox.FirefoxDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.firefox.FirefoxOptions;

public class FirefoxLocationExample {

    public static void main(String[] args) {

        FirefoxOptions options = new FirefoxOptions();

        options.addPreference(
            "permissions.default.geo", 1
        );

        WebDriver driver = new FirefoxDriver(options);

        driver.get("https://example.com");

        // Perform test steps

        driver.quit();
    }
}
3. What if it is a JavaScript Alert?

If the popup is an actual JavaScript alert, use Selenium's Alert interface.
import org.openqa.selenium.Alert;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebDriver;

Alert alert = driver.switchTo().alert();

System.out.println(alert.getText());

alert.accept(); // Click OK
// alert.dismiss(); // Click Cancel
This works only for JavaScript alerts, not standard browser location permission prompts.

Strong interview answer

“I handle browser location permission popups using browser-specific options before initializing the WebDriver. In Chrome, I configure ChromeOptions preferences to allow or block geolocation. In Firefox, I use FirefoxOptions preferences. If the popup is a JavaScript alert, I handle it using driver.switchTo().alert(). The approach depends on the type of popup.”

Interview tip: In your Selenium framework, configure these options inside your DriverFactory so the location permission behavior is consistent across test cases.

#=====================================================================================================================================
# Q : How to handle dynamic dropdowns in Selenium ?

==> In Selenium, I handle dynamic dropdowns by identifying the dropdown type, locating the available options dynamically, and selecting the required option based on its visible text or attribute. I generally use explicit waits to ensure the options are loaded before interacting with them
There are three common types of dropdowns in Selenium.

1. Dynamic dropdown using sendKeys() and click()
	For example, when entering a city name, suggestions appear dynamically.

import org.openqa.selenium.By;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebElement;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.ExpectedConditions;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.WebDriverWait;

import java.time.Duration;
import java.util.List;

public class DynamicDropdownExample {

    public static void main(String[] args) {

        WebDriverWait wait =
            new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

        // Enter city name
        WebElement cityInput = wait.until(
            ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfElementLocated(
                By.id("city")
            )
        );

        cityInput.sendKeys("Pune");

        // Wait for dynamic suggestions
        By optionsLocator = By.xpath(
            "//ul[@id='city-options']//li"
        );

        List<WebElement> options = wait.until(
            ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfAllElementsLocatedBy(
                optionsLocator
            )
        );

        // Select the required option
        for (WebElement option : options) {
            if (option.getText().trim().equalsIgnoreCase("Pune")) {
                option.click();
                break;
            }
        }
    }
}


2. Dynamic dropdown using Select class
	If the dropdown is a standard HTML <select> element, use Selenium's Select class.

import org.openqa.selenium.By;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebElement;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.Select;

WebElement dropdown = driver.findElement(By.id("country"));

Select select = new Select(dropdown);

select.selectByVisibleText("India");
select.selectByValue("IN");
select.selectByIndex(2);

Important: The Select class works only with HTML <select> elements, not custom dropdowns built using div, li, or other elements.

3. Dynamic dropdown using a custom dropdown
	For dropdowns built with div or li, click the dropdown and select the required option.

WebDriverWait wait =
    new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

// Open dropdown
wait.until(
    ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(
        By.id("countryDropdown")
    )
).click();

// Select option
wait.until(
    ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(
        By.xpath("//div[@role='option' and normalize-space()='India']")
    )
).click();



Strong interview summary:

“I first inspect whether the dropdown is a native HTML select element or a custom dropdown. For a native dropdown, I use the Select class. For custom or autocomplete dropdowns, I click or type into the field, wait for the dynamic options using WebDriverWait, locate the required option by visible text, click it, and verify the selection.

Best practices
1. Use explicit waits instead of Thread.sleep().
2. Locate options after opening the dropdown or entering search text.
3. Use normalize-space() in XPath when text contains extra whitespace.
4. Avoid hardcoded indexes when options can change.
5. Handle stale elements if the dropdown refreshes its options dynamically.
6. Verify that the expected option is selected after clicking.

#==========================================================================================================================
# Q : How to handle cookies ?

==> In Selenium, I handle cookies using the Options interface through driver.manage().getCookies(), addCookie(), deleteCookie(), and deleteAllCookies(). Cookies are useful for managing session information, authentication, and testing login or logout functionality.

1. Get all cookies
import org.openqa.selenium.Cookie;
import java.util.Set;

Set<Cookie> cookies = driver.manage().getCookies();

for (Cookie cookie : cookies) {
    System.out.println("Name: " + cookie.getName());
    System.out.println("Value: " + cookie.getValue());
    System.out.println("Domain: " + cookie.getDomain());
    System.out.println("Path: " + cookie.getPath());
    System.out.println("Expiry: " + cookie.getExpiry());
    System.out.println("--------------------");
}

2. Get a specific cookie :

Cookie cookie = driver.manage().getCookieNamed("session_id");

if (cookie != null) {
    System.out.println("Cookie value: " + cookie.getValue());
} else {
    System.out.println("Cookie not found");
}

3. Add a cookie
import org.openqa.selenium.Cookie;

Cookie cookie = new Cookie("testCookie", "testValue");

driver.manage().addCookie(cookie);

System.out.println("Cookie added successfully");

Important: Navigate to the target domain before adding a cookie. Selenium only permits adding cookies for the current domain, subject to cookie restrictions.

4. Delete a specific cookie

driver.manage().deleteCookieNamed("testCookie");	
Alternatively, delete a cookie object:

Cookie cookie = driver.manage().getCookieNamed("testCookie");

if (cookie != null) {
    driver.manage().deleteCookie(cookie);
}

5. Delete all cookies
driver.manage().deleteAllCookies();
This is commonly used during test cleanup to remove browser cookies for the current domain and help isolate test cases.

import org.openqa.selenium.Cookie;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.chrome.ChromeDriver;

import java.util.Set;

public class CookieHandlingExample {

    public static void main(String[] args) {

        WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver();

        try {
            driver.get("https://www.saucedemo.com/");

            // Add cookie
            driver.manage().addCookie(
                new Cookie("automation", "selenium")
            );

            // Retrieve all cookies
            Set<Cookie> cookies =
                driver.manage().getCookies();

            System.out.println("Total cookies: " + cookies.size());

            // Retrieve specific cookie
            Cookie cookie =
                driver.manage().getCookieNamed("automation");

            System.out.println(
                "Cookie found: " + (cookie != null)
            );

            // Delete specific cookie
            driver.manage().deleteCookieNamed("automation");

            // Delete remaining cookies
            driver.manage().deleteAllCookies();

        } finally {
            driver.quit();
        }
    }
}

“I use Selenium's cookie-management methods to retrieve, add, verify, and delete cookies. In automation frameworks, I commonly clear cookies to isolate test cases, inspect session cookies during authentication testing, and validate session behavior. I also make sure the browser is on the correct domain before adding cookies.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q : What is Synchronization and how to overcome it

==> Synchronization in Selenium means coordinating the execution speed of the automation script with the response time of the web application.

Sometimes Selenium executes commands faster than the application loads elements or updates the DOM. This can cause exceptions such as NoSuchElementException, ElementNotInteractableException, and TimeoutException.

To overcome synchronization issues, I use Implicit Wait, Explicit Wait, and Fluent Wait. In real-time automation frameworks, I prefer explicit waits because they wait for specific conditions.

1. Types of waits in Selenium
A. Implicit Wait

Implicit wait applies globally to element searches throughout the WebDriver session.

import java.time.Duration;

driver.manage().timeouts().implicitlyWait(Duration.ofSeconds(10));

Example: Selenium waits up to 10 seconds when locating an element before throwing NoSuchElementException.

B. Explicit Wait

Explicit wait waits for a specific condition, such as an element becoming visible or clickable.
import java.time.Duration;
import org.openqa.selenium.By;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebElement;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.ExpectedConditions;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.WebDriverWait;

WebDriverWait wait =
    new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

WebElement loginButton = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("login-button")));

loginButton.click();

# Common expected conditions:

1. visibilityOfElementLocated()

2. elementToBeClickable()

3. presenceOfElementLocated()

4. invisibilityOfElementLocated()

5. titleContains()

6. urlContains()

C. Fluent Wait

Fluent wait allows you to configure the maximum timeout, polling interval, and exceptions to ignore.

import java.time.Duration;
import org.openqa.selenium.By;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebElement;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.FluentWait;

import java.util.NoSuchElementException;

FluentWait<WebDriver> wait = new FluentWait<>(driver)
    .withTimeout(Duration.ofSeconds(20))
    .pollingEvery(Duration.ofMillis(500))
    .ignoring(NoSuchElementException.class);

WebElement element = wait.until(
    d -> d.findElement(By.id("login-button"))
);

element.click();

#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#

Synchronization in Selenium is the process of ensuring that Selenium WebDriver executes automation steps at the right time according to the application's response and loading behavior.

In real-time applications, web elements may take time to load because of network latency, JavaScript execution, AJAX calls, or dynamic DOM updates. If Selenium tries to interact with an element before it is ready, we may get exceptions such as NoSuchElementException, ElementNotInteractableException, or ElementClickInterceptedException.

To overcome synchronization issues, I mainly use three types of waits in Selenium.”

1. Implicit Wait

Implicit wait applies globally to element searches throughout the WebDriver session.

driver.manage().timeouts()
      .implicitlyWait(Duration.ofSeconds(10));

Use case: When I want a general waiting period for elements to appear in the DOM.

2. Explicit Wait — Preferred approach

Explicit wait waits for a specific condition, such as an element becoming visible, clickable, or an alert appearing.

WebDriverWait wait =
    new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

WebElement loginButton = wait.until(
    ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(
        By.id("login-button")
    )
);

loginButton.click();

Use case: In my automation framework, I prefer explicit waits for dynamic web applications because I can wait for the exact condition required before performing an action.

3. Fluent Wait

Fluent wait allows us to customize the timeout, polling interval, and ignored exceptions.

Wait<WebDriver> wait = new FluentWait<>(driver)
    .withTimeout(Duration.ofSeconds(30))
    .pollingEvery(Duration.ofSeconds(2))
    .ignoring(NoSuchElementException.class);

WebElement element = wait.until(
    d -> d.findElement(By.id("username"))
);

Use case: I use FluentWait when an element takes an unpredictable amount of time to appear or when I need customized polling behavior.

How I handle synchronization in a real-time project

“In my Selenium POM framework, I maintain reusable wait methods in a WaitUtils class and use explicit waits in page objects before interacting with dynamic elements. I avoid Thread.sleep() because it introduces fixed delays and increases execution time.

I also avoid mixing implicit and explicit waits because it can result in unpredictable overall wait times. For AJAX operations, I wait for the relevant UI condition, such as a loading spinner disappearing or search results becoming visible. If the DOM refreshes, I locate the element again to handle stale element references.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q : How do you handle AJAX synchronization?

==> In Selenium, I handle AJAX synchronization using explicit waits because AJAX requests update parts of a webpage asynchronously without reloading the entire page.

For example, when I search for a product, the application sends an AJAX request to the server, and the search results appear after some time. If Selenium tries to interact with the results before they are loaded, the test may fail.

To overcome this, I use WebDriverWait with ExpectedConditions to wait for specific UI conditions, such as element visibility, clickability, or a loading spinner disappearing. I prefer condition-based waits instead of Thread.sleep() because they improve test stability and execution time.

1. Example: Wait for AJAX search results

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(15));

// Enter search text
driver.findElement(By.id("searchBox")).sendKeys("Laptop");

// Wait until AJAX search results are visible
WebElement result = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfElementLocated(By.id("searchResults"));

// Verify the result
System.out.println(result.getText());

2. Example: Wait until the loading spinner disappears

Sometimes the application displays a loading spinner while the AJAX request is processing.

WebDriverWait wait =  new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(15));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.invisibilityOfElementLocated(By.id("loadingSpinner")));

// Continue after the spinner disappears
driver.findElement(By.id("submitButton")).click();

3. Example: Wait for AJAX requests to complete using JavaScript

If the application uses jQuery, we can wait until its active AJAX requests finish.

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(15));

wait.until(webDriver -> Boolean.TRUE.equals(((JavascriptExecutor) webDriver).executeScript(
            "return window.jQuery != null && " +
            "jQuery.active === 0;"
        )
    )
);

Important: This approach applies only to applications using jQuery AJAX. Modern applications using fetch, promises, or other networking mechanisms may require waiting for a specific UI condition instead.
# Best practices
1. Prefer explicit waits over fixed delays.
2. Wait for the actual result or state change rather than assuming a request has finished.
3. Wait for loading indicators to disappear when appropriate.
4. Re-locate elements if AJAX updates replace DOM elements and cause StaleElementReferenceException.
5. Avoid mixing implicit and explicit waits.

Strong interview closing answer

“In my Selenium POM framework, I handle AJAX synchronization through reusable explicit wait methods in a WaitUtils class. Depending on the application, I wait for search results to appear, loaders to disappear, or buttons to become clickable. I avoid fixed sleeps and synchronize with observable application states, which helps reduce flaky test failures.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q : What are Listeners and how do you use them?

==> Listeners in Selenium automation are used to monitor test execution events and perform specific actions when those events occur. In my framework, I use TestNG listeners to capture screenshots when tests fail, log execution details, update Extent Reports, and collect failed test cases.

TestNG provides the ITestListener interface, which contains methods such as onTestStart(), onTestSuccess(), onTestFailure(), and onTestSkipped(). These methods are automatically triggered based on the test execution result.

*. Common TestNG listener methods

1. onTestStart()   : Executes when a test method starts.
2. onTestSuccess() : Executes when a test passes.
3. onTestFailure() : Executes when a test fails.
4. onTestSkipped() : Executes when a test is skipped.
5. onStart()       : Executes when a TestNG test context starts.
6. onFinish()      : Executes when a TestNG test context finishes.

2. Example: Implement ITestListener

In my Selenium framework, I create a listener class to handle failed test cases and capture screenshots.


package listeners;

import org.openqa.selenium.OutputType;
import org.openqa.selenium.TakesScreenshot;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebDriver;
import org.testng.ITestContext;
import org.testng.ITestListener;
import org.testng.ITestResult;

import driver.DriverManager;

import java.io.File;
import java.nio.file.Files;
import java.nio.file.Path;
import java.nio.file.Paths;

public class TestListener implements ITestListener {

    @Override
    public void onTestStart(ITestResult result) {
        System.out.println(
            "Test Started: " + result.getName()
        );
    }

    @Override
    public void onTestSuccess(ITestResult result) {
        System.out.println("Test Passed: " + result.getName());
    }

    @Override
    public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {
        System.out.println("Test Failed: " + result.getName());

        WebDriver driver = DriverManager.getDriver();

        if (driver == null) {
            System.out.println("WebDriver is not available.");
            return;
        }

        try {
            File screenshot = ((TakesScreenshot) driver).getScreenshotAs(OutputType.FILE);

            Path directory = Paths.get("screenshots");
            Files.createDirectories(directory);

            Path destination = directory.resolve(result.getName() + "_" +System.currentTimeMillis() + ".png"    
            );

            Files.copy(screenshot.toPath(), destination );

            System.out.println("Screenshot saved: " + destination );

        } catch (Exception e) {
            System.err.println("Screenshot capture failed: " + e.getMessage() );
        }
    }

    @Override
    public void onTestSkipped(ITestResult result) {
        System.out.println("Test Skipped: " + result.getName() );
    }

    @Override
    public void onStart(ITestContext context) {
        System.out.println("Test Context Started: " + context.getName() );
    }

    @Override
    public void onFinish(ITestContext context) {
        System.out.println("Test Context Finished: " + context.getName());
    }
}
This example assumes your framework provides DriverManager.getDriver() and keeps the browser session alive until the listener captures the screenshot.

3. How do you register a listener?

Option 1: Using testng.xml — suitable for framework-level configuration
<!DOCTYPE suite SYSTEM "https://testng.org/testng-1.0.dtd">

<suite name="Automation Suite">
    <listeners>
        <listener class-name="listeners.TestListener"/>
    </listeners>

    <test name="Regression Tests">
        <classes>
            <class name="tests.LoginTest"/>
        </classes>
    </test>
</suite>

Option 2: Using the @Listeners annotation

import org.testng.annotations.Listeners;

@Listeners(listeners.TestListener.class)
public class LoginTest {
    // Test methods
}

Use one registration method where practical to avoid accidentally registering the same listener twice.

4. What types of listeners are available in TestNG?

1. ITestListener — monitors individual test method execution.

2. ISuiteListener — handles suite start and finish events.

3. IExecutionListener — handles overall TestNG execution start and finish.

4. IInvokedMethodListener — monitors test and configuration method invocations.

5. IAnnotationTransformer — modifies TestNG annotations programmatically, such as retry analyzer configuration.

5. How do you use listeners in your real-time project?

“In my Selenium Java framework, I use ITestListener to capture screenshots only for failed tests, log test results using SLF4J, and update Extent Reports with the test status and failure details. I also use the failure information to generate execution summaries and help with debugging.

Since my framework uses ThreadLocal<WebDriver>, I retrieve the driver associated with the current test thread. I ensure the browser is not closed in an @AfterMethod or Cucumber @After hook before the screenshot is captured.”

Important for Cucumber + TestNG: For ordinary TestNG @Test methods, ITestListener is appropriate. For individual Cucumber scenario failures, I generally use a Cucumber @After hook with scenario.isFailed() to attach the screenshot to the Cucumber report.

Strong interview closing answer

“Listeners help me centralize test execution monitoring without adding repetitive code to every test method. I use them for logging, screenshots on failure, Extent Reports integration, and failure summaries. This makes the automation framework more maintainable, reusable, and easier to debug.”


#====================================================================================================
# Q : Difference between Assert and Verify

==>  In Selenium automation, Assert and Verify are both used to validate actual results against expected results, but the main difference is how the test execution continues after a validation failure.

Assert is a hard assertion, which stops the current test method when the assertion fails. Verify is generally implemented using a soft assertion, which records the failure but allows the remaining test steps to execute. At the end, we call assertAll() to report the accumulated failures.

1. Difference between Assert and Verify :

Feature : Assert (Hard Assertion)
1. Failure behavior : Stops the current test method
2. Failure reporting : Reports failure immediately
3. Test execution : Remaining steps in the method are skipped
4. TestNG implementation : Assert
5. Best use case :  Critical validations

Feature : Verify (Soft Assertion)
1. Failure behavior : Continues executing subsequent steps
2. Failure reporting : Reports collected failures at assertAll()
3. Test execution : Remaining steps continue unless another exception occurs
4. TestNG implementation : SoftAssert
5. Best use case : Multiple independent validations

2. Example of Hard Assert :

import org.testng.Assert;
import org.testng.annotations.Test;

@Test
public void verifyLogin() {

    String actualTitle = driver.getTitle();

    Assert.assertEquals(
        actualTitle,
        "Swag Labs",
        "Page title mismatch"
    );

    System.out.println("Title verified successfully");

    // This step will not execute if the assertion fails.
}
Explanation: If the title does not match "Swag Labs", TestNG throws an assertion error and stops the remaining execution of this test method.

3. Example of Verify using SoftAssert :
import org.testng.asserts.SoftAssert;
import org.testng.annotations.Test;

@Test
public void verifyPageDetails() {

    SoftAssert softAssert = new SoftAssert();

    String actualTitle = driver.getTitle();

    softAssert.assertEquals(actualTitle, "Swag Labs", "Page title mismatch"  );

    boolean logoDisplayed = driver.findElement(By.className("logo")).isDisplayed();

    softAssert.assertTrue(logoDisplayed, "Logo is not displayed");

    boolean loginButtonDisplayed =  driver.findElement(By.id("login-button")).isDisplayed();

    softAssert.assertTrue(loginButtonDisplayed, "Login button is not displayed"  );

    // Reports all recorded assertion failures
    softAssert.assertAll();
}

Explanation: Even if the page title validation fails, the logo and login button validations can still execute. At assertAll(), TestNG reports the accumulated assertion failures.

Important: Always call assertAll() when using SoftAssert; otherwise, recorded assertion failures may not cause the test to fail.

4. When do you use Assert and Verify in real-time projects?

“In my automation framework, I use hard assertions for critical checkpoints, such as successful login, correct page navigation, or successful checkout. If these validations fail, continuing the test may not make sense.

I use soft assertions when I need to validate multiple independent UI elements on the same page, such as product names, prices, labels, and buttons. This allows me to collect multiple failures in a single execution instead of fixing them one at a time.”

Strong interview summary

“Assert is a hard assertion that stops the current test method on failure, whereas Verify is commonly implemented using TestNG's SoftAssert, which allows execution to continue and reports accumulated failures when assertAll() is called.

# Q :  what is the role of automation in your project

==> In my current project, automation plays an important role in improving testing efficiency, reducing repetitive manual effort, and ensuring the quality of the application across different releases.

My responsibility is not only to automate test cases but also to develop and maintain a reusable automation framework, execute regression suites, identify defects, and provide execution reports to the team.

I work on both UI automation using Selenium WebDriver with Java and API automation using REST Assured. I also have experience with Playwright and Python-based automation.

My key responsibilities in automation

1. Test case analysis and selection

Analyze functional requirements and user stories.

Identify stable, repetitive, business-critical, and regression test cases suitable for automation.

Discuss test scenarios with developers, manual testers, and business analysts.

2. Automation framework development

Develop a reusable framework using Java, Selenium WebDriver, Page Object Model, Cucumber, and TestNG.

Maintain reusable components, utility classes, driver management, configuration files, and test data.

Implement explicit waits, logging, exception handling, and parallel execution where appropriate.

3. UI automation

Automate end-to-end user workflows such as login, product selection, cart operations, checkout, and order confirmation.

Validate UI elements, error messages, page navigation, and business rules.

Handle dynamic elements, dropdowns, alerts, frames, and synchronization issues.

4. API automation

Automate REST API testing using REST Assured.

Validate HTTP status codes, response bodies, headers, response schemas, and authentication.

Verify positive and negative scenarios and perform data-driven testing.

5. Test execution and reporting

Execute smoke, sanity, and regression test suites.

Integrate automation execution with Maven and TestNG.

Generate execution reports using Extent Reports and Cucumber reports.

Capture screenshots, logs, and failure details to investigate failed tests.

Analyze failures to distinguish application defects from automation issues.
