Work with developers to verify fixes and execute regression tests.

Example from my project

“For example, in my SauceDemo automation project, I automated the complete shopping workflow, including login, inventory validation, adding products to the cart, checkout information validation, order overview, and order completion.

I developed reusable page classes and common wait utilities to reduce code duplication. I also implemented logging, screenshot capture on failures, and PDF receipt validation. This helps identify functional issues early and makes regression testing more consistent across releases.”

Strong closing answer

“Overall, my role in automation is to improve test coverage, reduce repetitive manual testing, identify defects early, and provide reliable feedback to the team. I focus on building maintainable and reusable automation solutions rather than simply automating individual test cases.”

Interview tip: If the interviewer asks about measurable results, mention your actual project metrics, such as the number of automated test cases, regression execution time saved, defect detection rate, or framework execution frequency. Avoid quoting numbers unless you can support them with your experience.

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	What is POM Design Pattern & Explain POM design pattern Rules ?

==> “POM stands for Page Object Model. It is a design pattern used in Selenium automation to improve code reusability, maintainability, and readability.

In POM, we create a separate Java class for each web page or component of the application. Each page class contains the locators of the web elements and the methods used to interact with those elements.

For example, in my SauceDemo project, I have separate page classes such as LoginPage, InventoryPage, CartPage, and CheckoutPage. If a locator changes, I update it in the corresponding page class rather than modifying multiple test cases.

This separation of test logic from page interaction logic makes the framework easier to maintain and scale.

2. Rules of the POM Design Pattern

Rule 1: Create a separate class for each page

	Each page should have its own class containing its web elements and related actions.

   Example:

	LoginPage.java

	InventoryPage.java

	CartPage.java

	CheckoutPage.java

Rule 2: Keep locators inside the page class

	Define locators using By or @FindBy within the corresponding page object instead of repeating them in test classes.

	@FindBy(id = "user-name")
	private WebElement usernameField;

	@FindBy(id = "password")
	private WebElement passwordField;

	@FindBy(id = "login-button")
	private WebElement loginButton;

Rule 3: Keep page interaction methods inside the page class
	Create reusable methods to interact with web elements.

	public void enterUsername(String username) {
    		usernameField.sendKeys(username);
	}

	public void enterPassword(String password) {
    		passwordField.sendKeys(password);
	}

	public void clickLogin() {
    		loginButton.click();
	}

Rule 4: Keep test logic separate from page logic

	Test classes or Cucumber step definitions should call page methods instead of directly interacting with locators.

	loginPage.enterUsername("standard_user");
	loginPage.enterPassword("secret_sauce");
	loginPage.clickLogin();

Rule 5: Initialize web elements properly

	If using @FindBy, initialize elements with PageFactory.initElements().

	public LoginPage(WebDriver driver) {
    		PageFactory.initElements(driver, this);
	}
Alternatively, use By locators with driver.findElement() inside reusable page methods.

Rule 6: Use meaningful method names
	Methods should represent user actions or page operations, such as login(), addProductToCart(), and proceedToCheckout().

Rule 7: Avoid code duplication
	Common operations such as clicking, typing, waiting, and retrieving text can be implemented in a BasePage class and reused by page objects.

Rule 8: Return the next page object when appropriate
	When an action navigates to another page, a page method can return the corresponding page object.

public InventoryPage login(
        String username, String password) {

    usernameField.sendKeys(username);
    passwordField.sendKeys(password);
    loginButton.click();

    return new InventoryPage(driver);
}

This also supports method chaining and makes test code cleaner.

3. Example POM folder structure


src/test/java
├── base
│   └── BasePage.java
├── pages
│   ├── LoginPage.java
│   ├── InventoryPage.java
│   ├── CartPage.java
│   └── CheckoutPage.java
├── stepdefinitions
│   ├── LoginSteps.java
│   └── CheckoutSteps.java
├── hooks
│   └── Hooks.java
└── runners
    └── TestRunner.java

5. Advantages of POM

1. Maintainability: Locator changes are handled in one place.
2. Reusability: Page methods can be reused across multiple test cases.
3. Readability: Test cases focus on business workflows.
4. Reduced duplication: Common page operations are centralized.
5. Scalability: New pages and workflows can be added more easily.
6. Easier debugging: Page interaction logic is separated from test logic.

6. Is PageFactory mandatory in POM?

      No. PageFactory is optional. POM is the design pattern, whereas PageFactory is a Selenium mechanism that supports initializing web elements using annotations such as @FindBy. We can implement POM using By locators and driver.findElement() without PageFactory.

Strong interview closing answer

“In my automation framework, I follow POM by maintaining separate page classes, encapsulating locators and page interactions, reusing common functionality through BasePage, and keeping test logic in step definitions or test classes. This helps me build a maintainable, reusable, and scalable Selenium framework.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	What is Actions Class

==> The Actions class in Selenium WebDriver is used to perform advanced user interactions with web elements, such as mouse hover, right-click, double-click, drag and drop, and keyboard actions.

It belongs to the org.openqa.selenium.interactions package. I use it when normal WebDriver methods like click() or sendKeys() are not sufficient to perform complex user interactions

1. Common methods of the Actions class

Method : Purpose
1. moveToElement(): Moves the mouse pointer over an element
2. click() : Performs a mouse click
3. doubleClick() : Performs a double-click
4. contextClick() : Performs a right-click
5. clickAndHold() : Clicks and holds an element
6. release() : Releases the held mouse button
7. dragAndDrop() : Drags an element and drops it onto another
8. dragAndDropBy() : Drags an element by a specified offset
9. keyDown() : Presses and holds a keyboard key
10. keyUp() : Releases a keyboard key
11. sendKeys() : Sends keyboard input
12. build() :  Builds a composite action sequence
13. perform() : Executes the action sequence


2. How to create an Actions object
import org.openqa.selenium.interactions.Actions;

Actions actions = new Actions(driver);

Example 1: Mouse hover :
	Suppose a menu displays a submenu when we hover over it.

WebElement menu = driver.findElement(By.id("menu"));
Actions actions = new Actions(driver);
actions.moveToElement(menu).perform();

Example 2: Right-click

WebElement element = driver.findElement(By.id("rightClick"));
Actions actions = new Actions(driver);
actions.contextClick(element).perform();

Example 3: Double-click

WebElement element = driver.findElement(By.id("doubleClick"));
Actions actions = new Actions(driver);
actions.doubleClick(element).perform();

Example 4: Drag and drop

WebElement source = driver.findElement(By.id("source"));
WebElement target = driver.findElement(By.id("target"));

Actions actions = new Actions(driver);

actions.dragAndDrop(source, target).perform();

Example 5: Keyboard actions

WebElement input = driver.findElement(By.id("username"));
Actions actions = new Actions(driver);

actions.click(input)
       .keyDown(Keys.CONTROL)
       .sendKeys("a")
       .keyUp(Keys.CONTROL)
       .sendKeys("standard_user")
       .perform();

This example selects the existing text using Ctrl+A and then types a new username.

3. Difference between build() and perform()

build() combines multiple actions into an action sequence and returns a composite action.

perform() executes the action sequence. For a chain of actions, calling perform() is generally sufficient because it builds and executes the sequence automatically.

Example:

Actions actions = new Actions(driver);

actions.moveToElement(menu)
       .click(submenu)
       .build()
       .perform();
4. How do you use Actions in your real-time project?

“In my Selenium automation framework, I use the Actions class when testing menus, hover-based navigation, drag-and-drop functionality, double-click operations, and keyboard shortcuts. I combine it with explicit waits to ensure the target element is available and interactable before performing the action.”

Interview tip: Remember that WebElement.click() performs a regular element click, whereas Actions supports more complex mouse and keyboard interactions. Also, perform() is required to execute the action chain.

Strong interview summary

“The Actions class is used to simulate advanced mouse and keyboard interactions in Selenium. Common methods include moveToElement(), contextClick(), doubleClick(), dragAndDrop(), keyDown(), and sendKeys(). I use it whenever a scenario requires user-like interactions beyond a normal click or text entry.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q : How to simulate network conditions in Selenium WebDriver?

==> 	In an Automation Engineer interview, a good answer is to explain that Selenium WebDriver itself does not directly throttle network speed, but you can use Chrome DevTools Protocol (CDP) with Selenium 4 to emulate network conditions.

Interview-ready answer

“We can simulate network conditions in Selenium using Chrome DevTools Protocol. Selenium 4 provides access to Chrome DevTools, so we can emulate conditions such as slow 3G, fast 3G, offline mode, or custom latency and download/upload throughput.

For example, I can use CDP to set network conditions before opening or interacting with the application. Then I can verify how the application behaves under slow or unstable network conditions.”
import org.openqa.selenium.chrome.ChromeDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.devtools.DevTools;
import org.openqa.selenium.devtools.v145.network.Network;

import java.util.Optional;

public class NetworkTest {

    public static void main(String[] args) {

        ChromeDriver driver = new ChromeDriver();

        DevTools devTools = driver.getDevTools();
        devTools.createSession();

        devTools.send(Network.enable(
                Optional.empty(),
                Optional.empty(),
                Optional.empty()
        ));

        // Simulate slow network
        devTools.send(Network.emulateNetworkConditions(
                false,       // offline
                200,         // latency in ms
                500 * 1024,  // download throughput
                500 * 1024,  // upload throughput
                Optional.empty()
        ));

        driver.get("https://example.com");

        // Perform validations

        driver.quit();
    }
}

}
What can we simulate?
Condition	|         Example
Offline		|	offline = true
Latency		|	200 ms, 500 ms, etc.
Download speed	|	500 KB/s
Upload speed	|	500 KB/s
Slow 3G		|	High latency + low throughput
Network failure	|	Offline/network interception


Practical automation scenario

Suppose you're testing an AI chatbot UI. Your test could:
Start browser
      ↓
Enable CDP Network
      ↓
Set slow network
      ↓
Open chatbot
      ↓
Send message
      ↓
Verify loading indicator
      ↓
Verify timeout/error handling
      ↓
Verify response after network recovery

This is useful for testing loading states, timeout handling, retries, error messages, and UI behavior when the backend is slow.
#-------------------------------------#
⭐ Strong follow-up answer

If the interviewer asks “Have you actually used this?”, don't claim hands-on experience unless you have. You can say:

“I understand and can implement network throttling using Selenium 4 and Chrome DevTools Protocol. The approach is to create a DevTools session, enable the Network domain, and emulate the required latency and throughput. For more complex network mocking, I would use a proxy tool such as BrowserMob Proxy or an external network simulation tool.

#====================================================================================================
# Q : Describe a challenging situation with developers or deadlines and how you handled it.

==>  One challenging situation I faced was when we had a tight deadline for a release, and some of the automation tests were failing because of issues in the test environment and changes in the application.

The developers were focused on completing the feature and fixing the functional issues, while from the QA side we needed a stable build and enough time to complete regression testing. Because the deadline was close, there was some pressure between development and testing teams.

Instead of treating it as a conflict, I first analyzed the failures and categorized them into three areas: actual product defects, automation issues, and environment/configuration issues. I shared the evidence with the developers, including logs and the exact steps to reproduce the failures, so that we could quickly identify which issues required development changes.

For the automation failures, I worked on fixing the scripts and improving the reusable components. For environment-related problems, I coordinated with the relevant team to get the required configuration and simulation environment stable.

At the same time, I prioritized the regression suite based on critical functionality and release risk. We executed the high-priority tests first instead of waiting for the complete suite to finish.

This helped us avoid unnecessary back-and-forth with developers and focus on the actual blockers. We were able to complete the critical validation within the deadline and provide clear test results to the team.

The main lesson I learned was that during a tight deadline, communication and prioritization are just as important as automation. I always try to bring evidence to discussions and focus on solving the problem rather than assigning blame.

#------------------------------------------------#
How did you handle conflict with the developer?”

Say:

“I don't approach it as QA versus development. I focus on evidence. I provide the failed test case, logs, expected versus actual behavior, and reproduction steps. If it's a genuine defect, we discuss the impact and priority. If it's an automation or environment issue, I take ownership and fix it. This keeps the discussion technical rather than personal.”


#====================================================================================================
# Q :	Root cause of flakiness in Selenium scripts - 
	how to fix, how many ways. Explain Root Cause Analysis techniques ?

==> For an Automation Engineer interview, answer this as RCA + prevention, not just “use explicit waits.” Flaky Selenium tests usually come from synchronization, unstable locators, environment issues, browser/driver differences, test-data dependencies, and poor test isolation.
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
1. What is a flaky Selenium test?
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
“A flaky test is a test that sometimes passes and sometimes fails without any relevant change in the application code or test code.”

For example:

Run 1 → PASS
Run 2 → FAIL
Run 3 → PASS
Run 4 → PASS
Run 5 → FAIL

The important point is: the failure is inconsistent and not reliably reproducible.
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
2. Root causes of Selenium flakiness
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
I would categorize the root causes into these major areas:
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
1. Synchronization / timing issues — MOST COMMON
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Example: driver.findElement(By.id("submit")).click();

The page may not have finished rendering when Selenium tries to click.

Fix:	Use explicit waits based on actual conditions:

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

WebElement button = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("submit")));

button.click();

Prefer:

Wait for condition
       ↓
Element ready
       ↓
Perform action

instead of:

Thread.sleep(5000)
       ↓
Hope element is ready
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
2. Poor / unstable locators
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
For example: // Bad
//div[3]/div[2]/button

If the DOM structure changes, the locator breaks.
Prefer stable attributes:

By.id("loginButton"); or By.cssSelector("[data-testid='login-button']");

Fix:	Use a locator hierarchy such as:

ID
↓
data-testid / stable custom attribute
↓
CSS
↓
reliable XPath

Avoid depending on dynamic classes, indexes, or changing DOM structures.
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
3. Dynamic elements / AJAX
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
	Modern applications frequently update elements asynchronously.

For example:

Click Search
     ↓
API call
     ↓
Spinner
     ↓
DOM updated
     ↓
Results displayed

If Selenium immediately searches for the result, the test can fail.

Fix:	Wait for a meaningful application condition:

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfElementLocated(By.id("searchResult")));

Or wait for the spinner to disappear:

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.invisibilityOfElementLocated(By.id("spinner")));
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
3. StaleElementReferenceException
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
This is another very common cause.

Suppose Selenium finds an element:

WebElement element = driver.findElement(By.id("username"));

Then JavaScript refreshes/rebuilds the DOM. The old reference is no longer valid.

Fix : Locate the element again after the DOM update:

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("username"))).click();

Instead of keeping old WebElement references for too long.
#------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
4. Test data problems
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Sometimes the Selenium script is correct but the test data is not.

Example:

Test expects user = Uzma
But previous test already deleted the user

Then:

Test A → modifies data
        ↓
Test B → expects original data
        ↓
FAIL
Fix : Use:
1. Independent test data
2. Data setup/teardown
3. Unique test data
4. API/database setup where appropriate
5. Avoid dependency between tests
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
5. Test dependency
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#

Bad design:

Test 1 → Create user
Test 2 → Login with user
Test 3 → Update user
Test 4 → Delete user
If Test 1 fails, Tests 2–4 may fail too.

Better : Each test should establish its own required state.
Test 1 → Setup → Execute → Cleanup

Test 2 → Setup → Execute → Cleanup

Test 3 → Setup → Execute → Cleanup

This is called test isolation.

#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
6. Environment instability
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Sometimes the problem isn't Selenium.

For example:

Selenium
   ↓
Application
   ↓
Backend API
   ↓
Database

If the backend is slow or unavailable, the Selenium test may fail.
How I investigate

Check:

1. Application logs
2. Server logs
3. API response
4. Network failures
5. Database availability
6. CPU/memory
7. Test environment status

This is especially important in your embedded/Connected Services work because your automation can involve multiple systems and environments. Your resume specifically mentions simulation environments and end-to-end validation across embedded modules
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
7. Browser / WebDriver mismatch
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#

Chrome = Version 145
ChromeDriver = Version 146

This can cause session or execution failures.

Fix : Maintain compatible:

Browser
   +
WebDriver
   +
Selenium version

Also keep the execution environment consistent across machines/Jenkins agents.
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
8. Network issues
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Selenium → Application
             ↓
          API call
             ↓
       Network delay

The page may take longer than usual.

Fix
1. Explicit waits
2. Appropriate timeout configuration
3. Retry only for genuinely transient operations
4. Monitor network/API failures
5. Avoid arbitrary sleep()
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
9. Pop-ups / overlays / animations
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Suppose:

Button exists
      ↓
Modal overlay still present
      ↓
Selenium clicks button
      ↓
ElementClickInterceptedException

Fix : Wait for the overlay to disappear:
wait.until(ExpectedConditions.invisibilityOfElementLocated(By.cssSelector(".loading-overlay")));
Then click the element.
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
10. Parallel execution problems
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Suppose two tests use the same:

Browser session
Test account
File
Database record

at the same time.

They can interfere with each other.

Fix : Use:
1. Separate WebDriver instances
2. ThreadLocal WebDriver where appropriate
3. Unique test data
4. Independent users/resources
5. Thread-safe utilities
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
11. Poor exception handling
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#

If the test simply fails with:

Element not found : it's difficult to understand the real problem.

Fix : Capture:

1. Screenshot
2. Browser console logs
3. Page source
4. Test logs
5. API/network information where relevant
6. Stack trace

Your resume actually gives you a strong point here: you've worked on advanced logging that captured synchronized serial and video logs during automation execution.


#====================================================================================================# Q :	12. How do you perform Root Cause Analysis?

This is the part I'd emphasize if the interviewer asks “How do you identify the root cause?”
I use a systematic approach.
#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Step 1 — Reproduce
#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
First determine:

Always fails?
Sometimes fails?
Only fails in Jenkins?
Only fails locally?
Only fails in Chrome?
Only fails during parallel execution?

This immediately narrows the problem.

#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Step 2 — Collect evidence
#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
I collect:

1. Test logs
2. Screenshot
3. Stack trace
4. Browser logs
5. Page source 
6. Execution video
7. Application logs
8. API response
9. Environment information

#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Step 3 — Categorize the failure
#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
I classify it as:

1. Application defect
2. Automation defect
3. Synchronization issue
4. Test-data issue
5. Environment issue
6. Infrastructure issue
7. Browser/driver issue
8. Network issue
#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Step 4 — Use 5 Whys
#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Why did the test fail? → Element was not clickable.

Why wasn't it clickable? → Loading overlay was still present.

Why was the overlay still present? → API response was slow.

Why was the API slow? → Backend service was under heavy load.

Why was the service under heavy load? → Another test suite was running simultaneously against the same environment.

Root cause:

Environment contention, not a Selenium locator problem.

This is much better than simply increasing the timeout from 10 to 30 seconds.

#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
13. Other RCA techniques
#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
You can mention these in an interview: 5 Whys
	Best for finding the underlying cause.

Problem
 ↓
Why?
 ↓
Why?
 ↓
Why?
 ↓
Root Cause

Fishbone / Ishikawa Diagram

Categorize possible causes:

                    FLAKY TEST
                         │
       ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
       ↓                 ↓                 ↓
     People           Process           Machine
       ↓                 ↓                 ↓
     Code            Test data          Browser
     Skill            Timing            Driver
                        │
      ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
      ↓                 ↓                 ↓
   Environment         Network       Application
Pareto Analysis

If you have 100 flaky failures, identify which causes account for most failures.

For example:

Synchronization       40%
Test data              25%
Environment            15%
Locator                10%
Browser                 5%
Other                   5%

Then prioritize fixing synchronization and test-data issues first.

Comparison / A-B analysis

Run: Local → PASS , Jenkins → FAIL

Then compare:

Browser version
OS
Resolution
Environment
Network
Test data
Parallelism
Configuration

This is very useful for CI failures.
#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
14. Should we use Retry?
#---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
This is a very important interview point.

Don't say: “I add retry to every flaky test.”

Instead say:

“Retry should not be used as the first solution. First I identify and fix the root cause. Retry can be used for genuinely transient failures such as temporary network or environment issues, but excessive retry can hide real defects.”

That's a strong Automation Engineer answer.

#====================================================================================================# Q : How many ways can we fix Selenium flakiness?

==> You can summarize your approach into 8 major areas:

1. Explicit/condition-based waits
2. Stable locators
3. Proper handling of dynamic elements
4. Test-data management
5. Test isolation
6. Environment/browser stability
7. Better logging and diagnostics
8. Controlled retry for transient failures

⭐ Best 2-minute interview answer

If the interviewer asks this as a direct question, don't explain all 12 causes immediately. Give this answer first:

“The first thing I do when I see Selenium flakiness is identify whether the failure is reproducible. I check whether it happens locally, in Jenkins, only in a particular browser, during parallel execution, or only under certain test data.

The most common root cause I have seen is synchronization. For example, Selenium tries to interact with an element before an AJAX operation or page rendering is completed. I fix this using explicit, condition-based waits rather than unnecessary Thread.sleep.

Other common causes are unstable locators, stale elements, dynamic DOM changes, test-data dependency, test dependency, environment instability, network issues, browser/driver mismatch, and parallel execution conflicts.

For RCA, I first collect evidence such as stack traces, screenshots, logs, browser information and application logs. Then I categorize the failure as an application defect, automation defect, data issue, environment issue, or infrastructure issue.

I also use techniques like 5 Whys, Fishbone analysis and Pareto analysis when the issue is recurring. For example, if an element is not clickable, I don't immediately increase the timeout. I ask why it's not clickable, check whether an overlay is present, whether an API is slow, and whether the environment is overloaded.

Finally, I fix the actual root cause and add preventive measures. I use retry only for genuinely transient failures because retrying everything can hide real defects.”

#====================================================================================================# Q :	How would you add new fields to the object repository?

==> If the interviewer asks “How would you add new fields to the Object Repository?”, they're usually testing your understanding of Page Object Model (POM) and locator management.

Your resume specifically mentions Selenium WebDriver + POM, so answer from that perspective.

In my Selenium framework, I maintain the UI elements using the Page Object Model. When a new field or UI element is introduced, I first identify a stable locator for that element, such as an ID, name, CSS selector, or XPath.

I then add the locator in the corresponding Page Object class rather than directly inside the test case. Along with the locator, I create a reusable method if there is an operation that needs to be performed on that field.

For example, if a new Email field is added to the Login page, I would add its locator to the LoginPage class and create a method such as enterEmail(). The test case then calls that method instead of knowing the actual locator. This keeps the test layer independent from the UI implementation.

Example:  Suppose the application gets a new Email field.

public class LoginPage {

    private WebDriver driver;

    private By username =  By.id("username");

    private By password = By.id("password");
            

    private By loginButton = By.id("login");
            

    public void enterUsername(String value) {
        driver.findElement(username).sendKeys(value);
    }

    public void enterPassword(String value) {
        driver.findElement(password).sendKeys(value);
    }

    public void clickLogin() {
        driver.findElement(loginButton).click();
    }
}

Add the new field
private By email = By.id("email");

Then add its reusable action:
public void enterEmail(String value) {
    driver.findElement(email).sendKeys(value);
}

Now the test remains clean:

LoginPage loginPage = new LoginPage(driver);

loginPage.enterEmail("test@example.com");
loginPage.enterUsername("Priya");
loginPage.enterPassword("password");
loginPage.clickLogin();
        
#----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
If they ask: “What if the locator keeps changing?”
#----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Say:

“I first check whether there is a stable attribute such as id, name, data-testid, or another application-specific attribute. I avoid absolute XPath and index-based locators. If the application generates dynamic IDs, I use a reliable relative CSS/XPath strategy. I also keep the locator only in the Page Object so that if it changes later, I need to update it in one place.”

#----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
If they ask: “Do you put every field in Object Repository?”
#----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
A good answer:

I don't blindly add every element. I add elements that are required for automation and expose reusable actions for them. I keep locators centralized in the Page Object or component layer, while test cases contain only the business flow

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	What is Maven . Explain What is Maven Framework

==> What is Maven?

“Maven is a build automation and dependency management tool primarily used for Java projects. It helps us manage project dependencies, compile the code, execute tests, generate reports, and manage the overall build lifecycle

In an automation project, instead of manually downloading Selenium, TestNG, REST Assured, etc., we define the required dependencies in the pom.xml file.

<dependency>
    <groupId>org.seleniumhq.selenium</groupId>
    <artifactId>selenium-java</artifactId>
    <version>4.41.0</version>
</dependency>
Maven downloads the required JARs and their transitive dependencies automatically.

#-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
What is Maven Framework?
#-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
Strictly speaking, Maven itself is not a testing framework.

A good interview correction is:

“Maven is a build and project management tool, not a test automation framework. In my automation project, Maven is used to manage the Selenium/TestNG project, dependencies, compilation, test execution, and reporting.”

Your actual automation framework could be:
Selenium + Java + TestNG + POM
              │
              ▼
            Maven

Maven manages the project around that framework.
Main components of Maven
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
1. pom.xml : POM = Project Object Model
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
This is the most important Maven file.

It contains things such as:
1. Project information
2. Dependencies
3. Plugins
4. Build configuration
5. Test configuration
6. Java version

<project>

    <groupId>com.uzma</groupId>
    <artifactId>selenium-framework</artifactId>
    <version>1.0</version>

    <dependencies>

        <!-- Selenium -->
        <dependency>
            <groupId>org.seleniumhq.selenium</groupId>

</project>

Remote Repository
       ↑
       │
Central Repository

Local repository: Dependencies downloaded to your machine, generally under .m2.

Central repository: Maven's public repository containing many commonly used Java libraries.

Remote/private repository: Organizations can maintain their own repository for internal dependencies.

Maven also provides a standard project structure and build lifecycle. For example, using mvn clean test, Maven cleans the previous build, compiles the project and executes the configured test cases.

# Q : What is pom.xml?
==> 	pom.xml stands for Project Object Model. It is the main configuration file of a Maven project. It contains information about the project, dependencies, plugins, build configuration, and test execution configuration.
In a Selenium automation project, I use pom.xml to manage libraries such as Selenium WebDriver, TestNG, REST Assured, etc., instead of manually downloading JAR files.

POM stands for Project Object Model. pom.xml is the main configuration file of a Maven project. In my Selenium automation framework, I use it to manage project dependencies like Selenium and TestNG, define their versions, configure Maven plugins such as Surefire, and control the build and test execution. For example, when I run mvn clean test, Maven reads the pom.xml, resolves the required dependencies, compiles the project and executes the configured tests


Important sections of pom.xml

1. groupId : Identifies the organization/project.

	<groupId>com.uzma</groupId>

2. ArtifactId : The name of the project/artifact.

	<artifactId>selenium-framework</artifactId>

3. version : Specifies the project version.

	<version>1.0</version>

4. dependencies : Defines external libraries required by the project.

	<dependencies>
	    ...
	</dependencies>

For example:
1. Selenium
2. TestNG
3. REST Assured
4. Jackson
5. Apache POI

5. plugins : Plugins control Maven build and execution behavior.

For example, the Maven Surefire Plugin can be configured to execute TestNG tests.

How it works in your Selenium framework
                pom.xml
                   │
       ┌────────┼────────┐
       ↓           ↓           ↓
   Selenium      TestNG   REST Assured
       │           │          │
       └────────┼────────┘
                   ↓
                 Maven
                  ↓
             mvn clean test
                  ↓
             Test execution
                  ↓
              Test results
