# Q :  What is Maven lifecycle?

==> 	Maven lifecycle is a predefined sequence of phases that Maven follows to build, test, package, verify, install, and deploy a project. Each phase performs a specific task, and Maven executes the phases in order.

Maven has three built-in lifecycles:

1. Default lifecycle – handles the project build
2. Clean lifecycle – cleans previous build output
3. Site lifecycle – generates project documentation/site

The Default lifecycle is the most important one for Selenium automation interviews.

1. Maven Default Lifecycle

The major phases are:
validate
   ↓
compile
   ↓
test
   ↓
package
   ↓
verify
   ↓
install
   ↓
deploy

1. validate : Checks whether the project is correctly configured and all required information is available.

	Example: mvn validate
It validates the Maven project structure/configuration before continuing.

2. compile : Compiles the main source code.
	mvn compile
src/main/java
       ↓
     compile
       ↓
target/classes

If your Java code has compilation errors, this phase fails.

3. test : Compiles and executes the test cases using the configured test framework/plugin.

	mvn test
For your Selenium + TestNG framework:
TestNG Tests
      ↓
    Maven
      ↓
Surefire Plugin
      ↓
Test execution

This is one of the most commonly used Maven commands in automation.

4. package : Packages the compiled application into the appropriate distributable format.

	For example: mvn package
Depending on the project, Maven may create:

.jar
.war

For a typical Selenium test automation project, the important point is that Maven completes the build/package phase after executing the relevant earlier phases.

5. verify : Performs additional checks on the project to verify that the package meets the required criteria.

	mvn verify
It occurs after tests and packaging and can be used for additional verification/integration checks.

6. install : Installs the generated artifact into your local Maven repository.

	 mvn install

Usually the local repository is:  ~/.m2/repository

Project
   ↓
Build
   ↓
Package
   ↓
Install
   ↓
Local Maven Repository

7. deploy : Publishes the artifact to a remote Maven repository.

	mvn deploy

For example, an organization may have a private artifact repository.
Developer Machine
       ↓
    Maven Build
       ↓
     Package
       ↓
    Install
       ↓
 Remote Repository
#------------------------------------------------------------------#
2. Very Important Maven Concept
#------------------------------------------------------------------#
If you execute:

mvn test

Maven doesn't execute only the test phase.

It executes all required earlier phases:

validate
   ↓
compile
   ↓
test

Similarly : mvn package
runs
validate
   ↓
compile
   ↓
test
   ↓
package

& mvn install runs : 
validate
   ↓
compile
   ↓
test
   ↓
package
   ↓
verify
   ↓
install

#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
3. Clean Lifecycle
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
The Clean lifecycle is separate from the Default lifecycle.

Its main phases are:

pre-clean
   ↓
clean
   ↓
post-clean

The most commonly used command is: mvn clean
	It removes the target directory and previous build output.
Before:

target/
 ├── classes/
 ├── test-classes/
 └── reports/

        ↓ mvn clean

After:

target/  → removed

#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
4. Site Lifecycle
#-------------------------------------------------------------------------------#
The Site lifecycle is used to generate project documentation and reports.

Its phases include:

pre-site
   ↓
site
   ↓
post-site
   ↓
site-deploy

For a Selenium interview, you generally don't need to go deeply into this unless the interviewer specifically asks.

#===============================================================================================
# Q : What happens with mvn clean test?

==> If I execute: mvn clean test

Maven executes two lifecycle phases:

First:
	clean
 	  ↓
	Removes target/

Then:
	validate
 	   ↓
	compile
 	   ↓
	  test

So the overall flow is:

mvn clean test

     │
     ▼
Remove old target
     │
     ▼
Validate project
     │
     ▼
Compile code
     │
     ▼
Compile tests
     │
     ▼
Execute tests
     │
     ▼
Generate test results

This is commonly useful in Jenkins because you're starting with a clean build.
6. Maven Lifecycle in your Automation Framework

For your Selenium + Java + TestNG framework, you can explain:
                    pom.xml
                       │
                       ▼
                     Maven
                       │
                       ▼
                  Clean Build
                       │
                       ▼
                   Compile Code
                       │
                       ▼
                  Compile Tests
                       │
                       ▼
                 Surefire Plugin
                       │
                       ▼
                  TestNG Tests
                       │
                       ▼
                Test Execution
                       │
                       ▼
               Reports / Results

| Command             | Purpose                        |
| ------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| `mvn validate`      | Validate project               |
| `mvn compile`       | Compile main code              |
| `mvn test`          | Run tests                      |
| `mvn package`       | Package project                |
| `mvn verify`        | Perform verification           |
| `mvn install`       | Install artifact locally       |
| `mvn deploy`        | Deploy artifact remotely       |
| `mvn clean`         | Remove previous build output   |
| `mvn clean test`    | Clean and execute tests        |
| `mvn clean install` | Clean, build, test and install |
--------------------------------------------------------

⭐ Best interview answer

If the interviewer asks “Explain Maven lifecycle”, you can give this answer:

“Maven lifecycle is a predefined sequence of phases used to manage the complete build process of a Maven project. Maven has three built-in lifecycles: Clean, Default, and Site.

The Default lifecycle is the most important for my automation project. Its important phases are validate, compile, test, package, verify, install, and deploy.

Validate checks the project configuration, compile compiles the source code, test executes the test cases, package creates the distributable artifact, verify performs additional checks, install puts the artifact into the local Maven repository, and deploy publishes it to a remote repository.

For Selenium automation, I commonly use mvn test to execute the TestNG test suite and mvn clean test when I want to clean the previous build and execute the tests from a fresh state. Maven executes the required lifecycle phases sequentially.

There is also the Clean lifecycle, mainly used to remove the previous target directory, and the Site lifecycle, which is used for generating project documentation and reports

Summary : 
Default: Validate → Compile → Test → Package → Verify → Install → Deploy

Clean: Clean the old build

Site: Generate project documentation


#====================================================================================================
# Q : Difference between mvn clean, mvn test, and mvn install?
==> 	The core difference between these three Apache Maven commands lies in which lifecycle they belong to and how much of the build process they execute.
• mvn clean belongs to the Clean Lifecycle and simply deletes the target directory to wipe away all previous build artifacts.

• mvn test belongs to the Default Lifecycle and stops after compiling the code and running your unit tests.

• mvn install also belongs to the Default Lifecycle, but it goes all the way—compiling, testing, packaging the code into a JAR/WAR file, and moving that file into your local repository (~/.m2)

Understanding the Sequential Nature of Maven
Maven operates on sequential build phases. When you run a command in the Default Lifecycle, Maven automatically runs every phase that comes before it.

Command	Deletes Old Files?	Compiles Code?	Runs Tests?	Creates JAR/WAR?	Copies to 											Local .m2?
mvn clean	Yes			❌ No		❌ No		❌ No		❌ No
mvn test	❌ No			Yes		Yes		❌ No		❌ No
mvn install	❌ No			Yes		Yes		Yes		Yes

Detailed Breakdown
1. mvn clean

Purpose: Removes previously generated build files.
• What it does: 
	Deletes the target directory where Maven stores compiled .class files, reports, and packaged JARs from previous builds.
mvn clean removes the previous build artifacts from the target directory so that we can perform a fresh build.
• Why use it: 
	To guarantee a fresh start. If you rename files or delete code, older compiled versions can sometimes linger in the target folder and cause unpredictable bugs.

2. mvn test
Purpose: Compiles the application and test code, then executes the tests.
mvn test compiles the source and test code and executes the test cases. It is mainly used when I want to run my automation test suite without creating or installing the final artifact.
• What it does: 
	Executes the sequence: validate → compile → test-compile → test. It compiles your source and test code, then runs your unit tests (using frameworks like JUnit or TestNG).
• Why use it: 
	When you are actively coding, practicing Test-Driven Development (TDD), or verifying that a quick bug fix didn't break existing functionality.

3. mvn install :
Purpose:  Builds the project, runs tests, packages the application, and installs the generated artifact into the local Maven repository.
mvn install performs the complete build up to the install phase, including compilation, testing, packaging, and installing the generated JAR or other artifact into the local .m2 repository.

• What it does: 
	Executes almost the entire default lifecycle: validate → compile → test → package → verify → install. It builds the final package (like a .jar or .war file) and installs it into your local machine's Maven repository (usually located at ~/.m2/repository).
• Why use it: 
	When your current project is needed as a dependency by another local project on your machine. Once "installed," other projects can look inside your .m2 directory and use it.
For example, if Maven creates:

target/my-project-1.0.jar

it installs it into:

~/.m2/repository/

This allows another local Maven project to use that artifact as a dependency.

#---------------------------------------------------------------#
What mvn clean install does ?
mvn clean install first removes all previous build artifacts from the target directory and then performs the Maven build through the install phase. It compiles the code, executes tests, packages the application, performs verification, and finally installs the generated artifact into the local .m2 repository

1. clean
   ↓
   Deletes target/ directory

2. compile
   ↓
   Compiles Java source code

3. test-compile
   ↓
   Compiles test code

4. test
   ↓
   Executes test cases

5. package
   ↓
   Creates JAR/WAR artifact

6. verify
   ↓
   Performs verification checks

7. install
   ↓
   Copies the generated artifact into ~/.m2/repository/

#====================================================================================================
# Q :  What is Maven Surefire Plugin?
==> Maven Surefire Plugin is a Maven plugin used to execute unit and automation tests during the Maven test phase. In my automation projects, I have used it with TestNG to control which test classes or test methods should be executed and to generate test execution reports.

<plugin>
    <groupId>org.apache.maven.plugins</groupId>
    <artifactId>maven-surefire-plugin</artifactId>
    <version>3.5.3</version>
    <configuration>
        <suiteXmlFiles>
            <suiteXmlFile>testng.xml</suiteXmlFile>
        </suiteXmlFiles>
    </configuration>
</plugin>

In a Jenkins CI pipeline, Maven Surefire is useful because it executes the automated tests as part of the Maven build and generates test reports that can be consumed by Jenkins.

So, in simple terms, I would say Surefire is the Maven component responsible for running my TestNG/JUnit tests and producing the test execution results.

#-------------------------------------------------------------#
If the interviewer asks: "Why do you use Surefire?"

You can answer:

I use Surefire because it integrates TestNG execution with the Maven lifecycle. It allows me to control test execution from the command line or pom.xml, supports suite-based and parallel execution, and generates test results that can be used for CI reporting

#====================================================================================================
# Q : How do you execute only a particular TestNG class using Maven?

==> 1. Run a specific TestNG class

Suppose your class is: src/test/java/tests/LoginTest.java

Run: mvn test -Dtest=LoginTest

This tells Maven to execute only LoginTest

2. If the class is in a package
package tests;

public class LoginTest {
    // test methods
}
Run : mvn test -Dtest=LoginTest 
OR    mvn test -Dtest=tests.LoginTest

3. Run a specific test method

If LoginTest contains:
@Test
public void validLogin() {}

@Test
public void invalidLogin() {}

You can execute only validLogin:  mvn test -Dtest=LoginTest#validLogin

4. Multiple classes

You can run multiple specific classes: mvn test -Dtest=LoginTest,CheckoutTest

To execute a particular TestNG class using Maven, I use the Maven Surefire Plugin with the -Dtest parameter. For example, mvn test -Dtest=LoginTest executes only the LoginTest class. If I want to execute a specific test method, I can use mvn test -Dtest=LoginTest#validLogin

#====================================================================================================
# Q :  How do you pass environment/browser parameters from Maven?
==> 	In my automation framework, I pass environment-specific parameters such as browser, environment, or execution mode from the Maven command line using -D system properties. This helps me run the same automation code against different environments or browsers without changing the code
run : mvn test -Dbrowser=chrome -Denv=qa

In Java, I can read these values using:
String browser = System.getProperty("browser", "chrome");
String env = System.getProperty("env", "qa");

Then I use these values during driver initialization and environment configuration:
if (browser.equalsIgnoreCase("chrome")) {
    driver = new ChromeDriver();
} else if (browser.equalsIgnoreCase("firefox")) {
    driver = new FirefoxDriver();
}
For environment configuration, I can load the corresponding properties:

config-qa.properties
config-stage.properties
config-prod.properties
Then I can execute:

mvn test -Dbrowser=firefox -Denv=stage

The same framework will run on Firefox against the Stage environment.

n Jenkins

I can expose these as Jenkins parameters, for example:

Browser: chrome
Environment: QA

Jenkins then passes them to Maven:
mvn clean test -Dbrowser=${BROWSER} -Denv=${ENV}

Another common approach with TestNG

If the framework uses testng.xml, Maven parameters can be passed to TestNG through Surefire configuration, and TestNG can retrieve them using:

@Parameters({"browser", "env"})
@Test
public void loginTest(String browser, String env) {
    // test execution
}

I normally pass runtime parameters using Maven system properties with -D, such as mvn test -Dbrowser=chrome -Denv=qa. I retrieve them using System.getProperty() and use them for driver initialization and environment configuration. In Jenkins, I expose browser and environment as build parameters and pass those values to Maven, which allows the same automation framework to run across different browsers and environments without code changes.

#====================================================================================================
# Q : How do you run Maven tests in Jenkins?
==> In my automation projects, I integrate the Maven project with Jenkins so that the test suite can be executed automatically as part of the CI pipeline.

First, I configure the required JDK, Maven, and Git in Jenkins. Then I configure the job to pull the latest code from the Git repository.

For a freestyle Jenkins job, under Build → Execute shell/Windows batch command, I can run:

mvn clean test

If I want to execute a specific TestNG class:

mvn test -Dtest=LoginTest

I can also pass runtime parameters such as browser and environment:

mvn clean test -Dbrowser=chrome -Denv=qa
Jenkins captures the Maven console output and the test results generated by the framework. With the Maven Surefire reports, I can publish the test results in Jenkins and see passed, failed, and skipped test cases.

In a Pipeline job, I can execute Maven from a Jenkinsfile, for example:

pipeline {
    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            steps {
                checkout scm
            }
        }

        stage('Run Tests') {
            steps {
                bat 'mvn clean test -Dbrowser=chrome -Denv=qa'
            }
        }
    }
}

#---------------------------------------------------------------------#
"What happens when tests fail?"

If a test fails, Maven normally returns a non-zero exit code, so Jenkins marks the build as failed. I then check the Jenkins console logs, Surefire/TestNG reports, screenshots or other framework logs to identify the failure. If the failure is due to a genuine application defect, I log a defect; if it is an automation or environment issue, I perform RCA and fix the framework or environment accordingly.

One-line answer:

I configure Jenkins to checkout the Maven project, execute mvn clean test with required parameters, and publish the Surefire/TestNG results so the CI job provides automated pass/fail feedback.

#====================================================================================================
# Q : What is the difference between dependency and plugin in Maven?

==>	The main difference is that a dependency is a library that my application or test code uses, whereas a plugin performs a build-related task for Maven.	
1. Dependency

A dependency provides classes and functionality that I use in my Java code.

For example, in a Selenium automation framework:

<dependency>
    <groupId>org.seleniumhq.selenium</groupId>
    <artifactId>selenium-java</artifactId>
    <version>4.41.0</version>
</dependency>

2. Plugin

A plugin provides functionality that Maven uses during the build lifecycle.

For example, the Maven Surefire Plugin executes my TestNG tests:

<plugin>
    <groupId>org.apache.maven.plugins</groupId>
    <artifactId>maven-surefire-plugin</artifactId>
    <version>3.5.3</version>
</plugin>

Other examples:

Maven Compiler Plugin → compiles Java code
Maven Surefire Plugin → executes tests
Maven Failsafe Plugin → executes integration tests
Maven JAR Plugin → creates JAR files

Easy way to remember

Dependency = What my code needs.
Plugin = What Maven needs to build/test my project.

Strong interview closing:

In my automation framework, TestNG and Selenium are dependencies because my test code directly uses them, while Surefire is a plugin because Maven uses it to execute the TestNG tests during the Maven lifecycle.

#====================================================================================================
# Q : * How do you handle multiple frames in Selenium?

==> 	In Selenium, if an element is inside an iframe, I first need to switch the WebDriver's context from the main page to that particular frame using switchTo().frame(). After completing the operations inside the frame, I switch back using parentFrame() or defaultContent()

1. Frame using WebElement

If I can locate the iframe:
WebElement frame = driver.findElement(By.id("paymentFrame"));
driver.switchTo().frame(frame);

driver.findElement(By.id("cardNumber")).sendKeys("123456");

driver.switchTo().defaultContent();

2. Frame using index

If there are multiple frames and the index is reliable: driver.switchTo().frame(0);

But I generally avoid index-based switching because the index can change when the page structure changes.

3. Frame using name or ID : driver.switchTo().frame("paymentFrame");
	This works when the iframe has a suitable name or id.

4. Handling nested/multiple frames

Suppose the structure is:

Main Page
   └── Frame A
        └── Frame B
             └── Element

I switch into them sequentially:
driver.switchTo().frame("frameA");
driver.switchTo().frame("frameB");

driver.findElement(By.id("submit")).click();

driver.switchTo().parentFrame();    // Back to Frame A
driver.switchTo().defaultContent(); // Back to Main Page

parentFrame() 			vs defaultContent()
Method		 : Purpose
parentFrame()	 : Moves one level up to the immediate parent frame
defaultContent() : Moves directly back to the main page

#--------------------------------------------------#
What if the frame is not immediately available?
==> 	For dynamic applications, I use ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt():

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt(By.id("paymentFrame")));

This is preferable to using Thread.sleep() because Selenium waits until the frame is available.

⭐ Strong interview answer

"For multiple frames, I identify the frame hierarchy first. I switch into the required frame using switchTo().frame(). If frames are nested, I switch sequentially from the parent to the child frame. After completing the operation, I use parentFrame() to move one level back or defaultContent() to return to the main page. For dynamically loaded frames, I prefer frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt() with an explicit wait rather than using hard waits.

#====================================================================================================
# Q : Given 10 links, how would you print the title of each using the optimal approach?

==> If I have 10 links on a web page and need to print the title of each page, I would avoid opening and closing the browser repeatedly. I would first collect all the links, then navigate to each URL using the same WebDriver instance and get the page title.

List<WebElement> links = driver.findElements(By.tagName("a"));

for (WebElement link : links) {
    String url = link.getAttribute("href");

    if (url != null && !url.isEmpty()) {
        driver.get(url);
        System.out.println("URL: " + url);
        System.out.println("Title: " + driver.getTitle());
    }
}

If exactly 10 links are required
List<WebElement> links = driver.findElements(By.tagName("a"));

for (int i = 0; i < Math.min(10, links.size()); i++) {
    String url = links.get(i).getAttribute("href");

    if (url != null && !url.isEmpty()) {
        driver.navigate().to(url);
        System.out.println(driver.getTitle());
    }
}

How I would explain the optimization in an interview

"I would use findElements() once to get all the links instead of locating each link separately. I would extract the href values first and then navigate using the same WebDriver instance. This avoids creating multiple browser sessions and reduces unnecessary Selenium interactions. If the links are independent, for a larger number of URLs I could also consider parallel execution using TestNG with ThreadLocal<WebDriver>, but for just 10 links, sequential execution with a single driver is simpler and avoids unnecessary overhead."	

#====================================================================================================
# Q : In TestNG, how do you ensure screenshots are taken only for failed test cases?

==>	In TestNG, I handle screenshots for failed test cases using the ITestListener interface. I override the onTestFailure() method, so the screenshot is captured only when a test fails.
1. I usually create a reusable screenshot utility and call it from the listener.

public class TestListener implements ITestListener {

    @Override
    public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {

        WebDriver driver = DriverManager.getDriver();

        String testName = result.getName();

        TakesScreenshot ts = (TakesScreenshot) driver;

        File source = ts.getScreenshotAs(OutputType.FILE);

        File destination =  new File("./screenshots/" + testName + ".png");

        try {
            Files.copy(source.toPath(), destination.toPath());
        } catch (IOException e) {
            e.printStackTrace();
        }
    }
}
2. Then I register the listener in the TestNG class:
@Listeners(TestListener.class)
public class LoginTest {

    @Test
    public void validLoginTest() {
        // test steps
    }

    @Test
    public void invalidLoginTest() {
        // test steps
    }
}

I use TestNG's ITestListener because it provides lifecycle callbacks. In onTestFailure(), I get the WebDriver instance and capture the screenshot using TakesScreenshot. Since the screenshot logic is inside onTestFailure(), screenshots are generated only for failed test cases. I also use the test method name in the filename so that the screenshot can be easily identified."

For a parallel framework, I would use a ThreadLocal<WebDriver> or a driver manager so that the listener captures the screenshot from the correct browser session/thread

#====================================================================================================
# Q :  How to extract only failed test case results from 200 tests when 49 fail — best approach?

==> If I have 200 test cases and 49 are failed, and I need to extract only those 49 failed test cases, I would use TestNG's ITestListener with ITestResult.FAILURE.

Since my framework uses Selenium + Java + TestNG + Cucumber, I can capture failed scenarios during execution and store their names in a list or generate a report.

1. Using ITestListener
public class TestListener implements ITestListener {

    @Override
    public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {

        String testName = result.getMethod().getMethodName();

        System.out.println("FAILED TEST: " + testName);

        // Store failed test name
        FailedTestData.failedTests.add(testName);
    }
}

A common utility class:

public class FailedTestData {

    public static List<String> failedTests = new ArrayList<>();
}

After execution, I can print only the failed tests:

for (String test : FailedTestData.failedTests) {
    System.out.println(test);
}

Output would contain only the 49 failed tests, not all 200.

2. In Cucumber + TestNG

In a Cucumber framework, I would identify the failed scenario from the ITestResult/Cucumber execution context and capture the scenario name.

For example:

@Override
public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {

    String scenarioName = result.getName();

    System.out.println("Failed Scenario: " + scenarioName);
}
I would also capture:

1. Scenario name
2. Feature name
3. Failure exception
4. Screenshot
5. Execution time
6. Browser/environment

This information can then be added to the Extent Report/Allure report.

#----------------------------------------------------------#
3. If I need to rerun only those 49 tests

This is where I would make the framework more efficient.

I would use TestNG's retry/rerun mechanism or generate a list of failed tests and execute only those tests.

For Cucumber, I can also use the generated rerun.txt file:
target/failed-scenarios.txt

The file contains failed scenarios, for example:

@Login.feature:25
@Checkout.feature:42
@Payment.feature:18

Then I can configure Cucumber to execute those failed scenarios.
target/failed-scenarios.txt

#------------------------------------------------#
Interview-style final answer

"If 200 tests are executed and 49 fail, I don't need to process all 200 again. I use TestNG's ITestListener and implement onTestFailure(). Whenever a test fails, I capture its test or scenario name, exception, and screenshot and store the information in a collection or reporting system. At the end of execution, I have only the 49 failed tests. In a Cucumber framework, I can also generate a rerun file containing only failed scenarios and use it to rerun those scenarios. This is useful in CI/CD because instead of executing all 200 tests again, I can execute only the failed 49 tests."

Best approach: ITestListener → onTestFailure() → collect failed tests → report/rerun only failures


#====================================================================================================
# Q :	How do you generate rerun file
==> For a Selenium + Java + TestNG + Cucumber framework, I generate a Cucumber rerun file using the rerun plugin in the Cucumber runner.

1. Configure the Cucumber TestNG Runner

@CucumberOptions(
    features = "src/test/resources/features",
    glue = {"stepDefinitions"},
    plugin = {
        "pretty",
        "html:target/cucumber-report.html",
        "rerun:target/failed-scenarios.txt"
    },
    monochrome = true
)
public class TestRunner extends AbstractTestNGCucumberTests {
}

The important part is:

"rerun:target/failed-scenarios.txt"

Cucumber automatically writes the failed scenario locations into this file.

For example, after 200 scenarios execute and 49 fail, the file may contain:
src/test/resources/features/Login.feature:25
src/test/resources/features/Checkout.feature:42
src/test/resources/features/Payment.feature:18

So the file contains only the failed scenarios.

2. Create a runner for failed scenarios

Then I create a separate runner:

@CucumberOptions(
    features = "@target/failed-scenarios.txt",
    glue = {"stepDefinitions"},
    plugin = {
        "pretty",
        "html:target/rerun-report.html"
    },
    monochrome = true
)
public class FailedTestRunner extends AbstractTestNGCucumberTests {
}

==> The important part is:

features = "@target/failed-scenarios.txt"

The @ tells Cucumber to read the feature/scenario locations from the rerun file.

#-----------------------------------------#
Interview explanation

"I generate the rerun file using Cucumber's rerun plugin. In my main runner, I configure rerun:target/failed-scenarios.txt. After execution, Cucumber writes only the failed scenario locations into that file. Then I create a separate runner with features = '@target/failed-scenarios.txt'. This allows me to rerun only the failed scenarios instead of executing all 200 tests again."

#====================================================================================================
# Q : How do you decide which test cases to automate?
==> I don't automate every test case. I prioritize test cases based on business importance, repetition, stability, and automation ROI.

I generally consider these factors:

High-priority business scenarios
Critical flows such as login, payment, checkout, API integration, or core functionality should be automated.
Frequently executed test cases
Regression and sanity test cases that run in every release are good automation candidates.
Repetitive manual tests
If a test is executed repeatedly and has predictable steps, automation can save significant time.
Stable functionality
I prefer automating features whose requirements and UI are relatively stable. Frequently changing UI can create high maintenance.
Data-driven scenarios
When the same flow needs to be executed with many combinations of test data, automation provides good value.
Cross-browser/platform testing
Scenarios that need to be executed across Chrome, Edge, Firefox, different environments, etc., are good candidates.
Long and time-consuming tests
Tests that take significant manual effort are useful to automate, provided the application behavior is stable.
Negative and boundary scenarios
These can also be automated when they are repeatable and have clear expected results.
What I generally don't automate initially
Frequently changing requirements/UI
One-time tests
Exploratory testing
Usability/visual assessment that requires human judgment
Tests where automation maintenance would cost more than the execution benefit
Example from my experience

"In my projects, I prioritize sanity and regression scenarios first because they need to run frequently. For example, if I have 100 regression test cases and 30 are critical and executed in every release, I would prioritize those 30 first. Then I look at repetitive, data-driven, and time-consuming scenarios. I also consider application stability and maintenance effort before adding a test to the automation suite."

Strong interview conclusion

"My approach is basically risk versus ROI. I prioritize tests that are business-critical, repetitive, stable, time-consuming, and frequently executed. I don't automate simply because a test can be automated; I automate when it provides measurable value in terms of execution time, regression coverage, and reliability."


#====================================================================================================
# Q :  How to download all the driver executables automatically?
==>	The automatic download of the drivers can be done by WebDriverManager. WebDriverManager is a library that allows controlling web browsers programmatically. It provides a cross-browser API that can be used to drive web browsers (e.g., Chrome, Edge, or Firefox, among others) using different programming languages (e.g., Java, JavaScript, Python, C#, or Ruby). The primary use of Selenium WebDriver is implementing automated tests for web applications.

The communication between the WebDriver API and the driver binary is done using a standard protocol called W3C WebDriver (formerly the so-called JSON Wire Protocol). Then, the communication between the driver and the browser is done using the native capabilities of each browser.

The basic use of these managers is the following:

1. WebDriverManager.chromedriver().setup();
2. WebDriverManager.firefoxdriver().setup();
3. WebDriverManager.edgedriver().setup();
4. WebDriverManager.operadriver().setup();
5. WebDriverManager.chromiumdriver().setup()
6. WebDriverManager.iedriver().setup();

How To add WebDriverManager to a Selenium project using Maven or Gradle?
To use WebDriverManager in a Maven built project, the below-mentioned dependency is needed to add to the pom.xml.


<!-- https://mvnrepository.com/artifact/io.github.bonigarcia/webdrivermanager -->
<dependency>
    <groupId>io.github.bonigarcia</groupId>
    <artifactId>webdrivermanager</artifactId>
    <version>5.1.0</version>
</dependency>

#====================================================================================================
# Q : Element is not clickable due to overlay – how will you handle it?
==> With 3.5 years of SDET experience, if an element is not clickable because an overlay, popup, loader, or another element is covering it, I would first identify the root cause instead of immediately using JavaScript click.

How I handle it

1. Identify the overlay
	I inspect the DOM and check whether there is a:

1. Loading spinner
2. Cookie popup
3. Modal/dialog
4. Advertisement
5. Backdrop/overlay
6. Sticky header

2. Wait for the overlay to disappear
	I prefer an explicit wait:

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.invisibilityOfElementLocated(By.cssSelector(".loading-overlay")));

WebElement loginButton = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("loginBtn")));

loginButton.click();

This is my preferred approach, because I'm synchronizing with the application's actual state.

3. If the overlay requires an action	
	For example, if a cookie popup is blocking the button:

WebElement accept = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("acceptCookies")));
accept.click();

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("loginBtn"))).click();

4. Scroll the element into view if required

WebElement element = driver.findElement(By.id("loginBtn"));

((JavascriptExecutor) driver).executeScript("arguments[0].scrollIntoView({block:'center'});", element
);

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(element)).click();

5. JavaScript click — last option

If the element is genuinely clickable from the UI but Selenium's native click is still having an issue, I may use:

((JavascriptExecutor) driver).executeScript("arguments[0].click();", element);

But I don't use JavaScript click as the first solution, because it can bypass the actual user interaction and may hide a real UI synchronization problem.

Strong interview answer

"If an element is not clickable because of an overlay, I first inspect the DOM to identify what is blocking it. If it's a loader, popup, or modal, I use an explicit wait such as invisibilityOfElementLocated() and then wait for the target element to be clickable. If necessary, I handle the popup and scroll the element into view. JavaScript click is my last option because it can bypass normal browser interaction and hide synchronization issues. I always try to fix the root cause rather than simply forcing the click."

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	What is a window handle in Selenium?

==>	A window handle is a unique identifier that Selenium uses to identify and switch between browser windows or tabs. getWindowHandle() returns the current window's handle, while getWindowHandles() returns all open window handles. In my automation framework, when a new tab or window opens, I store the parent handle, get all handles, switch to the new handle, perform the required validation, and then switch back to the parent window

1. A window handle stores the unique address of the browser windows. It is just a pointer to a window, whose return type is alphanumeric. The window handle in Selenium helps in handling multiple windows and child windows. Each browser will have a unique window handle value with which we can uniquely identify it.
2. A window handle in Selenium is a unique identifier assigned by the browser to each open browser window or tab.

3. Selenium uses window handles to switch between multiple windows or tabs.


String parentWindow = driver.getWindowHandle();

getWindowHandle() returns the handle of the current window.

Set<String> allWindows = driver.getWindowHandles();

String parentWindow = driver.getWindowHandle();

driver.findElement(By.id("openNewTab")).click();

Set<String> allWindows = driver.getWindowHandles();

for (String window : allWindows) {
    if (!window.equals(parentWindow)) {
        driver.switchTo().window(window);
        break;
    }
}

#====================================================================================================
# Q :  What are the different methods used for window handling in Selenium?

==> Selenium WebDriver provides various methods for handling of windows. Few of them are:

getWindowHandle( ): When a website opens, we need to handle the main window i.e the parent window using driver.getWindowHandle( ); method. With this method, we get a unique ID of the current window which will identify it within this driver instance. This method will return the value of the String type.
getWindowHandles( ): To handle all opened windows which are the child windows by web driver, we use driver.getWindowHandles( ); method. The windows store in a Set of String type and here we can see the transition from one window to another window in a web application. Its return type is Set <String>.
switchto(): Using this method we perform switch operation within windows.
action: This method helps in performing certain actions on the windows.


#====================================================================================================
# Q :	What is a headless browser?
==>	A headless browser is a term used to define browser simulation programs that do not have a GUI. These programs execute like any other browser but do not display any UI. In headless browsers, when Selenium tests run, they execute in the background. Almost all modern browsers provide the capabilities to run them in a headless mode.
#-----------------------------------#
Why use a headless browser for test execution?
Running a Selenium test case in headless mode provides the following benefits:

1. Useful in CI pipeline: When we need to execute automated test cases remotely on a server or in any of the build and release pipelines for continuous integration servers like Jenkins, it is not always possible to install real browsers on such remote machines. We can use headless browsers to run automation tests efficiently.

2. Beneficial in web scraping: When you want to write a web scraper or data extractor that needs to visit some websites and collect data, headless browsers are a perfect choice. Because we are not concerned about functionality in these cases, we visit web pages and get the data.

3. Support for multiple browser versions: Sometimes, the tester would like to simulate multiple browser versions on the same machine. In that case, you would want to use a headless browser because most of them support the simulation of different versions of browsers.

4.Faster automation test execution: The performance with a headless browser is better compared to real browser automation. The real browsers like Google Chrome, Firefox, and Internet Explorer take a significant amount of time to load CSS, JavaScript, Images, and open and render HTML. Headless browsers do not require all this to load and will start performing functions without waiting for a page to load completely. When we need to run the regression scripts, in headless browsers, we could save time as there are much faster and can render results quickly.

5. Multi-Tasking: Headless browsers can help you, multi-task.You can use your browser or your machine to do anything else while the tests run in the background. Save hours that we otherwise spend staring at the screen.
Even though running the Selenium test provides these benefits, certain limitations are also attached to the same. A few of them are as listed below:

Limitations of headless browser testing
1. Debugging will not be feasible, as the only way to check what's running on the browser is to grab the screenshots and validate the output.

2. Headless browsers don't mimic the exact user behavior, as the page doesn't render precisely with all the dependencies that it will render in an actual browser.

3. Cosmetic bugs like the location of a web element, the color of a web element may get missed while running the tests in headless mode.

# Q :	What is AutoIt
==>	AutoIt v3 is a freeware BASIC-like scripting language designed for automating the Windows GUI and general scripting. It uses a combination of simulated keystrokes, mouse movement and window/control manipulation in order to automate tasks in a way not possible or reliable with other languages (e.g. VBScript and SendKeys). AutoIt is also very small, self-contained and will run on all versions of Windows out-of-the-box with no annoying “runtimes” required!



#====================================================================================================
# Q : Can you explain a real scenario where inheritance caused an issue?

==>  Yes. In one of my automation frameworks, I initially used inheritance heavily by creating a common BaseTest class and extending it in all my test classes.

For example:

public class BaseTest {
    protected WebDriver driver;

    @BeforeMethod
    public void setUp() {
        driver = new ChromeDriver();
    }

    @AfterMethod
    public void tearDown() {
        driver.quit();
    }
}

Then:

public class LoginTest extends BaseTest {
    
    @Test
    public void loginTest() {
        driver.get("https://www.saucedemo.com");
        // test steps
    }
}

Initially, this worked well because common setup and teardown were reused.

But later, when the framework grew, I started adding more functionality to BaseTest—things like screenshots, waits, configuration, browser handling, reporting, and test-data handling.

The problem was that every child test class inherited everything from BaseTest, even when it didn't need those features. Also, when I changed something in the base class, it could unexpectedly affect multiple test classes.

For example, when I modified the @AfterMethod in BaseTest to capture screenshots for failures, some tests using a different driver lifecycle started facing issues because the teardown was being executed automatically.

How I fixed it

I reduced the responsibility of the base class and moved reusable functionality into separate utility/component classes.

For example:

BaseTest
   |
   +-- DriverManager
   +-- WaitUtils
   +-- ScreenshotUtils
   +-- ConfigReader
   +-- TestDataUtils

Instead of putting everything into inheritance, I used composition, where a class uses the required utility rather than automatically inheriting all functionality.

Strong closing statement

“So, the issue wasn't inheritance itself. The problem was overusing inheritance and creating a large BaseTest class. I learned that inheritance is useful for a genuine is-a relationship, but for reusable utilities like waits, screenshots, configuration, and test data, composition is usually more maintainable.”

If interviewer asks for the concept

You can say:

“Inheritance created tight coupling between my test classes and BaseTest. A small change in the parent class could impact many child classes. I solved this by keeping BaseTest focused on common test lifecycle responsibilities and using composition for independent utilities.

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	 How do you decide between interface and abstract class?

===> I decide between an interface and an abstract class based on whether I need to define a contract or share common implementation/state.

I use an interface when different classes need to follow the same behavior, but they may have completely different implementations

For example, in an automation framework, I could define:

public interface BrowserActions {
    void launchBrowser();
    void closeBrowser();
}

Different implementations can provide Chrome, Firefox, or other browser-specific behavior.

I use an abstract class when the classes have a strong common relationship and I want to provide some common implementation along with abstract methods.

For example:

public abstract class BasePage {

    protected WebDriver driver;

    public BasePage(WebDriver driver) {
        this.driver = driver;
    }

    public void click(By locator) {
        driver.findElement(locator).click();
    }

    public abstract boolean isPageLoaded();
}

Then:

public class LoginPage extends BasePage {

    public LoginPage(WebDriver driver) {
        super(driver);
    }

    @Override
    public boolean isPageLoaded() {
        return driver.findElement(By.id("login-button")).isDisplayed();
    }
}

Here, BasePage provides common Selenium functionality, while each page provides its own implementation of isPageLoaded().

Real-world framework example

“In my Selenium framework, if I want to say ‘any browser implementation must provide these operations’, I would prefer an interface.

If I want to say ‘all my Page Objects share common WebDriver operations, waits, and utilities’, I would use an abstract BasePage.

So, my simple rule is:

Interface = what the class can do.
Abstract class = what the class is and what common behavior it should inherit.”

That distinction is usually enough to handle the follow-up questions in an interview.

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	How does polymorphism help in framework design?

==>	Polymorphism helps me make the automation framework flexible, loosely coupled, and easier to maintain.

In my Selenium framework, a practical example is WebDriver. WebDriver is an interface, and I can use different browser implementations through the same reference:

WebDriver driver;

if (browser.equalsIgnoreCase("chrome")) {
    driver = new ChromeDriver();
} else if (browser.equalsIgnoreCase("firefox")) {
    driver = new FirefoxDriver();
}

After that, my test code doesn't need to know which browser implementation is being used:

driver.get("https://www.saucedemo.com");
driver.findElement(By.id("user-name")).sendKeys("standard_user");
driver.findElement(By.id("login-button")).click();

The same test code works with Chrome or Firefox because the same WebDriver reference can represent different implementations.

Another example: method overriding

Suppose I have:

public abstract class BasePage {
    public abstract boolean isPageLoaded();
}

My different Page Objects can implement it differently:

public class LoginPage extends BasePage {

    @Override
    public boolean isPageLoaded() {
        return driver.findElement(By.id("login-button")).isDisplayed();
    }
}
public class ProductsPage extends BasePage {

    @Override
    public boolean isPageLoaded() {
        return driver.findElement(By.className("title")).isDisplayed();
    }
}

The calling code can work with the BasePage reference:

BasePage page = new LoginPage(driver);
page.isPageLoaded();

The correct overridden implementation is executed at runtime.

How it helps my framework

I use polymorphism to:

Support multiple browsers without changing test logic.
Reduce tight coupling between test code and implementation.
Make components replaceable when implementation changes.
Improve maintainability and scalability.
Follow the Open/Closed Principle—I can add a new implementation with minimal changes to existing code.
Strong interview closing

“In short, polymorphism allows my framework to program against an abstraction rather than a concrete implementation. For example, my test works with the WebDriver interface, while the actual implementation can be ChromeDriver or FirefoxDriver. This makes the framework more flexible and maintainable.”

If the interviewer asks for the types:
“In Java, polymorphism is mainly compile-time polymorphism through method overloading and runtime polymorphism through method overriding. Selenium frameworks commonly benefit from runtime polymorphism

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	How do you achieve encapsulation in Page Object Model?

==> In my Selenium Page Object Model framework, I achieve encapsulation by keeping the page elements and their implementation details private inside the Page Object class, and exposing only the required business methods to the test layer.

public class LoginPage {

    private WebDriver driver;

    // Encapsulated locators
    private By username = By.id("user-name");
    private By password = By.id("password");
    private By loginButton = By.id("login-button");

    public LoginPage(WebDriver driver) {
        this.driver = driver;
    }

    // Public business method
    public void login(String user, String pass) {
        driver.findElement(username).sendKeys(user);
        driver.findElement(password).sendKeys(pass);
        driver.findElement(loginButton).click();
    }
}
My Cucumber step doesn't directly access the locators:

loginPage.login("standard_user", "secret_sauce");

The test layer doesn't need to know:

What locator is used for the username
How the password field is located
How the login button is clicked
What Selenium code is executed internally

Those details are encapsulated inside LoginPage.

Interview closing

“So, in POM, I achieve encapsulation by making locators and internal WebDriver operations private and exposing public methods representing business actions. This gives us data hiding, reduces duplication, and makes the framework easier to maintain.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q :  How do you deal with stale element exceptions in Selenium?
===> StaleElementReferenceException is a common exception that occurs in Selenium when an element on the web page becomes stale or no longer valid. This typically happens when the DOM (Document Object Model) of the page changes, such as after a page refresh or when an element is modified dynamically.

To handle this exception, you can refresh the page, refreshing the page before attempting to interact with the element again. This helps ensure that you are working with an updated version of the DOM or try to locate the element again using a different locator strategy.


#====================================================================================================
# Q : How can we move to the nth-child element using XPath?
We can move to the nth-child element using XPath by using the following expression:

1
(//parentElement/*)[n]
In the above XPath expression:
– `parentElement` represents the parent element that contains multiple child elements.
– `n` is the index number of the desired child element.

For instance, if you want to move to the 5th child element under a parent div, you would use `(//div/*)[5]`. This will select and navigate to the 5th child element under any div on the page.

# Q :	Explain how you would perform parallel test execution using Selenium WebDriver.
==> 
1.TestNG and JUnit are popular testing frameworks that support parallel execution.

2. JUnit 5 also supports parallel execution via the @Execution(Execution.Mode.CONCURRENT) annotation.

3. Selenium Grid provides a built-in solution for parallel test execution across various browsers and devices

4. Docker containers can be used to create isolated environments for running tests in parallel.

5. Cloud-based testing platforms like Sauce Labs, BrowserStack, or LambdaTest offer parallel execution capabilities. They provide infrastructure to run tests in parallel across various configurations (browsers, devices, operating systems) and environments.

# Q :	Is it possible to test the database using Selenium?
==> No, we cannot test the database using Selenium WebDriver as it is designed to test web-based applications. For comprehensive and thorough database testing, it’s recommended to use appropriate tools specifically designed for working with databases such as SQL clients (e.g., MySQL Workbench).

# Q :	What is the Page Object Model in Selenium?
The Page Object Model (POM) is a design pattern used in Selenium automation testing to enhance test code maintainability, reusability, and readability. It promotes the concept of creating separate classes for each web page or component of an application being tested.

In POM, each web page or component is represented by its own class file, known as a “Page Object.” These Page Objects encapsulate the elements, actions, and behaviors associated with that specific page or component. They provide a higher-level abstraction layer that makes it easier to interact with the application under test.

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	How would you optimize your Selenium scripts for speed and performance?
Optimizing Selenium scripts for speed and performance is key to decreasing execution time. To optimize Selenium scripts for speed and performance, you can consider the following strategies:

1.Use headless mode – Running tests in headless mode allows the execution of browser automation without the graphical user interface (GUI), which can significantly improve performance by reducing rendering time and resource consumption.

2. Optimize data handling – When dealing with large datasets or repetitive operations, optimize data handling techniques such as reading data from external files (e.g., CSV) instead of hardcoding it within the script itself.

3. Proper use of wait – Instead of using long static wait times, leverage implicit waits or dynamic waits that wait only until a specific condition is met (e.g., element visibility or presence). This helps reduce unnecessary waiting time during script execution.

4. Parallelize test execution – If your test suite contains multiple independent tests, consider running them in parallel across multiple threads or machines using tools like TestNG or JUnit’s parallel execution features. This approach can help save time by utilizing available resources efficiently.

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	 How do you handle security testing in Selenium?
1. Security testing plays a crucial role in testing an application. There are several techniques we can use for Security Testing in Selenium:

2. Cross-Site Scripting (XSS) – Cross-site scripting (XSS) is an attack in which an attacker injects malicious executable scripts into the code of a trusted application or website. Create test for XSS vulnerabilities by injecting script tags or malicious code into input fields and verifying if they get executed on the page.

3. Cross-Site Request Forgery (CSRF) – Cross-Site Request Forgery (CSRF) is an attack that forces an end user to execute unwanted actions on a web application in which they’re currently authenticated. Automate tests to verify if CSRF tokens are properly implemented and protect against unauthorized requests being made on behalf of a user.

4. Regularly update test data and configurations: Keep your test data up-to-date to reflect any changes made to how an application handles its anti-CSRF measures (e.g., updated field names, header formats).

5. Vulnerability scanning tools – Integrate vulnerability scanning tools like OWASP ZAP or Burp Suite into your Selenium tests to identify common security issues automatically.

6. Validate if sensitive information like session IDs or tokens are not exposed in URLs or stored insecurely.

7. We can make sure that the Selenium scripts are configured to work with SSL-secured websites by using the appropriate browser capabilities or options.

# Q :   Can we integrate Selenium Framework with CI/CD pipelines? If yes, then explain.

==> Selenium can be integrated with CI/CD pipelines. It involves a series of steps that can help automate the testing process, ensuring that your code is always in a deployable state.

We need to create Configuration file that will define the CI/CD Pipelines

For Jenkins, this could be a Jenkinsfile.
For GitLab CI, you use .gitlab-ci.yml.
For GitHub CI, you use .maven.yml. Yml file name is not fixed here.
For CircleCI, you use .circleci/config.yml.
For Travis CI, you use .travis.yml.
Selenium tests need to be headless to be able to run in any pipeline

# Q :	An element is present in the DOM, but Selenium says it is not clickable. How would you debug and fix it?

==> In my experience, just because an element is present in the DOM doesn't mean it is ready for interaction. I first identify whether the problem is due to timing, visibility, an overlay, iframe, stale element, or an incorrect locator.

My approach would be:

Verify the locator
1. I first check whether Selenium is identifying the correct element using findElement() and inspect it in browser DevTools.
2. Check visibility and state
	The element may be present but hidden or disabled. I check:
	isDisplayed()
	isEnabled()
	whether the element has the expected attributes.
3. Check for overlays or loaders
	A very common issue is a popup, spinner, cookie banner, or transparent overlay covering the element. In that case, I wait for the overlay to disappear before clicking.
4. Use an explicit wait
	I avoid Thread.sleep() and use an explicit wait, for example:
WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

WebElement element = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("loginButton")));

element.click();
5. Check if the element is inside an iframe :
	If the element belongs to an iframe, I switch to that frame first:
wait.until(ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt(By.id("paymentFrame")));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("payButton"))).click();

driver.switchTo().defaultContent();

6. Handle ElementClickInterceptedException
If another element is covering the target, I identify that element and wait for it to disappear rather than immediately using JavaScript click.
7. Check for stale elements
If the page dynamically refreshes or re-renders the element, the previously located WebElement can become stale. I locate the element again after the DOM update.
8. Use JavaScript click only as a last resort
If the application uses custom UI components where normal Selenium click doesn't work even though the element is genuinely ready, I may use JavaScript click, but I don't use it as the first solution because it can bypass real user interaction behavior.

WebElement element = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfElementLocated(By.id("submit")));

((JavascriptExecutor) driver).executeScript("arguments[0].click();", element);

So, my first choice is not simply to add a wait. I would identify why the element isn't clickable, fix the synchronization or application-state issue, and then perform the click."

Short interview version

"If an element is present in the DOM but not clickable, I first check whether it is visible, enabled, covered by an overlay, inside an iframe, or being re-rendered. I inspect the DOM and browser console, then use an explicit wait such as elementToBeClickable(). If an overlay is present, I wait for it to disappear. If it's inside an iframe, I switch to the frame. For stale elements, I locate the element again. I use JavaScript click only as a last resort. This approach helps me fix the root cause rather than masking the synchronization issue.

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	 The XPath of an element changes every time you refresh the page. How would you create a stable locator?

==> Yes, I have faced this type of situation with dynamic web applications. If the XPath changes after every refresh, I would first identify which part of the XPath is dynamic instead of directly hardcoding the complete XPath.

My approach would be:

Inspect the element attributes
I check whether there are stable attributes such as id, name, class, placeholder, aria-label, data-testid, or other custom attributes.
Avoid dynamically generated attributes
For example, if the ID changes like:
user_12345
user_67891
user_98765

I would not use the complete ID.

Instead, I can use:

By user = By.xpath("//input[starts-with(@id,'user_')]");

or:

By user = By.cssSelector("input[id^='user_']");
Use partial matching when appropriate

For example:

By element = By.xpath("//input[contains(@id,'user_')]");

But I prefer this only when the partial value is unique enough.

Use stable business attributes

If the application provides something like:

<input data-testid="username">

I would prefer:

By username = By.cssSelector("[data-testid='username']");

because it is usually more stable than a generated XPath.

Use relative XPath instead of absolute XPath

I avoid XPath like:

/html/body/div[2]/div[1]/div[3]/input

because any DOM structure change can break it.

Instead, I would use a relationship with a stable element:

By username = By.xpath("//label[text()='Username']/following::input[1]");
Use parent-child relationships

If the element itself doesn't have a stable attribute, but its parent does, I can locate it through the parent:

By email = By.xpath("//div[@data-testid='user-form']//input[@type='email']");
Ask developers for a test-friendly attribute if necessary

In a real project, if there is no reliable locator, I would discuss it with the developer and request a stable attribute such as data-testid.

Real-time example

Suppose I initially have:

By loginButton = By.id("button_12345");

After every refresh, the ID changes to:

button_67891
button_98231
button_45672

Instead of changing the automation code every time, I would inspect the element for another stable attribute.

For example:

<button id="button_67891"
        data-testid="login-button">
    Login
</button>

Then I would use:

By loginButton = By.cssSelector("[data-testid='login-button']");

This makes the locator independent of the dynamically generated ID.

So, my principle is: I don't try to make the changing XPath itself stable. I identify a stable attribute or relationship in the DOM and build the locator around that."

⭐ Short interview version

"If an XPath changes after every refresh, I first identify which attribute is dynamic. I avoid absolute XPath and dynamically generated IDs. I look for stable attributes like id, name, data-testid, aria-label, or placeholder. If necessary, I use XPath functions such as contains() or starts-with(), or create a relative XPath using a stable parent or sibling. In a real project, if no reliable locator exists, I would coordinate with the developer to add a stable data-testid attribute. My goal is to create a locator based on stable application behavior rather than the current DOM structure."

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	A button is visible but clicking it gives `ElementClickInterceptedException`. What would you do?
==> In this situation, the button may be visible, but another element is actually overlapping or intercepting the click. I would not immediately use JavaScript click. First, I would identify the root cause.

My approach would be:

1. Check the exception message and inspect the DOM
	I check which element Selenium says would receive the click. For example, it could be a popup, loader, cookie banner, tooltip, or another transparent element.

2. Check for overlays/loaders
	A common scenario is that a loader is still displayed over the button.
I would wait for the overlay to disappear:

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(15));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.invisibilityOfElementLocated( By.cssSelector(".loading-spinner")));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("submit"))).click();

3. Wait for the button to become clickable
	I would use an explicit wait rather than Thread.sleep():
WebElement submit = wait.until( ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("submit")));

submit.click();

4. Check whether the element is inside an iframe
If the button is inside an iframe, I switch to the appropriate frame before interacting with it:

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt(By.id("paymentFrame")));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("submit")
)).click();

driver.switchTo().defaultContent();

5. Scroll the element into view	
	Sometimes the element's position or viewport location causes interaction problems. I can scroll it into view:
WebElement button = wait.until(
    ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfElementLocated(By.id("submit")));

((JavascriptExecutor) driver).executeScript("arguments[0].scrollIntoView({block:'center'});", button);

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(button)).click();

6. Check for animation or dynamic UI changes
	If the page has an animation, modal transition, or sticky header, the element might be moving when Selenium attempts the click. I would wait for the relevant UI transition to complete.

7. JavaScript click as a last resort
	If the element is genuinely available but the application has a custom UI implementation where the normal Selenium click doesn't work, I may use JavaScript:
((JavascriptExecutor) driver).executeScript("arguments[0].click();", button);

But I would use this only as a last resort, because JavaScript click can bypass the normal browser interaction and may hide an actual UI problem.

Real-time example

"For example, in one scenario, the Submit button was visible, but clicking it resulted in ElementClickInterceptedException. When I inspected the page, I found that a loading overlay was still covering the button for a few seconds.

Instead of increasing the wait or adding Thread.sleep(), I waited for the loader to become invisible and then waited for the Submit button to become clickable. After that, the test became stable.

#-------------------------------------------#
⭐ Short interview version

"When I get ElementClickInterceptedException, I first check which element is intercepting the click. Usually, it is an overlay, loader, popup, animation, or sticky element. I inspect the DOM, wait for the overlay to disappear, and then use an explicit wait for elementToBeClickable(). I also verify iframe context and scrolling if required. I use JavaScript click only as a last resort. My focus is to fix the root cause rather than simply bypassing the Selenium click

#====================================================================================================
# Q :  An element takes 5–10 seconds to appear randomly. How would you handle this without using `Thread.sleep()`?
==> 1. In this situation, I would use an explicit wait instead of Thread.sleep(). The main advantage is that Selenium will continuously check for the expected condition and proceed as soon as the condition is satisfied, rather than waiting for a fixed amount of time.
WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(15));

WebElement element = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfElementLocated(By.id("submit")));
element.click();

2. If the element needs to be clickable, I would use:

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(15));
WebElement button = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("submit")));
button.click();

3. If the application has a loader
In a real project, if the element appears only after an API call or loader finishes, I would wait for the loader to disappear first.

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(20));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.invisibilityOfElementLocated(By.cssSelector(".loader")));

WebElement element = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("submit")));

element.click();

4. Why I don't use Thread.sleep(): Thread.sleep(10000);
	This always waits 10 seconds, even if the element appears after 2 seconds.

With explicit wait:

1. Element appears in 2 seconds → proceed immediately
2. Element appears in 8 seconds → proceed after 8 seconds
3. Element doesn't appear within 15 seconds → TimeoutException

So it makes the automation faster and more reliable.
Real-time scenario

"In my automation framework, I had scenarios where data was loaded dynamically after an API response, so the UI element didn't appear at a fixed time. Instead of using Thread.sleep(), I used explicit waits based on conditions such as visibility, presence, clickability, or loader invisibility. This helped reduce flaky failures, especially when tests were executed in slower environments such as Jenkins."

⭐ Short interview answer

"I would use an explicit wait instead of Thread.sleep(). I would wait for the specific condition required by the test—for example, visibilityOfElementLocated() if I only need the element to appear, or elementToBeClickable() if I need to click it. If the application has a loader or API call, I can also wait for the loader to disappear. Explicit waits are better because they poll until the condition is satisfied and don't unnecessarily wait for the full timeout."

#====================================================================================================
# Q : You have multiple elements with the same XPath. How would you identify and click the correct one?
==> "If multiple elements have the same XPath, I would not blindly use findElement() because Selenium will generally interact with the first matching element. I would first understand what makes the required element unique.
1. Use a more specific locator
	For example, suppose I have multiple Edit buttons:

<button>Edit</button>
<button>Edit</button>
<button>Edit</button>

Instead of:

By editButton = By.xpath("//button[text()='Edit']");

I would use the relationship with the required row:

By editButton = By.xpath("//tr[td[text()='Priya']]/td/button[text()='Edit']");

driver.findElement(editButton).click();

Here, I'm identifying the row based on the user's name and then finding the Edit button inside that row.
2. Use findElements() when multiple matches are expected
	If I intentionally expect multiple elements, I use:

List<WebElement> buttons = driver.findElements(By.xpath("//button[text()='Edit']"));

System.out.println("Number of buttons: " + buttons.size());

Then I can select the required element based on some business condition.

for (WebElement button : buttons) {
    if (button.isDisplayed() && button.isEnabled()) {
        button.click();
        break;
    }
}

But I would prefer a unique business-based locator instead of relying on index.

3. Use index only when the position is guaranteed

For example: driver.findElement(By.xpath("(//button[text()='Edit'])[2]")).click();

I can use this when the second element is consistently the required one.	
However, I avoid index-based XPath when the page structure is dynamic because adding/removing an element can change the index.

4. Use parent-child relationships

For example, if every product has its own container:

By addToCart = By.xpath(
    "//div[@class='product-card' and .//h3[text()='iPhone 15']]//button[text()='Add to Cart']"
);

driver.findElement(addToCart).click();

This is more reliable because I'm identifying the specific product first, then locating its button.

5. Use Java code to filter elements when necessary
List<WebElement> products = driver.findElements( By.cssSelector(".product-card"));

for (WebElement product : products) {
    String name = product.findElement(By.cssSelector(".product-name")).getText();

    if (name.equals("iPhone 15")) {
        product.findElement(By.cssSelector(".add-to-cart")).click();
        break;
    }
}

Real-time scenario

"For example, suppose an application displays 20 users in a table and every row has an Edit button. All Edit buttons have the same locator. If I need to edit a specific user, I don't use the second or third Edit button based on its index.

I first locate the row using a unique value such as username or employee ID and then locate the Edit button inside that row."

By editUser = By.xpath("//tr[td[text()='EMP001']]/td/button[text()='Edit']");

driver.findElement(editUser).click();

This makes the automation more maintainable if the order of rows changes.

#-----------------------------------------#
⭐ Short interview answer

"If multiple elements have the same XPath, I first try to make the locator unique using a business identifier or parent-child relationship. For example, if every user row has an Edit button, I locate the row using the username or employee ID and then find the Edit button inside that row. If multiple elements are intentionally expected, I use findElements() and filter them based on text, attributes, or visibility. I use XPath indexes only when the position is guaranteed to be stable. I generally prefer a unique, business-based locator over hardcoded indexes."

#====================================================================================================
# Q : The application has dynamically generated IDs such as `user_12345`, `user_67891`. How would you locate the element?
==> If the ID is dynamically generated, I would not use the complete ID because it can change every time the page loads. First, I would identify whether there is a stable part or another unique attribute available."

1. Use starts-with() in XPath
	If the IDs always follow the pattern user_<number>:

By user = By.xpath("//input[starts-with(@id,'user_')]");
driver.findElement(user).sendKeys("Priya");

2. Use contains() when appropriate
	If the stable portion occurs somewhere in the ID:

By user = By.xpath("//input[contains(@id,'user_')]");

However, I would make sure this returns only the intended element
3. Use CSS selectors
   I can also use CSS attribute selectors:

By user = By.cssSelector("input[id^='user_']");

Here:
^= means starts with
$= means ends with
*= means contains
By user = By.cssSelector("input[id^='user_']");

4. Prefer another stable attribute if available
	Before using partial ID matching, I would inspect the DOM for attributes such as:
<input id="user_67891" name="username" data-testid="username">
By user = By.cssSelector("[data-testid='username']");

because data-testid is more stable than the dynamically generated ID.

5. Use a relative XPath based on surrounding elements

If there is no stable attribute on the input itself, I can use a stable label or parent:

By username = By.xpath("//label[text()='Username']/following::input[1]");

#-----------------------------------------#
⭐ Short interview answer

"I would not hardcode a dynamically generated ID. First, I check whether there is another stable attribute such as name, data-testid, or aria-label. If the ID follows a predictable pattern like user_12345, I can use XPath starts-with() or CSS ^=. For example, By.xpath("//input[starts-with(@id,'user_')]") or By.cssSelector("input[id^='user_']"). If possible, I prefer a stable business or test-specific attribute because it makes the locator more maintainable."

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	Selenium finds the element, but `sendKeys()` doesn't enter the text. What could be the reason?
===> es, I have faced this type of issue. If Selenium finds the element but sendKeys() doesn't enter the text, I first check whether the element is actually interactable, not just present in the DOM.

I would debug it step by step
1. Check whether the element is visible and enabled
WebElement username = driver.findElement(By.id("username"));

System.out.println(username.isDisplayed());
System.out.println(username.isEnabled());

If the element is hidden or disabled, sendKeys() may not work as expected.

2. Wait until the element is visible

If the application loads the input dynamically, I use an explicit wait:

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(15));

WebElement username = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfElementLocated(By.id("username")));

username.clear();
username.sendKeys("Priya");

If the field needs to be interactable, I can wait for it to be clickable:
WebElement username = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("username")));

username.sendKeys("Priya");

3. Check whether the element is inside an iframe
	This is another common reason.
	If the input is inside an iframe, Selenium may need to switch to that frame first:
wait.until(ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt(By.id("loginFrame")));

driver.findElement(By.id("username")).sendKeys("Priya");

driver.switchTo().defaultContent();

4. Check for an overlay or popup
	Sometimes a loader, popup, cookie banner, or transparent overlay is covering the input.
I would inspect the page and wait for the overlay to disappear:

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.invisibilityOfElementLocated(By.cssSelector(".loader")));

Then perform sendKeys().

5. The element may have been re-rendered
	In modern applications such as React or Angular, the input can be replaced in the DOM after it is located.
That can cause a stale reference or make interaction unreliable.
Instead of keeping the WebElement for a long time, I prefer locating it immediately before interaction:
WebElement username = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfElementLocated(By.id("username")));

username.sendKeys("Priya");

6. Check whether it is actually an input element
	Sometimes what looks like a text box is actually a custom component.

<div class="custom-input">
    <input type="text">
</div>
