In my POM framework, I use explicit waits, reusable page methods, logging, and failure screenshots to identify the root cause and make the tests more reliable. I avoid using Thread.sleep() as a permanent solution because it can make tests slower and less stable."

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	Your test randomly fails with `TimeoutException`. How would you perform RCA?
===> 	n my automation framework, TimeoutException occurs when Selenium waits for a particular condition, but that condition is not satisfied within the specified timeout period.

When a test fails randomly with TimeoutException, I perform a Root Cause Analysis (RCA) by checking the logs, screenshots, browser state, application behavior, and synchronization points instead of simply increasing the timeout value.

1. Identify the exact failure point

First, I check the Cucumber report, TestNG report, and automation logs to identify the step where the timeout occurred.

For example, in my SauceDemo project, the failure might occur while clicking the Add to Cart button or waiting for the cart badge to update.

WebDriverWait wait =
        new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(
        By.id("add-to-cart-sauce-labs-backpack")
)).click();

If this code fails, I investigate why the button did not become clickable within 10 seconds.

2. Check the possible root causes
	I investigate the following areas:

Possible root cause : How I investigate it

1. Synchronization issue : Check whether the element is loaded dynamically
2. Slow application response : Check page load time and API/network delays
3. Incorrect locator : Validate the locator against the current DOM
4. Element not clickable : Check overlays, loaders, and other elements blocking interaction
5. Dynamic DOM changes : Check for re-rendering or stale element references
6. Browser performance : Review browser logs, CPU usage, and resource constraints
7. Parallel execution : Check whether tests share WebDriver instances or test data
8. Environment instability : Compare failures across QA, local, and CI environments

3. Analyze logs and screenshots
	In my framework, I use SLF4J logging and screenshots captured by Cucumber hooks when a scenario fails.
try {
    log.info("Waiting for Add to Cart button");

    wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(
            By.id("add-to-cart-sauce-labs-backpack")
    )).click();

    log.info("Add to Cart button clicked successfully");

} catch (TimeoutException e) {

    log.error("Timeout while clicking Add to Cart button");
    log.error("Current URL: {}", driver.getCurrentUrl());

    throw e;
}

I then review the screenshot, current URL, browser console logs if available, and the HTML DOM at the time of failure. This helps me determine whether the issue is caused by the application, the locator, or the synchronization strategy.

4. Fix synchronization issues using explicit waits
	I avoid using fixed delays such as Thread.sleep() as a permanent solution. Instead, I wait for the specific condition required by the next action.

For example, when verifying the cart badge after adding a product:
private final By cartBadge = By.className("shopping_cart_badge");

public void verifyCartBadge(String expectedCount) {

    log.info("Waiting for cart badge count: {}", expectedCount);

    WebDriverWait wait =
            new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

    wait.until(ExpectedConditions.textToBe(
            cartBadge, expectedCount
    ));

    log.info("Cart badge count updated successfully");
}

This waits for the expected cart count rather than merely waiting for the badge to appear.

5. Check parallel execution and test isolation
	Since I use Cucumber with TestNG, I also verify whether failures occur only during parallel execution.

I check whether: 
1. Each scenario gets its own WebDriver instance.
2. ThreadLocal<WebDriver> is implemented correctly.
3. Test data is independent between scenarios.
4. Browser sessions are properly closed in the @After hook.
5. Static variables or shared page objects are not causing interference.
	

If the test passes consistently when run individually but fails during parallel execution, I investigate thread safety and shared resources.

6. Avoid blindly increasing the timeout
	Increasing the timeout can help if the application legitimately needs more time, but it does not fix incorrect locators, blocked elements, or synchronization defects.

I adjust the timeout only after examining the actual application response time and identifying the relevant condition.

7. Verify the fix
	After identifying and correcting the root cause, I rerun the failed scenario multiple times, execute the related regression suite, and test under the same conditions that originally produced the failure.

I compare the results between local and CI execution to ensure that the fix addresses the underlying problem rather than temporarily hiding it.

Real-time project example

Interviewer: Can you give an example from your SauceDemo project?

My answer:

"In my SauceDemo Selenium framework, I encountered intermittent TimeoutException failures while verifying the cart badge after adding products.

Initially, the test expected the badge to update immediately after clicking Add to Cart. During investigation, I checked the failure logs, screenshots, and DOM state. I identified that the test was checking the badge before the expected count was available.

I changed the implementation to use an explicit wait for the expected badge text, rather than checking it immediately. I also added logging to capture the expected count and current URL when the operation failed.

After the change, I reran the scenario repeatedly and as part of the regression suite to verify that the synchronization issue was resolved."

A strong closing statement for the interview:

"My approach to intermittent TimeoutException failures is to identify the exact condition that timed out, collect evidence through logs and screenshots, isolate the root cause, apply the appropriate fix, and validate it through repeated execution. I focus on fixing the underlying synchronization or environment issue rather than blindly increasing timeouts or adding retries."

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	A test passes 9 out of 10 times and fails randomly once. How would you identify the problem?

==> In my automation framework, I consider this an intermittent or flaky test. It means the same test produces different results under seemingly identical conditions.

I don't immediately modify the test or increase the timeout. First, I perform Root Cause Analysis (RCA) to identify whether the issue comes from synchronization, application behavior, test data, browser execution, or framework implementation.

1. Identify the exact failure point
	First, I check the Cucumber HTML report, TestNG report, automation logs, and screenshots to determine the exact step where the test fails.

For example, in my SauceDemo project, the test might fail while adding products to the cart or verifying the cart badge count.
You get `WebDriverException` only in Jenkins but not locally. What would you check?
I compare the successful execution with the failed execution to identify what changed.

2. Investigate the possible root causes

Possible cause : How I investigate
1. Synchronization issue : Check whether elements or dynamic content load at different speeds
2. Dynamic DOM : Look for StaleElementReferenceException or re-rendering
3. Test data dependency : Check whether previous tests modified shared data
4. Browser or environment : Compare local and CI execution, browser versions, and machine load
5. Parallel execution : Verify WebDriver isolation and thread safety
6. Application instability : Check network delays, server errors, and API responses
7. Locator instability : Check whether XPath or CSS selectors identify the correct element consistently
8. Test dependency : Run the scenario independently instead of relying on previous tests

3. Add logging and screenshots
	In my Selenium Java framework, I use SLF4J logging and Cucumber hooks to capture screenshots when a scenario fails.
try {
    log.info("Starting Add to Cart operation");

    WebDriverWait wait =
            new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

    wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(
            By.id("add-to-cart-sauce-labs-backpack")
    )).click();

    log.info("Add to Cart operation completed");

} catch (TimeoutException | StaleElementReferenceException e) {

    log.error("Add to Cart operation failed: {}", e.getMessage());
    log.error("Current URL: {}", driver.getCurrentUrl());

    throw e;
}

I also capture the screenshot, browser console logs when available, and the current DOM state to understand why the operation failed.

4. Reproduce the failure consistently
	I run the test multiple times under controlled conditions.

For example, if the test fails once in ten executions, I execute it repeatedly and compare the failure logs. I also run it independently and then as part of the complete regression suite.

I use TestNG's invocation feature for controlled repeated execution:
@Test(invocationCount = 10, threadPoolSize = 1)
public void verifyAddToCart() {

    log.info("Executing Add to Cart test");

    inventoryPage.addProductToCart();

    Assert.assertEquals(
            inventoryPage.getCartBadgeCount(),
            "1",
            "Cart badge count mismatch"
    );
}

Here, threadPoolSize = 1 keeps these invocations sequential. However, the test must reset its browser state and test data appropriately between invocations.

5. Fix the root cause
	Suppose my SauceDemo cart test fails intermittently because the badge count is checked before the UI updates.

Instead of checking the value immediately, I wait for the expected condition.
public void verifyCartBadge(String expectedCount) {

    By cartBadge = By.className("shopping_cart_badge");

    WebDriverWait wait =
            new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

    log.info("Waiting for cart badge count: {}", expectedCount);

    wait.until(
            ExpectedConditions.textToBe(cartBadge, expectedCount)
    );

    log.info("Cart badge count verified successfully");
}
If the issue is related to test data, I make the test independent. If it occurs only during parallel execution, I investigate ThreadLocal<WebDriver>, shared objects, and static variables.

I use retries only as a diagnostic or recovery mechanism when justified, not to hide the underlying failure.

6. Validate the fix
	After identifying the cause, I:

1. Rerun the failing test repeatedly.
2. Execute it independently and within the regression suite.
3. Verify that the test passes in the original failure environment.
4. Check that the fix does not introduce additional failures.
5. Monitor the results in CI execution

Real-time project example — SauceDemo

Interviewer: Can you explain a practical example?

My answer:

"In my SauceDemo Selenium Java framework, I had a scenario where adding products to the cart passed most of the time but occasionally failed during cart badge verification.

I started by reviewing the Cucumber report, logs, and failure screenshots. I compared successful and failed executions and found that the test was checking the badge count before the UI had updated.

I replaced the immediate assertion with an explicit wait for the expected badge text and added logging around the operation. I then reran the scenario repeatedly, both individually and within the regression suite.

I also checked browser and test-data isolation to ensure the issue wasn't caused by parallel execution or leftover state. This helped me address the underlying synchronization issue rather than simply retrying the test."

Strong closing statement for the interview:

"With 3.5 years of automation experience, I treat intermittent failures as a test reliability problem that requires evidence-based investigation. I reproduce the issue, compare successful and failed executions, isolate the root cause, implement a targeted fix, and validate it through repeated execution. My goal is to make the test consistently reliable, not just make it pass once."

#====================================================================================================
# Q : You get `WebDriverException` only in Jenkins but not locally. What would you check?
==> In my automation framework, if I get a WebDriverException in Jenkins but the same test passes locally, I suspect an environment-specific issue rather than immediately assuming there is a problem with the test script.

WebDriverException is a general Selenium exception that can occur due to browser startup failures, driver incompatibility, headless execution issues, resource constraints, session failures, or infrastructure problems.

I follow a systematic Root Cause Analysis (RCA) approach.
1. Check browser and WebDriver versions
	First, I compare the browser and WebDriver versions between my local machine and the Jenkins agent.

For example, my local machine might use Chrome 154, while the Jenkins agent has a different Chrome version or an outdated browser installation.

I verify:
1. Chrome or other browser version.
2. Selenium version in pom.xml.
3. ChromeDriver or WebDriverManager configuration.
4. Java and Maven versions.
5. Whether the Jenkins agent has permission to execute the browser driver.

WebDriverManager.chromedriver().setup();

ChromeOptions options = new ChromeOptions();

options.addArguments("--headless=new");
options.addArguments("--window-size=1920,1080");

WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver(options);

I use headless mode when required by the Jenkins environment, rather than assuming that the local browser configuration will work identically in CI.

2. Check headless browser configuration
	Jenkins commonly runs as a background service without an interactive desktop session.

If my test works locally but fails in Jenkins, I check whether the browser requires headless execution or additional Linux dependencies.

For Linux-based agents, I may need configuration such as:

ChromeOptions options = new ChromeOptions();

options.addArguments("--headless=new");
options.addArguments("--window-size=1920,1080");
options.addArguments("--disable-dev-shm-usage");

WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver(options);

If the agent runs inside a container, I also investigate shared memory limits and required browser libraries.

I use --no-sandbox only when necessary for the container environment and after reviewing the security implications.

3. Check Jenkins environment variables and configuration

In my framework, I use configuration files and system properties for browser selection and environment URLs.

I verify that Jenkins provides the expected values for:
1. Java installation and JAVA_HOME.
2. Maven installation.
3. Browser configuration.
4. Application base URL.
5. Environment-specific properties.
6. Download and screenshot directories.

For example, if my application URL is missing or incorrect in Jenkins, the test may navigate to an unexpected page and fail during subsequent WebDriver operations.

4. Check permissions and file paths
	My SauceDemo framework generates logs, screenshots, reports, and PDF downloads.

I verify that Jenkins has permission to create and modify these files.

Path downloadPath = Paths.get(System.getProperty("user.dir"), "downloads", "pdf");

Files.createDirectories(downloadPath);

ChromeOptions options = new ChromeOptions();

Map<String, Object> preferences = new HashMap<>();

preferences.put("download.default_directory", downloadPath.toAbsolutePath().toString());

preferences.put("download.prompt_for_download", false);

options.setExperimentalOption("prefs", preferences);

I also check for hardcoded Windows paths, because Jenkins might run on Linux.

5. Check resource constraints and synchronization
	Jenkins agents may have limited memory, CPU, or network resources, especially when several builds execute simultaneously.

I investigate:
1. Browser startup delays.
2. High CPU or memory utilization.
3. Network connectivity to the application.
4. Application response time.
5. Element loading and synchronization.
6. Browser crashes or session termination.

I use explicit waits for application conditions rather than relying on fixed delays.

6. Check parallel execution and WebDriver lifecycle
	Since my framework uses Selenium, Cucumber, and TestNG, I verify that each scenario receives its own WebDriver instance when tests run in parallel.

For example, I use ThreadLocal<WebDriver> in my DriverManager to prevent different test threads from accidentally sharing the same browser session.
public class DriverManager {

    private static final ThreadLocal<WebDriver> driver =
            new ThreadLocal<>();

    public static void setDriver(WebDriver webDriver) {
        driver.set(webDriver);
    }

    public static WebDriver getDriver() {
        return driver.get();
    }

    public static void quitDriver() {
        WebDriver webDriver = driver.get();

        if (webDriver != null) {
            webDriver.quit();
            driver.remove();
        }
    }
}
I also verify that the Cucumber @Before hook initializes the driver correctly and the @After hook closes it reliably.

7. Capture diagnostic information in Jenkins
	I add logs around browser initialization and test execution to identify exactly where the failure occurs.
try {
    log.info("Starting browser initialization");

    WebDriverManager.chromedriver().setup();

    ChromeOptions options = new ChromeOptions();
    options.addArguments("--headless=new");
    options.addArguments("--window-size=1920,1080");

    WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver(options);

    log.info("Browser initialized successfully");

} catch (WebDriverException e) {

    log.error("WebDriver initialization failed: {}",
            e.getMessage(), e);

    throw e;
}
I also preserve the Jenkins console output, browser and driver logs, screenshots where possible, and test reports as build artifacts.

Practical example from my SauceDemo project

Interviewer: Can you explain a real-world scenario?

My answer:

"In my Selenium Java automation framework for SauceDemo, suppose the tests pass locally but fail in Jenkins with a WebDriverException during browser initialization.

First, I check the Jenkins console output to determine whether the failure happens while creating the WebDriver session or during test execution.

If it occurs during initialization, I compare Java, Selenium, browser, and driver versions and check whether the Jenkins agent has the required browser dependencies and permissions.

If the browser starts successfully but the test fails later, I investigate the application URL, headless browser behavior, file paths, resource utilization, and synchronization.

I also verify my DriverFactory, DriverManager, and Cucumber hooks to ensure that each scenario gets a valid WebDriver instance and that sessions are closed correctly.

Once I identify the root cause, I fix the relevant configuration or framework code and rerun the same Jenkins job to verify that the issue is resolved."

Interview closing statement

"My approach is to isolate whether the WebDriverException originates from browser initialization, the Jenkins environment, or test execution. I compare the local and CI configurations, inspect the complete stack trace, collect diagnostic logs, and apply a targeted fix. I avoid blindly adding retries because retries can hide infrastructure or framework problems without resolving their root cause."

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	ChromeDriver and Chrome versions don't match. How would you resolve the issue?
==> In my Selenium automation framework, a ChromeDriver and Chrome version mismatch can cause errors such as SessionNotCreatedException or WebDriverException when Selenium tries to launch the browser.

I resolve this by checking the installed Chrome version, ensuring a compatible ChromeDriver is used, and making the configuration consistent across local and Jenkins environments.

1. Check the Chrome and ChromeDriver versions
	First, I check the installed Chrome version by opening:

chrome://settings/help

I can also check the ChromeDriver version from the command line:
chromedriver --version

Then I compare the versions and review the error message to identify the mismatch.

2. Use WebDriverManager in my framework
	In my SauceDemo Selenium Java framework, I use WebDriverManager to manage the ChromeDriver binary automatically.
WebDriverManager.chromedriver().setup();

ChromeOptions options = new ChromeOptions();

WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver(options);

WebDriverManager helps resolve and configure a compatible driver based on the installed browser. I also verify that the correct browser and driver are being selected in the execution environment.

3. Configure ChromeDriver in DriverFactory
	In my POM framework, I centralize browser initialization inside DriverFactory rather than creating drivers separately in every page class.
public class DriverFactory {

    public static WebDriver createDriver() {

        String browser = System.getProperty("browser", "chrome");

        WebDriver driver;

        switch (browser.toLowerCase()) {

            case "chrome":
                WebDriverManager.chromedriver().setup();

                ChromeOptions options = new ChromeOptions();
                options.addArguments("--start-maximized");

                driver = new ChromeDriver(options);
                break;

            case "firefox":
                WebDriverManager.firefoxdriver().setup();
                driver = new FirefoxDriver();
                break;

            case "edge":
                WebDriverManager.edgedriver().setup();
                driver = new EdgeDriver();
                break;

            default:
                throw new IllegalArgumentException(
                        "Unsupported browser: " + browser);
        }

        return driver;
    }
}
his keeps browser configuration centralized and easier to maintain.

4. Resolve the issue in Jenkins

If the test passes locally but fails in Jenkins, I check whether Jenkins has a different Chrome version installed.

My troubleshooting steps are:
1. Verify the Chrome version installed on the Jenkins agent.
2. Check the ChromeDriver version selected during the build.
3. Confirm that the agent can access the required driver download location.
4. Remove any conflicting hardcoded ChromeDriver paths.
5. Ensure that the Jenkins job uses the intended Java and Maven configuration.
6. Rerun the failed job and review the startup logs.

I also check whether Jenkins is using a cached or outdated ChromeDriver binary.

5. Alternative: Use Selenium Manager
	With modern Selenium 4 releases, Selenium Manager can automatically manage browser drivers in supported configurations.

For example:

ChromeOptions options = new ChromeOptions();

WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver(options);

When no explicit driver binary is configured, Selenium Manager can discover or obtain a compatible driver, subject to network access and environment configuration.

I generally choose one driver-management approach and avoid conflicting manual paths or configurations.

6. Verify the fix
After resolving the mismatch, I:
1. Start the browser and confirm the WebDriver session is created.
2. Execute a simple navigation test.
3. Run the SauceDemo login scenario.
4. Execute the relevant regression suite.
5. Verify the same configuration in Jenkins

Interviewer: Can you explain how you would handle this in your project?

My answer:

"In my SauceDemo Selenium Java framework, if I encounter a SessionNotCreatedException because ChromeDriver and Chrome versions do not match, I first inspect the complete exception message and check both versions.

If the framework uses an outdated hardcoded driver, I update it to a compatible version or configure WebDriverManager or Selenium Manager to resolve the driver automatically.

If the issue occurs only in Jenkins, I verify the browser version and driver configuration on the Jenkins agent rather than changing my local setup unnecessarily.

Since my framework uses a centralized DriverFactory, I make the correction there, rerun the login scenario, and then execute regression tests to ensure browser initialization works consistently."

Interview summary

Key points to remember:

1. Identify the installed Chrome version and actual ChromeDriver version.
2. Use a compatible driver for the installed browser.
3. Prefer WebDriverManager or Selenium Manager over manual driver maintenance when appropriate.
4. Centralize driver initialization in DriverFactory.
5. Check Jenkins configuration, cached binaries, and network access.
6. Validate the fix locally and in CI.

Strong closing statement: "I resolve browser-driver compatibility issues at the driver configuration level, centralize the solution in my framework, and verify it in both local and CI environments instead of applying a temporary workaround to individual test cases."

#==========================================================================================================================================
# Frames & Alerts
#==========================================================================================================================================

# Q : A button is inside an iframe. Your XPath is correct, but Selenium cannot find it. What is wrong?
===> The most likely reason is that the button is inside an iframe, and Selenium is still focused on the parent document.

Even if my XPath is correct, Selenium cannot directly locate elements inside an iframe from the main page DOM. I must first switch to the iframe using driver.switchTo().frame() and then locate the button
1. Example: Button inside an iframe

Suppose the HTML structure looks like this:

<html>
  <body>
    <iframe id="paymentFrame">
      <html>
        <body>
          <button id="submitButton">Submit</button>
        </body>
      </html>
    </iframe>
  </body>
</html>

If I try to click the button directly, Selenium may throw a NoSuchElementException.

import java.time.Duration;

import org.openqa.selenium.By;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebElement;
import org.openqa.selenium.chrome.ChromeDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.ExpectedConditions;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.WebDriverWait;

public class IframeExample {

    public static void main(String[] args) {

        WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver();

        WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(
                driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

        try {
            driver.get("https://example.com");

            // Switch to the iframe
            wait.until(ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt(By.id("paymentFrame")) );

            // Locate and click the button inside the iframe
            WebElement submitButton = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable( By.xpath("//button[@id='submitButton']")));

            submitButton.click();

            // Switch back to the main document
            driver.switchTo().defaultContent();

        } finally {
            driver.quit();
        }
    }
}

Note: The URL and locators are illustrative; the iframe selector must match the actual application.

2. Different ways to switch to an iframe
	Selenium supports three common approaches.
1. Using an ID or name: driver.switchTo().frame("paymentFrame");
2. Using an index: driver.switchTo().frame(0);
3. Using a WebElement: 
	WebElement frame = driver.findElement(By.cssSelector("iframe.payment-frame"));

	driver.switchTo().frame(frame);
I generally prefer switching by ID, name, or WebElement because iframe indexes can change when the page structure changes.

3. What if the button is still not found?
	In my Selenium framework, I troubleshoot the following possibilities:

Possible issue : How I troubleshoot it

1. Selenium is in the parent document 
==> Switch to the correct iframe first.

2. The iframe loads asynchronously 
==> Use frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt() with an explicit wait.

3. The button is inside a nested iframe 
==> Switch to the parent iframe, then switch to the nested iframe.
	
4. The iframe locator is incorrect
==> Inspect the iframe's id, name, or other attributes.

5. The element is dynamically rendered
==> Wait for the element to be present or visible.

6. The iframe has been refreshed or replaced
==> Locate the current iframe again instead of using a stale WebElement.

4. How to handle nested iframes
	If a button is inside a nested iframe, I switch through each iframe in sequence.

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait( driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt(By.id("parentFrame")));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt( By.id("childFrame")));
    
wait.until( ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.xpath("//button[@id='submitButton']"))).click();
   
// Return to the main page
driver.switchTo().defaultContent();

Remember that parentFrame() moves one level up, whereas defaultContent() returns directly to the main document.

5. How I handle this in my TestNG + Cucumber framework
	In my Selenium Java POM framework, I would keep iframe switching logic in a reusable utility or page object method, use explicit waits, and switch back to the main document after completing the operation.
This helps prevent iframe context issues from affecting subsequent steps or scenarios.

Short interview answer

"The XPath may be correct, but Selenium searches within the currently selected browsing context. If the button is inside an iframe, Selenium cannot locate it from the parent document. I first switch to the iframe using driver.switchTo().frame() or frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt(), locate and interact with the button, and then return to the main document using driver.switchTo().defaultContent(). If the issue persists, I check for nested iframes, dynamic loading, and incorrect iframe identification."

#====================================================================================================
# Q :  A page contains nested iframes. How would you interact with an element inside the innermost iframe?
===> In Selenium WebDriver, when a page contains nested iframes, I switch to each iframe from the outermost iframe to the innermost iframe, one level at a time. Once I reach the innermost iframe, I locate and interact with the required element.

Selenium cannot directly access an element inside a nested iframe from the main document because each iframe has its own browsing context
<html>
  <body>
    <iframe id="outerFrame">
      <iframe id="middleFrame">
        <iframe id="innerFrame">
          <button id="submitButton">Submit</button>
        </iframe>
      </iframe>
    </iframe>
  </body>
</html>

import java.time.Duration;

import org.openqa.selenium.By;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.chrome.ChromeDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.ExpectedConditions;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.WebDriverWait;

public class NestedIframeExample {

    public static void main(String[] args) {

        WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver();

        WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

        try {
            driver.get("https://example.com");

            // Step 1: Switch to the outermost iframe
            wait.until(ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt(By.id("outerFrame")));

            // Step 2: Switch to the middle iframe
            wait.until(ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt(By.id("middleFrame")));

            // Step 3: Switch to the innermost iframe
            wait.until(ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt(By.id("innerFrame")));

            // Step 4: Locate and click the button
            wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("submitButton"))).click();

            System.out.println("Button clicked successfully");

            // Step 5: Return directly to the main document
            driver.switchTo().defaultContent();

        } finally {
            driver.quit();
        }
    }
}

Note: The URL and iframe locators are illustrative; use the actual iframe hierarchy from the application under test.

3. Important iframe switching methods

Method : Purpose
1. driver.switchTo().frame("outerFrame"): Switch to an iframe using its name or ID
2. driver.switchTo().frame(index)       : Switch using a zero-based iframe index
3. driver.switchTo().frame(webElement)  : Switch using a located iframe element
4. driver.switchTo().parentFrame()      : Move one level up in the iframe hierarchy
5. driver.switchTo().defaultContent()   : Return directly to the main document

4. What if I need to return to the parent iframe?
	Suppose I have clicked the button in the innermost iframe and want to interact with another element in the middle iframe.
// Currently inside innerFrame

driver.switchTo().parentFrame();

// Now inside middleFrame
driver.findElement(By.id("middleFrameButton")).click();

// Return to the main page
driver.switchTo().defaultContent();

parentFrame() moves up exactly one level, whereas defaultContent() exits all iframe levels.

5. How I troubleshoot nested iframe issues in a real-time framework

In my Selenium Java, TestNG, and Cucumber framework, I follow these checks:
1. Verify the iframe hierarchy using browser developer tools.
2. Switch through the frames in the correct order.
3. Use WebDriverWait and frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt() for dynamically loaded frames.
4. Check whether an iframe is replaced or reloaded during execution.
5. Return to the appropriate frame context after interacting with the element.
6. Keep reusable frame-switching methods in a utility class or page object.

Short interview answer

"In my Selenium automation framework, I handle nested iframes by switching sequentially from the outermost iframe to the innermost iframe using driver.switchTo().frame(). I use explicit waits to ensure each frame is available, interact with the target element, and then use parentFrame() to move up one level or defaultContent() to return to the main page. This ensures Selenium operates in the correct browsing context and avoids NoSuchElementException issues."

#====================================================================================================
# Q :	How would you switch back from an iframe to the main page?
==> In Selenium WebDriver, I use driver.switchTo().defaultContent() to switch from an iframe back to the main page.

There are two commonly used methods, depending on the requirement:

defaultContent() – switches directly to the main document, regardless of the iframe nesting level.

parentFrame() – switches to the immediate parent frame, one level up.

1. Example: Switch from iframe to the main page
import java.time.Duration;

import org.openqa.selenium.By;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.chrome.ChromeDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.ExpectedConditions;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.WebDriverWait;

public class IframeSwitchExample {

    public static void main(String[] args) {

        WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver();

        WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(
                driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

        try {
            driver.get("https://example.com");

            // Switch to iframe
            wait.until(ExpectedConditions.frameToBeAvailableAndSwitchToIt(By.id("paymentFrame")));

            // Perform action inside iframe
            wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.id("submitButton")).click();           

            // Switch back to the main document
            driver.switchTo().defaultContent();

            // Interact with an element on the main page
            wait.until( ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable( By.id("continueButton")).click();

        } finally {
            driver.quit();
        }
    }
}
Note: The URL and locators are illustrative examples.

2. Difference between parentFrame() and defaultContent()
Method : Explanation
1. driver.switchTo().parentFrame() : Moves one level up in the iframe hierarchy.
2. driver.switchTo().defaultContent() : Returns directly to the main HTML document.

For example, if the hierarchy is:  Main Page → Parent iframe → Child iframe

Calling parentFrame() from the child iframe takes me to the parent iframe.

Calling defaultContent() from the child iframe takes me directly to the main page.

3. How I use this in my Selenium TestNG + Cucumber framework

In my framework, after interacting with an element inside an iframe, I explicitly switch back to the main document before interacting with other page elements. This prevents context-related NoSuchElementException failures in subsequent steps.

Short interview answer

"I use driver.switchTo().defaultContent() to return from an iframe to the main page. If I need to move only one level up in nested iframes, I use driver.switchTo().parentFrame(). In my Selenium Java framework, I explicitly manage the frame context and use explicit waits to make iframe interactions reliable."


#====================================================================================================
# Q : An unexpected JavaScript alert appears during execution and causes the test to fail. How would you handle it?
===> In Selenium WebDriver, if an unexpected JavaScript alert appears during test execution, it can interrupt browser interactions and cause exceptions such as UnexpectedAlertPresentException.

To handle this, I use Selenium's Alert interface along with driver.switchTo().alert(). I first check whether an alert is present, then handle it according to the test requirements.

There are three types of JavaScript dialogs:

Alert: Displays a message with an OK button.

Confirmation: Displays OK and Cancel buttons.

Prompt: Accepts user input.
1. Handle an unexpected alert using Selenium Java
import java.time.Duration;

import org.openqa.selenium.Alert;
import org.openqa.selenium.NoAlertPresentException;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.chrome.ChromeDriver;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.ExpectedConditions;
import org.openqa.selenium.support.ui.WebDriverWait;

public class UnexpectedAlertHandling {

    public static void main(String[] args) {

        WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver();

        WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(
                driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

        try {
            driver.get("https://example.com");

            // Perform test actions here

            try {
                Alert alert = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.alertIsPresent());

                String alertText = alert.getText();

                System.out.println("Unexpected alert detected: " + alertText);

                // Accept the alert if the test requires it
                alert.accept();

            } catch (org.openqa.selenium.TimeoutException e) {
                System.out.println("No alert appeared.");
            }

        } finally {
            driver.quit();
        }
    }
}
Important: I would not blindly accept every alert in a real-time framework. I would first determine whether the alert is expected or unexpected and handle it according to the test scenario.

2. Handle alerts using a reusable utility method

In my Selenium TestNG + Cucumber framework, I create a reusable utility method so that different page objects or step definitions can handle unexpected alerts consistently.
import org.openqa.selenium.Alert;
import org.openqa.selenium.NoAlertPresentException;
import org.openqa.selenium.WebDriver;

public class AlertUtils {

    public static boolean isAlertPresent(WebDriver driver) {
        try {
            driver.switchTo().alert();
            return true;
        } catch (NoAlertPresentException e) {
            return false;
        }
    }

    public static String acceptAlertIfPresent(WebDriver driver) {
        try {
            Alert alert = driver.switchTo().alert();

            String alertText = alert.getText();
            alert.accept();

            return alertText;

        } catch (NoAlertPresentException e) {
            return null;
        }
    }

    public static String dismissAlertIfPresent(WebDriver driver) {
        try {
            Alert alert = driver.switchTo().alert();

            String alertText = alert.getText();
            alert.dismiss();

            return alertText;

        } catch (NoAlertPresentException e) {
            return null;
        }
    }
}

Usage : 
String alertText = AlertUtils.acceptAlertIfPresent(DriverManager.getDriver());

if (alertText != null) {
    log.warn("Unexpected alert handled: {}", alertText);
}

Here, DriverManager.getDriver() represents the WebDriver accessor in my framework.

3. How I handle this in Cucumber hooks
	If an unexpected alert appears during scenario execution, I can handle it in a suitable hook or failure-handling utility, depending on when it occurs.
@After
public void afterScenario(Scenario scenario) {

    WebDriver driver = DriverManager.getDriver();

    if (driver != null) {
        try {
            String alertText = AlertUtils.acceptAlertIfPresent(driver);

            if (alertText != null) {
                log.warn("Alert found during scenario cleanup: {}", alertText);
            }

        } catch (Exception e) {
            log.error("Error while handling alert", e);
        }
    }
}
I would use this approach cautiously. An alert appearing during cleanup should not automatically be accepted if doing so could hide an application defect. Also, an @After hook is useful for cleanup, but it does not prevent an alert from interrupting an earlier step.

4. How I troubleshoot the root cause

Problem : My approach
1. Alert appears randomly
==> Check application logs, timing, and the action that triggered it.

2. UnexpectedAlertPresentException occurs
==> Handle the active alert before performing further browser operations.

3. Alert text contains an application error
==> Capture the text, log it, and fail the test when appropriate.

4. Alert is expected
==> Accept, dismiss, or enter text based on the scenario.

5. Alert appears only in Jenkins
==> Compare browser versions, execution timing, environment configuration, and application responses.

Short interview answer

"In my Selenium Java framework, I handle unexpected JavaScript alerts using driver.switchTo().alert(). I capture the alert text for logging and either accept or dismiss it based on the test requirements. I use explicit waits when an alert is expected and reusable alert utilities for common handling. If an unexpected alert indicates an application defect, I record the message and fail the test rather than blindly dismissing it. In my TestNG and Cucumber framework, I integrate this handling carefully with error logging and scenario cleanup."

#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you handle confirmation and prompt alerts?
==> In Selenium, I handle confirmation and prompt alerts using the Alert interface and driver.switchTo().alert().

The main difference is:

Confirmation alert → I can use accept() for OK or dismiss() for Cancel.
Prompt alert → I use sendKeys() to enter text, then accept() or dismiss().
1. Handling a Confirmation Alert
Alert alert = driver.switchTo().alert();

String message = alert.getText();
log.info("Confirmation alert message: {}", message);

// Click OK
alert.accept();

If I want to click Cancel:

Alert alert = driver.switchTo().alert();

log.info("Confirmation alert: {}", alert.getText());

alert.dismiss();

2. Handling a Prompt Alert

A prompt allows the user to enter some text.

Alert alert = driver.switchTo().alert();

log.info("Prompt message: {}", alert.getText());

alert.sendKeys("Automation Test");

alert.accept();

If I want to cancel the prompt:

Alert alert = driver.switchTo().alert();

alert.sendKeys("Automation Test");

alert.dismiss();
3. Using Explicit Wait

In my framework, I prefer an explicit wait when the alert may take some time to appear.

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

Alert alert = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.alertIsPresent());

log.info("Alert displayed: {}", alert.getText());

alert.accept();

For a prompt:

Alert alert = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.alertIsPresent());

log.info("Prompt displayed: {}", alert.getText());

alert.sendKeys("Test User");
alert.accept();

4. Reusable Alert Utility

In my Selenium TestNG + Cucumber framework, I would keep common alert operations in an AlertUtils class.
public class AlertUtils {

    public static String getAlertText(WebDriver driver) {

        Alert alert = driver.switchTo().alert();

        String text = alert.getText();

        log.info("Alert text: {}", text);

        return text;
    }

    public static void acceptAlert(WebDriver driver) {

        Alert alert = driver.switchTo().alert();

        log.info("Accepting alert");

        alert.accept();
    }

    public static void dismissAlert(WebDriver driver) {

        Alert alert = driver.switchTo().alert();

        log.info("Dismissing alert");

        alert.dismiss();
    }

    public static void enterTextAndAccept(
            WebDriver driver, String text) {

        Alert alert = driver.switchTo().alert();

        log.info("Entering text into prompt: {}", text);

        alert.sendKeys(text);
        alert.accept();
    }
}
Then my Cucumber step can remain simple:

@When("the user accepts the confirmation alert")
public void acceptConfirmationAlert() {

    AlertUtils.acceptAlert(
            DriverManager.getDriver());

    log.info("Confirmation alert accepted");
}
5. Important Interview Point

I don't use XPath or CSS selectors for JavaScript alerts because they are not HTML DOM elements.

Short interview answer

"For confirmation alerts, I use driver.switchTo().alert() and then accept() for OK or dismiss() for Cancel. For prompt alerts, I first use sendKeys() to enter the required value and then call accept() or dismiss(). In my Selenium TestNG and Cucumber framework, I normally put these operations into a reusable AlertUtils class and use WebDriverWait with ExpectedConditions.alertIsPresent() when the alert is asynchronous."

#====================================================================================================
# Q : What would you do if an alert appears randomly and not on every execution?
==> If an alert appears randomly and not on every execution, I would treat it as an intermittent synchronization or application-behavior issue. I would not blindly accept every alert, because that could hide a real application defect.

First, I would identify whether the alert is expected behavior or an unexpected application issue.

1. Wait for the alert conditionally
	I can use ExpectedConditions.alertIsPresent() with a short, controlled timeout.

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait( driver, Duration.ofSeconds(3));

try {
    Alert alert = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.alertIsPresent());

    String alertText = alert.getText();

    log.warn("Unexpected alert appeared: {}", alertText);

    alert.accept();

} catch (TimeoutException e) {

    log.info("No alert appeared. Continuing test execution.");
}

The important point is that TimeoutException here means no alert appeared within the expected short window, so the test can continue.

2. Create a reusable utility
	In my Selenium TestNG + Cucumber framework, I would put this logic into AlertUtils rather than duplicating it across step definitions.
public static boolean acceptAlertIfPresent( WebDriver driver) {

    try {

        WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait( driver, Duration.ofSeconds(3));

        Alert alert = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.alertIsPresent());

        String alertText = alert.getText();

        log.warn("Alert detected: {}", alertText);

        alert.accept();

        log.info("Alert accepted successfully");

        return true;

    } catch (TimeoutException e) {

        log.info("No alert appeared.");

        return false;
    }
}
Then my step or page method can simply call:

boolean alertHandled = AlertUtils.acceptAlertIfPresent(DriverManager.getDriver());

if (alertHandled) {
    log.info("Random alert was handled.");
}

3. I would investigate why it is random
	If the alert is appearing only occasionally, I would perform RCA rather than just suppressing it.
I would check:

1. Application behavior — Is the alert actually expected under certain conditions?
2. Synchronization — Is an asynchronous operation sometimes triggering it?
3. Network/API response — Does a backend response occasionally cause the alert?
4. Test data — Does particular data trigger the alert?
5. Browser differences — Does it happen only in Chrome, Firefox, or headless mode?
6. Environment — Does it occur only in Jenkins/QA but not locally?
7. Timing — Does it occur after a specific click or page transition?
8. Frequency — Run the test multiple times and record when the alert occurs.
9. Alert text — Log the exact message so I can identify the underlying condition.

4. I would capture evidence
	For an intermittent issue, I would log:
log.warn("Alert text: {}", alertText);
log.info("Current URL: {}", driver.getCurrentUrl());
log.info("Page title: {}", driver.getTitle());

If the alert indicates an application error, I would also capture a screenshot and relevant browser/framework logs.

Important point: I would not put this blindly in an @After hook and always call accept().

For example, if the application unexpectedly shows: "Payment failed"
and my framework automatically accepts it, the test might continue and falsely appear successful.

Instead, I would distinguish between:

Expected intermittent alert: Alert appears → capture message → accept → continue

Unexpected error alert: Alert appears → capture message → screenshot/logs → fail test

Short interview answer

"If an alert appears randomly, I use WebDriverWait with ExpectedConditions.alertIsPresent() inside a controlled try-catch. If the alert appears, I capture and log its text and handle it according to the expected behavior. If it doesn't appear, I continue execution without failing the test. At the same time, I investigate the intermittent behavior by checking synchronization, test data, network responses, browser/environment differences, and execution logs. I would never blindly accept every unexpected alert because that could hide an application defect."

#===========================================================================================================================================================
#  Dynamic Web Applications
#===========================================================================================================================================================
# Q : A table contains 100 rows, and you need to find a particular user and click Edit. How would you implement it?
===> If a table contains 100 rows and I need to find a particular user and click the corresponding Edit button, I would avoid hardcoding the row number.

I would identify the row dynamically based on a unique user attribute, such as username or email, and then locate the Edit button within that same row.

This is important because the user's position can change due to sorting, filtering, pagination, or new records being added.
Suppose the table looks like this:
<table id="userTable">
    <tr>
        <td>101</td>
        <td>John</td>
        <td>john@test.com</td>
        <td>
            <button>Edit</button>
        </td>
    </tr>

    <tr>
        <td>102</td>
        <td>David</td>
        <td>david@test.com</td>
        <td>
            <button>Edit</button>
        </td>
    </tr>
</table>
If I want to edit david@test.com, I can use a relative XPath.
String email = "david@test.com";

String editButtonXpath ="//table[@id='userTable']//tr[td[normalize-space()='"+ email + "']]//button[normalize-space()='Edit']";

WebElement editButton = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10)).until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(By.xpath(editButtonXpath)));

editButton.click();

The important part is:

Find the row containing the user
            ↓
Stay within that row
            ↓
Find its Edit button
            ↓
Click Edit
Better approach for a reusable framework

In my Selenium TestNG + Cucumber POM framework, I would create a reusable method in the page object.
public void clickEditForUser(String email) {

    log.info("Searching for user: {}", email);

    String rowXpath = "//table[@id='userTable']//tr[td[normalize-space()='"+ email+ "']]";

    WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

    WebElement row = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfElementLocated(By.xpath(rowXpath)));

    WebElement editButton = row.findElement(By.xpath(".//button[normalize-space()='Edit']"));

    wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(editButton)).click();

    log.info("Clicked Edit for user: {}", email);
}
I prefer this approach because once I locate the specific row, I search for the Edit button relative to that row using:

row.findElement(By.xpath(".//button[...]"));

The . is important because it makes the XPath relative to the row rather than searching the entire table.

Alternative: Using findElements()

If the table structure doesn't allow a clean relative XPath, I can retrieve all rows and iterate through them.

List<WebElement> rows = driver.findElements(By.cssSelector("#userTable tbody tr"));

for (WebElement row : rows) {

    List<WebElement> cells = row.findElements(By.tagName("td"));

    if (!cells.isEmpty()) {

        String userEmail = cells.get(2).getText().trim();

        if (userEmail.equalsIgnoreCase("david@test.com")) {

            log.info("User found: {}", userEmail);

            row.findElement(By.xpath(".//button[normalize-space()='Edit']"))
                    .click();

            break;
        }
    }
}
#---------------------------------------------------#
What if the table has pagination?
	This is a common interview follow-up.

If 100 records are distributed across multiple pages, I would handle pagination dynamically.

boolean userFound = false;

while (!userFound) {

    List<WebElement> rows = driver.findElements(By.cssSelector("#userTable tbody tr"));

    for (WebElement row : rows) {

        if (row.getText().contains("david@test.com")) {

            row.findElement(By.xpath(".//button[normalize-space()='Edit']"))
                    .click();

            userFound = true;
            break;
        }
    }

    if (!userFound) {

        List<WebElement> nextButtons = driver.findElements(By.id("next"));

        if (nextButtons.isEmpty() || !nextButtons.get(0).isEnabled()) {

            break;
        }

        nextButtons.get(0).click();
    }
}

Assert.assertTrue(userFound, "User was not found in the table");

In a production framework, I would improve this further by using an explicit wait after clicking Next and checking that the table content has changed before processing the next page.

Why I don't use a fixed row number
I would avoid something like:

driver.findElement(By.xpath("//table/tbody/tr[50]/td[4]/button")).click();

Because tomorrow the user could become row 30 or row 75 due to sorting or new records.
Instead, I use:

Unique user → Find matching row → Find Edit inside row
Short interview answer

"If I have 100 rows, I don't hardcode the row number. I identify the user using a unique attribute such as username or email, locate the corresponding <tr>, and then find the Edit button relative to that row. For example, I can use an XPath like //tr[td[normalize-space()='david@test.com']]//button[normalize-space()='Edit']. If the table is paginated, I iterate through the pages until I find the user, using explicit waits for pagination. In my POM framework, I would encapsulate this as a reusable clickEditForUser(String user) method."

#====================================================================================================
# Q : The table pagination contains 20 records per page, but your required record may be on any page. How would you automate it?
==> f a table has 20 records per page and my required record can be on any page, I would handle pagination dynamically. I would not assume which page contains the record.

My approach is:
1. Read all rows on the current page.
2. Search for the required user using a unique value such as username/email.
3. If found, click the Edit button within that row and stop.
4. If not found, check whether the Next button is available/enabled.
5. Click Next.
6. Wait for the next page's data to load.
7. Repeat until the record is found or there are no more pages.

Example : Suppose I need to find david@test.com.
public void editUser(String email) {

    WebDriverWait wait =  new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

    boolean userFound = false;

    while (true) {

        log.info("Searching for user: {}", email);

        List<WebElement> rows = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfAllElementsLocatedBy( By.cssSelector("#userTable tbody tr")));

        for (WebElement row : rows) {

            String rowText = row.getText();

            if (rowText.contains(email)) {

                log.info("User found: {}", email);

                WebElement editButton = row.findElement(By.xpath(".//button[normalize-space()='Edit']"));

                wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable( editButton)).click();

                userFound = true;
                break;
            }
        }

        if (userFound) {
            break;
        }

        // Check Next button
        WebElement nextButton = driver.findElement(By.cssSelector("button.next"));

        if (!nextButton.isEnabled()) {
            log.warn("Reached last page. User not found: {}", email);
            break;
        }

        // Store current page information
        String currentPage =  driver.findElement(By.cssSelector(".current-page")).getText();

        // Go to next page
        nextButton.click();

        // Wait until page changes
        wait.until(ExpectedConditions.not(ExpectedConditions.textToBe( By.cssSelector(".current-page"), currentPage)));
    }

    Assert.assertTrue(userFound, "User was not found in the paginated table: " + email);
}
The locators such as #userTable, .next, and .current-page are examples; I would use the application's actual DOM locators.

A more robust approach

Sometimes there is no reliable current-page element. In that case, I can wait for the first row or table content to change after clicking Next.

For example:
String firstRowBefore =
        rows.get(0).getText();

nextButton.click();

wait.until(driver -> {

    List<WebElement> updatedRows = driver.findElements( By.cssSelector("#userTable tbody tr"));

    return !updatedRows.isEmpty() && !updatedRows.get(0).getText().equals(firstRowBefore);
});
This is useful when the application updates the table asynchronously.

Important point: Find the Edit button inside the row
I would not do this:
driver.findElement(By.xpath("//button[text()='Edit']")).click();

Because there may be 20 Edit buttons on the page.
Instead:

row.findElement(By.xpath(".//button[normalize-space()='Edit']")).click();

The . makes the XPath relative to the specific row containing the required user.

Handling a disabled Next button
	Some applications don't disable the button; instead, they add a CSS class or aria-disabled.

boolean disabled = nextButton.getAttribute("aria-disabled").equalsIgnoreCase("true");

if (disabled) {
    break;
}
I would inspect the actual DOM and use whichever indication the application provides.

Cucumber step :
	In my TestNG + Cucumber framework, I would keep the pagination logic inside the Page Object rather than putting Selenium loops inside the step definition.

@When("the user edits the record for {string}")
public void editUser(String email) {

    log.info("Editing user: {}", email);

    userPage.editUser(email);
}

This keeps the step definition readable and makes the pagination logic reusable.

How I would explain it in an interview

	If there are 20 records per page and the required record can exist on any page, I implement dynamic pagination. I search all rows on the current page using a unique identifier such as email or username. If the record is found, I locate the Edit button relative to that row and click it. If it isn't found, I check whether the Next button is available, click it, wait for the table data to refresh, and repeat the process. I stop when I find the record or reach the last page. I avoid hardcoding page numbers or row indexes because the record position can change


#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you retrieve all values from a dynamic web table?
==> “For a dynamic web table, I avoid hardcoding row or column indexes. I locate the table, identify all rows dynamically, then iterate through each row and retrieve the cells using relative locators.”

Selenium Java example

Suppose the table structure is:

<table id="userTable">
    <thead>
        <tr>
            <th>Name</th>
            <th>Email</th>
            <th>Role</th>
        </tr>
    </thead>
    <tbody>
        <tr>
            <td>John</td>
            <td>john@test.com</td>
            <td>Admin</td>
        </tr>
        ...
    </tbody>
</table>

I would write:

public void getAllTableValues() {

    WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

    WebElement table = wait.until(
            ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfElementLocated(
                    By.id("userTable")));

    List<WebElement> rows = table.findElements(
            By.cssSelector("tbody tr"));

    for (WebElement row : rows) {

        List<WebElement> cells = row.findElements(
                By.cssSelector("td"));

        for (WebElement cell : cells) {

            System.out.print(cell.getText() + " | ");
        }

        System.out.println();
    }
}
If I want all values as a Java List
public List<String> getAllTableValues() {

    List<String> values = new ArrayList<>();

    WebElement table = driver.findElement(By.id("userTable"));

    List<WebElement> rows = table.findElements(
            By.cssSelector("tbody tr"));

    for (WebElement row : rows) {

        List<WebElement> cells = row.findElements(
                By.cssSelector("td"));

        for (WebElement cell : cells) {
            values.add(cell.getText().trim());
        }
    }

    return values;
}
For a dynamic table with pagination

If the table contains only 20 records per page, I would additionally handle pagination:

public void getAllRecordsFromAllPages() {

    WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

    while (true) {

        List<WebElement> rows = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfAllElementsLocatedBy( By.cssSelector("#userTable tbody tr")));

        for (WebElement row : rows) {

            List<WebElement> cells = row.findElements(By.cssSelector("td"));

            for (WebElement cell : cells) {
                System.out.print(cell.getText().trim() + " | ");
            }

            System.out.println();
        }

        WebElement nextButton =
                driver.findElement(By.cssSelector("button.next"));

        if (!nextButton.isEnabled()) {
            break;
        }

        nextButton.click();

        wait.until(ExpectedConditions.stalenessOf(rows.get(0)));
    }
}
Industry-level approach :
	In my framework, I would keep this logic inside a Table/Page Object utility, rather than putting Selenium loops directly inside step definitions.
I would also consider:

1. Dynamic row count using tbody tr
2. Dynamic column count using td
3. Relative locators such as row.findElement(...)
4. Explicit waits instead of Thread.sleep()
5. Pagination handling
6. Empty-table handling
7. Logging the number of rows/columns retrieved

Short interview answer:

“I retrieve dynamic table data by locating all rows and then all cells within each row using relative locators. I don't hardcode row indexes because the number of records can change. If pagination is present, I iterate through each page until the Next button is disabled, collecting the data from every page.”


#====================================================================================================
# Q : A dropdown loads its values from an API after you click it. How would you automate it?
==> I would treat this as an asynchronous dropdown. The important point is that clicking the dropdown triggers an API call, so I would wait for the options to be populated, rather than using a fixed Thread.sleep().

Selenium Java approach
	Suppose the dropdown is:

<div id="countryDropdown">Select Country</div>
<div class="dropdown-options">
    <div class="option">India</div>
    <div class="option">USA</div>
    <div class="option">Canada</div>
</div>

I would implement it like this:
public void selectCountry(String country) {

    WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

    log.info("Opening country dropdown");
    WebElement dropdown = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable( By.id("countryDropdown")));

    dropdown.click();

    log.info("Waiting for API-loaded dropdown options");

    By options = By.cssSelector(".dropdown-options .option");

    wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfAllElementsLocatedBy(options));

    List<WebElement> countryOptions = driver.findElements(options);

    log.info("Number of countries loaded: {}", countryOptions.size());

    for (WebElement option : countryOptions) {

        String value = option.getText().trim();

        if (value.equalsIgnoreCase(country)) {

            log.info("Selecting country: {}", country);
            option.click();
            return;
        }
    }

    throw new NoSuchElementException(
            "Country not found in dropdown: " + country);
}

What I would check during RCA

If the dropdown sometimes fails, I would verify:

1. Click actually triggers the API request
2. API response status is successful, usually 200
3. Options are added to the DOM after the response
4. There isn't a loader/spinner still present
5. The dropdown isn't being re-rendered by JavaScript
6. The locator is targeting the current options, not stale elements
7. Network/API response time is reasonable in Jenkins/CI

For example, if there is a loading spinner:
dropdown.click();

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfElementLocated(By.cssSelector(".loading-spinner")));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.invisibilityOfElementLocated(By.cssSelector(".loading-spinner")));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfAllElementsLocatedBy(By.cssSelector(".dropdown-options .option")));

If it's a native <select>

If the API populates a real <select>, I would use Selenium's Select class:

dropdown.click();

wait.until(driver -> driver.findElements(By.cssSelector("#country option")).size() > 1 );

Select select = new Select(driver.findElement(By.id("country")));

select.selectByVisibleText("India");

API-level validation

For an industry-level framework, if this API is important to the test, I may also validate the API independently using Rest Assured:

given()
    .when()
    .get("/countries")
    .then()
    .statusCode(200);

Then the UI test verifies that the returned data is correctly rendered in the dropdown.

#-----------------------------------------------------#
Interview answer

“Since the dropdown values are loaded asynchronously from an API, I first click the dropdown and wait explicitly for the options to become visible or for the loading indicator to disappear. Then I retrieve the dynamically generated options and select the required value by visible text. I avoid Thread.sleep() because API response time can vary. If failures are intermittent, I check the API response, network timing, JavaScript re-rendering, and stale-element issues.”


#====================================================================================================
# Q : A search result appears dynamically after typing into a search box. How would you select the correct result?
==> I would handle this as a dynamic autocomplete/search suggestion. After entering the text, I would wait for the search results to be populated, then iterate through the suggestions and select the one that exactly matches the expected value.

Selenium Java example :
public void selectSearchResult(String searchText, String expectedResult) {

    WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

    By searchBox = By.id("search");
    By suggestions = By.cssSelector(".search-result");

    log.info("Entering search text: {}", searchText);

    WebElement input = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(searchBox));

    input.clear();
    input.sendKeys(searchText);

    log.info("Waiting for dynamic search results");

    wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfAllElementsLocatedBy(suggestions));

    List<WebElement> results = driver.findElements(suggestions);

    log.info("Found {} search results", results.size());

    for (WebElement result : results) {

        String actualText = result.getText().trim();

        log.info("Checking search result: {}", actualText);

        if (actualText.equalsIgnoreCase(expectedResult)) {

            log.info("Selecting matching result: {}", expectedResult);

            wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(result)).click();

            return;
        }
    }

    throw new NoSuchElementException(
            "Search result not found: " + expectedResult);
}
Why I prefer exact matching

Suppose the search results are:

iPhone 15
iPhone 15 Pro
iPhone 15 Pro Max
iPhone 15 Case

If the requirement is iPhone 15 Pro, I wouldn't simply use:

if (result.getText().contains("iPhone 15"))

because that could select the wrong result.

Instead:

if (result.getText().trim().equalsIgnoreCase("iPhone 15 Pro")) {
    result.click();
}
Handling results that appear after an API call

If the application shows a spinner while the API is executing:

input.sendKeys(searchText);

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.invisibilityOfElementLocated(By.cssSelector(".search-loader")));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.visibilityOfAllElementsLocatedBy(By.cssSelector(".search-result")));

This is better than:

Thread.sleep(3000);

because the API response time can vary.

If results are re-rendered
Sometimes React/Angular applications recreate the suggestion elements. In that case, I prefer retrieving the elements after the wait, rather than storing them before the API response:

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.numberOfElementsToBeMoreThan(suggestions, 0));

List<WebElement> results = driver.findElements(suggestions);

This also helps avoid StaleElementReferenceException.

Interview answer

“I treat it as an asynchronous autocomplete. After typing the search text, I explicitly wait until the dynamic results are populated. Then I iterate through the suggestions and compare their visible text with the expected value, preferably using an exact or normalized comparison. Once I find the correct result, I wait for it to be clickable and select it. I avoid fixed sleeps and handle re-rendering or stale elements if the application uses dynamic frameworks like React or Angular.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q :  An application uses infinite scrolling instead of pagination. How would you find an element that may be far down the page?
==> I would handle infinite scrolling by repeatedly checking the currently loaded DOM, scrolling down, waiting for new content, and stopping when either the target element is found or no more content is loaded.

I would not use a fixed number of scrolls, because the target could appear at different positions.
public WebElement findProduct(String productName) {

    WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

    By products = By.cssSelector(".product-card");

    long previousHeight = 0;

    while (true) {

        List<WebElement> productElements = driver.findElements(products);

        log.info("Currently loaded products: {}", productElements.size());

        // Search currently loaded elements
        for (WebElement product : productElements) {

            String name = product.getText().trim();

            if (name.equalsIgnoreCase(productName)) {

                log.info("Product found: {}", productName);

                ((JavascriptExecutor) driver).executeScript("arguments[0].scrollIntoView({block:'center'});", product);

                return product;
            }
        }

        // Get current page height
        long currentHeight = ((Number)((JavascriptExecutor) driver).executeScript( "return document.body.scrollHeight")).longValue();

        // No new content loaded
        if (currentHeight == previousHeight) {

            log.warn("No additional content loaded. Product not found: {}", productName);
            break;
        }

        previousHeight = currentHeight;

        // Scroll to bottom
        ((JavascriptExecutor) driver).executeScript("window.scrollTo(0, document.body.scrollHeight);");

        // Wait for new content
        wait.until(driver -> {

            long newHeight = ((Number)((JavascriptExecutor) driver).executeScript("return document.body.scrollHeight")).longValue();

            return newHeight > currentHeight;
        });
    }

    throw new NoSuchElementException(
            "Product not found: " + productName);
}

A more reliable approach

For applications where the page height doesn't change reliably, I would compare the number of loaded elements instead:
int previousCount = 0;

while (true) {

    List<WebElement> products =  driver.findElements(By.cssSelector(".product-card"));

    for (WebElement product : products) {
        if (product.getText().trim().equalsIgnoreCase(productName)) {

            product.click();
            return;
        }
    }

    int currentCount = products.size();

    if (currentCount == previousCount) {
        break;
    }

    previousCount = currentCount;

    ((JavascriptExecutor) driver).executeScript( "window.scrollTo(0, document.body.scrollHeight);");

    wait.until(driver -> driver.findElements(By.cssSelector(".product-card")).size() > currentCount);
}
Important considerations

In a real project, I would also handle:

1. Loading spinner → wait until it disappears.
2. Lazy-loaded elements → wait for the element count to increase.
3. Stale elements → re-fetch elements after every scroll.
4. End of content → stop when no new elements are loaded.
5. Target not found → fail with a meaningful assertion/message.
6. Virtualized lists → only currently rendered rows may exist in the DOM, so I would scroll incrementally rather than relying only on scrollHeight

Interview answer

“For infinite scrolling, I don't assume the target is already present in the DOM. I search the currently loaded elements first. If the target isn't found, I scroll down and explicitly wait for new elements to load. I repeat this until I find the target or detect that no additional content is being loaded. I also re-fetch elements after each scroll to avoid stale-element issues. This makes the approach reliable for dynamically loaded lists.”


#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you verify that a particular row was added to a table after submitting a form?
==> I would verify the row using a unique business value, such as email, username, or employee ID, rather than relying on the row number. After submitting the form, I would explicitly wait until the new row appears in the table.
public void verifyUserAddedToTable(String email) {

    WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

    By rows = By.cssSelector("#userTable tbody tr");

    log.info("Waiting for user to appear in table: {}", email);

    wait.until(driver -> {  List<WebElement> tableRows = driver.findElements(rows);

        return tableRows.stream()
                .anyMatch(row -> row.getText().trim().toLowerCase().contains(email.toLowerCase()));
    });

    List<WebElement> tableRows = driver.findElements(rows);

    boolean userFound = tableRows.stream()
            			.anyMatch(row -> row.getText().trim().toLowerCase().contains(email.toLowerCase()));

    Assert.assertTrue(userFound, "Newly added user was not found in the table: " + email);

    log.info("User successfully verified in table: {}", email);
}

For example, after submitting:

John | john@test.com | Admin | Active

I can locate the row using the unique email and then validate individual cells:

public void verifyUserRow( String email, String expectedName, String expectedRole) {     
    WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

    WebElement row = wait.until(driver -> {

        List<WebElement> rows = driver.findElements(By.cssSelector("#userTable tbody tr"));

        return rows.stream()
                .filter(r -> r.getText().contains(email))
                .findFirst()
                .orElse(null);
    });

    Assert.assertNotNull(row, "User row not found: " + email);

    List<WebElement> cells = row.findElements(By.cssSelector("td"));

    Assert.assertEquals(cells.get(0).getText().trim(),  expectedName);

    Assert.assertEquals(cells.get(2).getText().trim(),  expectedRole);

    log.info("Complete row verified for user: {}", email);
}
f the table has pagination

I would combine this with pagination handling. After submission:

1. Check the current page.
2. Search for the unique identifier.
3. If not found, click Next.
4. Wait for the table to refresh.
5. Continue until the row is found or the last page is reached.

Interview answer

“After submitting the form, I verify the newly created row using a unique identifier such as email or employee ID. I use an explicit wait to wait until that row appears in the table, then validate the required columns. I avoid hardcoding the row number because the table can be dynamically sorted or paginated. If pagination is present, I search across the pages until the record is found or I reach the last page.”

#====================================================================================================================================================
#  TestNG Scenarios
#====================================================================================================================================================
# Q : You have 200 TestNG test cases and 49 fail. How would you execute only the failed tests?
==> n a TestNG framework, I would use TestNG's testng-failed.xml file.

When a TestNG suite finishes, TestNG automatically generates:

test-output/
    testng-results.xml
    testng-failed.xml

The testng-failed.xml contains the tests that failed in the previous execution.

1. Run the failed tests

From Maven, I can execute the generated XML:

mvn test -Dsurefire.suiteXmlFiles=test-output/testng-failed.xml

Or from Eclipse/IntelliJ, I can right-click:

testng-failed.xml
→ Run As
