→ TestNG Suite

Only the failed test methods from the previous execution will be executed.

2. Example

Suppose I have:

Total tests = 200
Passed     = 151
Failed     = 49

After execution:

test-output/testng-failed.xml

will contain those failed test methods.

I execute that file instead of the complete testng.xml.

3. Important point

If some of those 49 tests pass during the retry, they won't need another retry.

For example:

First execution:
200 tests
151 passed
49 failed

Retry:
49 tests
40 passed
9 failed
I can run the newly generated failed suite again for those remaining failures.

4. In an industry-level framework

I would also use IRetryAnalyzer for transient failures:
public class RetryAnalyzer implements IRetryAnalyzer {

    private int retryCount = 0;
    private static final int MAX_RETRY = 2;

    @Override
    public boolean retry(ITestResult result) {

        if (retryCount < MAX_RETRY) {
            retryCount++;
            return true;
        }

        return false;
    }
}
@Test(retryAnalyzer = RetryAnalyzer.class)
public void verifyLogin() {
    // test steps
}
But I would distinguish between automatic retry and rerunning the failed suite. RetryAnalyzer handles transient failures during the same execution, while testng-failed.xml lets me selectively rerun failures from the completed suite.

Interview answer

“If 200 TestNG tests run and 49 fail, I don't rerun all 200 tests. TestNG generates testng-failed.xml under test-output, which contains the failed test methods. I execute that XML as a TestNG suite, so only the failed tests are rerun. In my framework, I can additionally use IRetryAnalyzer for transient failures, and after the retry I investigate any remaining failures through logs, screenshots, and reports.”


#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you generate a failed test-case rerun file in TestNG?
==> In TestNG, I don't normally need to manually create the failed-test rerun file. TestNG automatically generates testng-failed.xml after the suite execution.
1. Execute the main suite

For example:

<suite name="Regression Suite">

    <test name="UI Tests">
        <classes>
            <class name="tests.LoginTest"/>
            <class name="tests.CartTest"/>
            <class name="tests.CheckoutTest"/>
        </classes>
    </test>

</suite>

Run:

mvn clean test

After execution, TestNG generates:

test-output/
├── index.html
├── testng-results.xml
└── testng-failed.xml
2. What does testng-failed.xml contain?

If:

200 tests executed
151 passed
49 failed

TestNG creates testng-failed.xml containing the failed test methods/classes from that execution.

Conceptually, it looks like:

<suite name="Failed suite">

    <test name="Failed tests">
        <classes>
            <class name="tests.LoginTest">
                <methods>
                    <include name="invalidLogin"/>
                </methods>
            </class>

            <class name="tests.CheckoutTest">
                <methods>
                    <include name="verifyCheckoutTotal"/>
                </methods>
            </class>
        </classes>
    </test>

</suite>

The actual generated XML structure can vary based on the suite configuration.

3. Execute only failed tests

I can run the generated file from the IDE:

test-output/testng-failed.xml
→ Run As
→ TestNG Suite

Or configure Maven/Surefire to execute it, depending on the project setup.

4. If interviewer asks “How would you generate it programmatically?”
	I would mention that TestNG's normal mechanism is automatic generation. I wouldn't manually maintain a failed-test XML.

If I need custom failure tracking, I can use an ITestListener
public class TestListener implements ITestListener {

    @Override
    public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {

        String testName = result.getMethod().getMethodName();

        System.out.println("Failed Test: " + testName);
    }
}
But for simply rerunning failed tests, testng-failed.xml is the standard TestNG mechanism.

Interview answer

“TestNG automatically generates testng-failed.xml under the test-output directory after a suite execution. It contains the test methods that failed in that run. I can execute this XML as a TestNG suite to rerun only those failures instead of executing the complete regression suite. For transient failures, I can additionally use IRetryAnalyzer.”


#====================================================================================================
# Q :	How would you take screenshots only when a test fails?
==> With TestNG + Selenium, I would use a ITestListener and capture the screenshot inside onTestFailure(). This keeps screenshot logic centralized instead of adding screenshot code to every test case
1. Screenshot utility

public class ScreenshotUtils {

    public static String captureScreenshot(WebDriver driver, String testName) {

        String timestamp = LocalDateTime.now().format(DateTimeFormatter.ofPattern("yyyyMMdd_HHmmss"));

        String filePath = System.getProperty("user.dir")+ "/screenshots/"+ testName + "_" + timestamp + ".png";

        File source = ((TakesScreenshot) driver).getScreenshotAs(OutputType.FILE);

        try {
            Files.createDirectories(Paths.get(System.getProperty("user.dir"), "screenshots"));

            Files.copy(
                    source.toPath(),
                    Paths.get(filePath),
                    StandardCopyOption.REPLACE_EXISTING);

            return filePath;

        } catch (IOException e) {
            throw new RuntimeException("Failed to save screenshot", e);
        }
    }
}
2. TestNG Listener:
public class TestListener implements ITestListener {

    private static final Logger log =
            LoggerFactory.getLogger(TestListener.class);

    @Override
    public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {

        String testName = result.getMethod().getMethodName();

        log.error("Test failed: {}", testName);

        WebDriver driver = DriverManager.getDriver();

        if (driver != null) {

            String screenshotPath = ScreenshotUtils.captureScreenshot(driver, testName);

            log.info("Failure screenshot saved at: {}",  screenshotPath);
        }
    }
}
3. Register the listener

Using @Listeners:

@Listeners(TestListener.class)
public class LoginTest {

    @Test
    public void verifyInvalidLogin() {
        // test steps
    }
}

Or globally through testng.xml:

<listeners>
    <listener class-name="listeners.TestListener"/>
</listeners>

4. With parallel execution

Since my framework uses ThreadLocal<WebDriver>, I would always retrieve the driver through:

WebDriver driver = DriverManager.getDriver();

rather than maintaining a static shared driver.

This ensures that when tests run in parallel, the screenshot belongs to the correct test's browser session.

5. Industry-level improvement

In my framework, I would also attach the screenshot to Extent Report:
@Override
public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {

    WebDriver driver = DriverManager.getDriver();

    if (driver != null) {

        String path = ScreenshotUtils.captureScreenshot( driver, result.getMethod().getMethodName());

        ExtentTestManager.getTest().fail("Test failed").addScreenCaptureFromPath(path);
    }
}
I would also include:

Test name
Timestamp
Browser/environment
Screenshot
Exception/stack trace
Logs
Interview answer

“I use a TestNG ITestListener and override onTestFailure(). When a test fails, I retrieve the current ThreadLocal WebDriver and capture a screenshot using TakesScreenshot. I save it with the test name and timestamp and attach it to the Extent Report. This keeps the screenshot logic centralized and ensures screenshots are captured only for failed tests. For parallel execution, I use the thread-local driver so the screenshot belongs to the correct test.

#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you execute 10 Selenium tests in parallel using TestNG?
==> I would use TestNG parallel execution through testng.xml. Since Selenium WebDriver is not thread-safe, in an industry-level framework I would combine parallel execution with ThreadLocal<WebDriver> so every test gets its own browser instance.
1. Configure testng.xml
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>

<!DOCTYPE suite SYSTEM "https://testng.org/testng-1.0.dtd">

<suite name="Parallel Suite" parallel="tests" thread-count="10">

    <test name="Test1">
        <classes>
            <class name="tests.LoginTest"/>
        </classes>
    </test>

    <test name="Test2">
        <classes>
            <class name="tests.CartTest"/>
        </classes>
    </test>

    <test name="Test3">
        <classes>
            <class name="tests.CheckoutTest"/>
        </classes>
    </test>

    <!-- Other tests -->

</suite>
Here:

parallel="tests"
thread-count="10"

means TestNG can execute up to 10 <test> blocks concurrently.

2. Use ThreadLocal WebDriver

I would maintain one WebDriver per thread:
public class DriverManager {

    private static final ThreadLocal<WebDriver> driver = new ThreadLocal<>();

    public static void setDriver(WebDriver webDriver) {
        driver.set(webDriver);
    }

    public static WebDriver getDriver() {
        return driver.get();
    }

    public static void unload() {
        driver.remove();
    }
}
Driver initialization:

@BeforeMethod
public void setUp() {

    WebDriver webDriver = new ChromeDriver();

    DriverManager.setDriver(webDriver);

    DriverManager.getDriver().manage()
            .window().maximize();

    DriverManager.getDriver().get(
            "https://www.saucedemo.com/");
}
Cleanup:

@AfterMethod
public void tearDown() {

    if (DriverManager.getDriver() != null) {
        DriverManager.getDriver().quit();
        DriverManager.unload();
    }
}
3. Maven execution

If my testng.xml is configured with Surefire: mvn clean test

TestNG will execute the configured tests in parallel.

Important distinction

I would mention this in an interview:

parallel="tests"

runs <test> blocks in parallel.

Other options are:

parallel="methods"

Runs individual test methods in parallel.

parallel="classes"

Runs test classes in parallel.

For Selenium, I choose based on the framework design and test isolation.
What I would verify for parallel execution

Before enabling 10-way parallelism, I would ensure:

1. Each thread has its own WebDriver
2. No shared mutable test data
3. No static WebDriver
4. Scenario/test context is thread-safe
5. Reports are thread-safe
6. Screenshots use the correct thread's driver
7. Tests don't depend on execution order
8. Test data doesn't cause collisions
9. Browser/session cleanup happens in @AfterMethod/hooks

For my Cucumber + TestNG framework, I would apply the same principle to Cucumber scenarios and use a thread-safe DriverManager/ScenarioContext.

Interview answer

“I would configure TestNG's parallel and thread-count attributes in testng.xml. For 10 parallel Selenium tests, I can use parallel='tests' with thread-count='10'. The critical part is WebDriver isolation—WebDriver is not thread-safe, so in my framework I use ThreadLocal<WebDriver> to maintain a separate browser instance for each thread. I also make test data, scenario context, reports, screenshots, and cleanup thread-safe

#====================================================================================================
# Q : Your parallel tests randomly use the wrong browser session. What could be wrong?
==> I would treat this as a WebDriver thread-safety / shared-state issue. If parallel tests are randomly interacting with another test's browser, the first thing I would check is whether the WebDriver is shared between threads.

1. Static WebDriver is the most common problem

A problematic implementation would be:

private static WebDriver driver;

If 10 tests run simultaneously, all threads can overwrite the same driver reference.

For example:

Thread-1 → Chrome Session A
Thread-2 → Chrome Session B

Thread-1 sets driver = Session A
Thread-2 sets driver = Session B
Thread-1 gets driver → Session B  ❌

This results in tests randomly operating on the wrong browser.

2. Use ThreadLocal<WebDriver>

I would maintain one driver

public class DriverManager {

    private static final ThreadLocal<WebDriver> driver = new ThreadLocal<>();

    public static void setDriver(WebDriver webDriver) {
        driver.set(webDriver);
    }

    public static WebDriver getDriver() {
        WebDriver webDriver = driver.get();

        if (webDriver == null) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("WebDriver is not initialized for current thread");
        }

        return webDriver;
    }

    public static void quitDriver() {

        WebDriver webDriver = driver.get();

        if (webDriver != null) {
            webDriver.quit();
            driver.remove();
        }
    }
}
Then every test accesses:

DriverManager.getDriver()

instead of a shared static driver.
3. Check driver initialization

I would verify that driver creation happens inside the current thread:

@BeforeMethod
public void setUp() {

    WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver();

    DriverManager.setDriver(driver);

    log.info("Driver created for thread: {} | Session: {}",
            Thread.currentThread().getId(),
            driver);
}

And cleanup:
@AfterMethod
public void tearDown() {
    DriverManager.quitDriver();
}

I wouldn't create one driver in @BeforeSuite and share it across tests.
4. Check other static variables

Even with ThreadLocal<WebDriver>, I would look for shared mutable objects such as:

static WebDriver driver;
static PageObject page;
static ScenarioContext context;
static String username;
static String orderId;

For example, this is dangerous:

private static LoginPage loginPage;

if that page object stores a driver/session-specific state.

I would either create page objects per test/thread or ensure their dependencies are thread-safe.
5. Check Cucumber ScenarioContext

In a Cucumber + TestNG framework, this can also happen if scenario data is stored globally:

static Map<String, Object> scenarioData;

Two parallel scenarios can overwrite each other's values.

I would make the context scenario/thread scoped, for example:

public class ScenarioContext {

    private final Map<String, Object> data = new HashMap<>();

    public void set(String key, Object value) {
        data.put(key, value);
    }

    public Object get(String key) {
        return data.get(key);
    }
}

and create a separate instance for each scenario rather than sharing one static instance.

6. Check TestNG configuration

I would verify whether the framework is accidentally creating tests in a way that shares state.

For example:

<suite name="Parallel Suite"
       parallel="tests"
       thread-count="10">

The parallel mode itself isn't the problem; shared resources are.

7. Add thread/session logging

This is one of the first debugging steps I would use:

log.info(
    "Thread: {} | Driver: {} | Current URL: {}",
    Thread.currentThread().getId(),
    DriverManager.getDriver(),
    DriverManager.getDriver().getCurrentUrl()
);

If I see:

Thread 12 → Driver Session A
Thread 13 → Driver Session B
Thread 14 → Driver Session A

I know I have driver sharing.

With proper isolation, I expect:

Thread 12 → Driver Session A
Thread 13 → Driver Session B
Thread 14 → Driver Session C
RCA checklist
Area	         : What I check
WebDriver        : No shared static driver
Thread isolation : ThreadLocal<WebDriver>
Setup	         : Driver created per test/thread
Teardown	 : quit() + ThreadLocal.remove()
Page Objects	 : No shared mutable instances
ScenarioContext	 : Not static/shared
Test data	 : No parallel overwrite
Reports	         : Thread-safe Extent/TestNG handling
Logs		 : Thread ID + session information
CI		 : Check whether parallel workers share resources
Interview answer

“If parallel tests randomly use the wrong browser session, my first suspicion is that WebDriver is being shared between threads. A static WebDriver can be overwritten by another thread. I would replace it with ThreadLocal<WebDriver> and create and quit the driver within the same thread. Then I would check for other shared static objects such as page objects, ScenarioContext, test data, and reporting objects. Finally, I would add thread ID and driver/session logging to identify exactly where the cross-thread contamination occurs.”


#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you make WebDriver thread-safe when executing tests in parallel?
==> For parallel Selenium execution, I would make WebDriver thread-safe using ThreadLocal<WebDriver>. The key principle is one WebDriver instance per execution thread.

1. Thread-safe DriverManager
public final class DriverManager {

    private static final ThreadLocal<WebDriver> DRIVER = new ThreadLocal<>();

    private DriverManager() {
        // Prevent object creation
    }

    public static void setDriver(WebDriver driver) {
        DRIVER.set(driver);
    }

    public static WebDriver getDriver() {

        WebDriver driver = DRIVER.get();

        if (driver == null) {
            throw new IllegalStateException("WebDriver is not initialized for thread: "+ Thread.currentThread().getId());
        }

        return driver;
    }

    public static void quitDriver() {

        WebDriver driver = DRIVER.get();

        if (driver != null) {
            try {
                driver.quit();
            } finally {
                DRIVER.remove();
            }
        }
    }
}

2. Create driver for each thread
@BeforeMethod
public void setUp() {

    WebDriver driver = new ChromeDriver();

    DriverManager.setDriver(driver);

    driver.manage().window().maximize();

    log.info("Driver created | Thread: {}",
            Thread.currentThread().getId());
}

Cleanup:
@AfterMethod
public void tearDown() {

    log.info("Closing driver | Thread: {}",
            Thread.currentThread().getId());

    DriverManager.quitDriver();
}
3. Use the current thread's driver

Instead of:

driver.findElement(By.id("user-name")).sendKeys("standard_user");

I use:

DriverManager.getDriver()
        .findElement(By.id("user-name"))
        .sendKeys("standard_user");

Or in my BasePage
public abstract class BasePage {

    protected WebDriver driver;

    protected BasePage() {
        this.driver = DriverManager.getDriver();
    }
}
Then page objects can simply use:

driver.findElement(By.id("login-button")).click();

because the page object receives the driver belonging to the current thread.

4. TestNG parallel configuration
<suite name="ParallelSuite"  parallel="tests"    thread-count="10">

    <test name="Test1">
        <classes>
            <class name="tests.LoginTest"/>
        </classes>
    </test>

    <test name="Test2">
        <classes>
            <class name="tests.CartTest"/>
        </classes>
    </test>

</suite>
Conceptually:

Thread-1 → WebDriver-1 → Chrome Session-1
Thread-2 → WebDriver-2 → Chrome Session-2
Thread-3 → WebDriver-3 → Chrome Session-3
...
Thread-10 → WebDriver-10 → Chrome Session-10

So Thread-1 cannot accidentally use Thread-2's browser.

5. What I would avoid

❌ Shared static driver:

private static WebDriver driver;

❌ One driver created in @BeforeSuite and shared by all tests.

❌ Static page objects containing mutable WebDriver state.

❌ Static ScenarioContext or test data that can be overwritten by parallel tests.

Interview answer

“I make WebDriver thread-safe using ThreadLocal<WebDriver>. Each parallel thread gets its own WebDriver instance and therefore its own browser session. I initialize the driver in the test's setup, access it through DriverManager.getDriver(), and call quit() followed by ThreadLocal.remove() during teardown. I also make sure page objects, ScenarioContext, test data, screenshots, and reporting objects don't introduce shared mutable state. This prevents one parallel test from interacting with another test's browser.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q : Two parallel tests modify the same test data. How would you prevent data conflicts?
==> I would handle this as a test-data isolation problem. In parallel execution, each test should ideally have its own independent data so that one test cannot overwrite data being used by another.

1. Generate unique test data

For example, if two tests create users, I wouldn't use the same email:

public String generateUniqueEmail() {

    return "testuser_"+ System.currentTimeMillis()+ "_"+ Thread.currentThread().getId()+ "@test.com";
}

Then:

String email = RandomDataUtils.generateUniqueEmail();

userPage.createUser("John", "Doe",  email);

This could produce:

testuser_1760001234567_12@test.com
testuser_1760001234589_13@test.com

So parallel tests don't create the same record.
2. Don't use shared mutable test data

I would avoid:

static String username;
static String orderId;
static User user;

because Thread-1 can modify the value while Thread-2 is using it.

Instead, keep test-specific data within the test/thread:

User user = RandomDataUtils.generateUser();
or use a thread-local/context object when data needs to be shared across steps within the same scenario.

3. Use separate test-data records

If I'm reading test data from JSON/Excel/CSV, I can assign different records to different tests:

Test-1 → User-001
Test-2 → User-002
Test-3 → User-003
Test-4 → User-004

The tests don't modify the same record.

4. Create → use → clean up
For database-backed tests, I prefer generating the required data at runtime:

@BeforeMethod
      ↓
Create unique test data
      ↓
Execute test
      ↓
Validate
      ↓
@AfterMethod
      ↓
Delete test data

For example:

@BeforeMethod
public void createTestData() {

    testUser = userDataFactory.createUniqueUser();

    log.info("Created test user: {}", testUser.getEmail());
}

@AfterMethod
public void cleanupTestData() {

    userService.deleteUser(testUser.getEmail());

    log.info("Test data cleaned up: {}", testUser.getEmail());
}
5. If the data must be shared

Sometimes two tests genuinely need the same record. In that case, I would avoid modifying the shared record concurrently.

Possible approaches include:

1. Read-only shared data
2. Database transactions
3. Test-specific database schemas
4. Separate environment/database per test worker
5. Locking/synchronization for unavoidable shared resources

But I wouldn't use synchronization as the first solution because it can eliminate the benefits of parallel execution.

6. For API + UI automation

For example, in my Rest Assured framework, instead of having every test use:

username = "testuser";

I would create the test entity through the API with a unique identifier, use that data in the UI test, and clean it up afterward.

This makes the test independent of the initial database state.

Interview answer

“I prevent parallel test-data conflicts by isolating data per test. For create/update scenarios, I generate unique values such as email, username, or order ID using a UUID or timestamp combined with the thread ID. I avoid static mutable test data and assign separate records when using JSON or Excel. For database or API tests, I prefer creating the required data before the test and cleaning it up afterward. If a resource genuinely must be shared, I use read-only access, transactions, or controlled locking rather than allowing concurrent modifications.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q : You need to execute smoke tests before regression tests. How would you configure TestNG?
==> I would use TestNG groups and configure the execution order so that the smoke group runs first and regression runs afterward.
1. Assign groups to test methods
@Test(groups = "smoke")
public void verifyLogin() {
    // Smoke test
}

@Test(groups = "smoke")
public void verifyApplicationLaunch() {
    // Smoke test
}

@Test(groups = "regression")
public void verifyCheckout() {
    // Regression test
}

@Test(groups = "regression")
public void verifyProductSorting() {
    // Regression test
}
2. Configure testng.xml
<?xml version="1.0" encoding="UTF-8"?>

<!DOCTYPE suite SYSTEM "https://testng.org/testng-1.0.dtd">

<suite name="Regression Suite">

    <!-- Smoke tests execute first -->
    <test name="Smoke Tests">
        <groups>
            <run>
                <include name="smoke"/>
            </run>
        </groups>

        <packages>
            <package name="tests"/>
        </packages>
    </test>

    <!-- Regression tests execute after Smoke -->
    <test name="Regression Tests">
        <groups>
            <run>
                <include name="regression"/>
            </run>
        </groups>

        <packages>
            <package name="tests"/>
        </packages>
    </test>

</suite>
The execution flow will be:

Smoke Tests
     ↓
Smoke completes
     ↓
Regression Tests
3. Important interview point
	I would not rely on the order of methods in the Java classes. By separating them into <test> blocks in testng.xml, I make the execution sequence explicit.

If smoke tests fail, whether regression should still execute depends on the project requirement. If regression should not execute when smoke fails, I would implement a suite-level gate/listener or CI pipeline condition.

4. CI approach

For example, I can also keep two suites:
testng-smoke.xml
testng-regression.xml

and execute them sequentially in Jenkins: mvn test -DsuiteXmlFile=testng-smoke.xml

then, only if smoke succeeds: mvn test -DsuiteXmlFile=testng-regression.xml

This gives the CI pipeline a clear Smoke → Regression flow.

Interview answer

“I would use TestNG groups. I tag the required tests with groups='smoke' and groups='regression', then create separate <test> blocks in testng.xml, with Smoke first and Regression second. If regression must run only when smoke passes, I would enforce that at the CI pipeline or suite-level gating layer. This keeps test classification and execution order maintainable.

#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you execute a particular group of TestNG tests?
==> I would use TestNG Groups. I assign a group to the test methods and then include only that group in testng.xml.

1. Define groups
@Test(groups = "smoke")
public void verifyLogin() {
    // Login test
}

@Test(groups = "smoke")
public void verifyHomePage() {
    // Home page test
}

@Test(groups = "regression")
public void verifyCheckout() {
    // Checkout test
}
2. Execute only the required group

For example, to execute only smoke tests:

<suite name="Smoke Suite">

    <test name="Smoke Tests">

        <groups>
            <run>
                <include name="smoke"/>
            </run>
        </groups>

        <packages>
            <package name="tests"/>
        </packages>

    </test>

</suite>

Now TestNG will execute only:

verifyLogin()
verifyHomePage()

and skip:

verifyCheckout()
3. Execute multiple groups

I can include multiple groups:

<groups>
    <run>
        <include name="smoke"/>
        <include name="sanity"/>
    </run>
</groups>
4. Exclude a group

For example, run everything except regression:

<groups>
    <run>
        <exclude name="regression"/>
    </run>
</groups>
5. Maven command

I can also pass the group from Maven:

mvn test -Dgroups=smoke

For multiple groups:

mvn test -Dgroups="smoke,sanity"

This is particularly useful in Jenkins because I can parameterize the group:

TestGroup = smoke

and execute:

mvn clean test -Dgroups=${TestGroup}
Interview answer

“I use TestNG Groups to execute a particular set of tests. I assign groups such as smoke, sanity, or regression using @Test(groups = "smoke"). Then I include the required group in testng.xml, or pass it through Maven using -Dgroups=smoke. This allows us to execute only the required tests without modifying the test classes.”


#====================================================================================================
# Q : A test depends on another test, but the first test fails. What happens to the dependent test?
==> In TestNG, I can handle this using dependsOnMethods or dependsOnGroups.

If the prerequisite test fails, TestNG will skip the dependent test by default.
@Test
public void loginTest() {

    System.out.println("Login test");

    Assert.assertTrue(false); // Fails
}

@Test(dependsOnMethods = "loginTest")
public void checkoutTest() {

    System.out.println("Checkout test");
}
Execution result:

loginTest     → FAILED
checkoutTest  → SKIPPED

The checkout test isn't executed because its prerequisite failed.

Using dependsOnGroups:

@Test(groups = "login")
public void loginTest() {

    Assert.assertTrue(false);
}

@Test( dependsOnGroups = "login")
public void checkoutTest() {

    System.out.println("Checkout");
}
If the login group fails, the dependent test is skipped.

What I would check in the report
TestNG will typically show:

Failed  → loginTest
Skipped → checkoutTest

The dependent test is not marked as failed, because it was never executed.

Important interview point

I would use dependencies only when there is a real logical dependency.

For example:

Create user
     ↓
Verify user

The verification genuinely depends on successful creation.

But I would avoid:
Login test
     ↓
Checkout test

if each test can independently log in. In a well-designed automation framework, tests should generally be independent and isolated, especially when running in parallel.

Interview answer

“In TestNG, if Test B depends on Test A using dependsOnMethods or dependsOnGroups, and Test A fails, TestNG skips Test B by default. The report shows Test A as failed and Test B as skipped. I use dependencies only when there is a genuine business or technical dependency; otherwise, I keep tests independent so they can run reliably in parallel.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you retry only failed/flaky tests in TestNG?
==>	I would use TestNG's IRetryAnalyzer to automatically retry only the tests that fail. I would keep the retry count small, usually 1–2 retries, because retrying should handle transient issues, not hide real defects.

1. Create RetryAnalyzer
public class RetryAnalyzer implements IRetryAnalyzer {

    private int retryCount = 0;

    private static final int MAX_RETRY_COUNT = 2;

    @Override
    public boolean retry(ITestResult result) {

        if (retryCount < MAX_RETRY_COUNT) {

            retryCount++;

            System.out.println(
                    "Retrying test: "
                    + result.getMethod().getMethodName()
                    + " | Attempt: "
                    + (retryCount + 1));

            return true;
        }

        return false;
    }
}
2. Apply it to a test
@Test(retryAnalyzer = RetryAnalyzer.class)
public void verifyLogin() {

    // Selenium test steps
}

If the test fails:

Attempt 1 → FAILED
Attempt 2 → FAILED
Attempt 3 → PASSED

The test can ultimately be reported as passed, depending on the reporting setup.

3. Apply retry globally

In a larger framework, I don't want to add:

retryAnalyzer = RetryAnalyzer.class

to hundreds of test methods.

I can use a TestNG listener, typically IAnnotationTransformer, to attach the retry analyzer automatically.

public class RetryListener implements IAnnotationTransformer {

    @Override
    public void transform(
            ITestAnnotation annotation,
            Class testClass,
            Constructor testConstructor,
            Method testMethod) {

        annotation.setRetryAnalyzer(RetryAnalyzer.class);
    }
}

Register it in testng.xml:

<listeners>
    <listener class-name="listeners.RetryListener"/>
</listeners>

Now failed tests can automatically use the retry mechanism.

4. Add logging

In my framework, I would log every retry:

log.warn(
    "Retrying failed test: {} | Retry attempt: {}",
    result.getMethod().getMethodName(),
    retryCount);

I would also capture the screenshot and exception information when the test ultimately fails.

Retry vs rerun failed suite

These are two different things:

Mechanism	   :    Purpose
IRetryAnalyzer     :	Retry a failed test immediately
testng-failed.xml  :	Rerun failed tests from a completed suite
CI rerun	   :    Re-execute failures as a separate pipeline stage

For example:

200 tests
   ↓
10 failures
   ↓
IRetryAnalyzer
   ↓
7 pass + 3 still fail
   ↓
Investigate the 3 failures
Important interview point

I would not blindly retry every failure. If the failure is a genuine assertion failure, retrying it three times doesn't fix the defect.

I would use retries mainly for known transient issues such as:
1. Temporary network delays
2. Browser rendering timing
3. Remote WebDriver/Grid instability
4. Occasional stale-element conditions

But I would still investigate recurring failures through logs, screenshots, videos, and reports.

Interview answer

“I use TestNG's IRetryAnalyzer to retry only failed tests. I normally configure one or two retries and log each retry attempt. For a large framework, I can use IAnnotationTransformer to apply the retry analyzer globally instead of annotating every test. I distinguish this from testng-failed.xml, which is used to rerun failures after the complete suite finishes. I also make sure retries don't hide genuine application defects.”

#=============================================================================================================================================
#	Framework-Based Scenarios
#=============================================================================================================================================
# Q : You are asked to create a Selenium framework from scratch. How would you design it?
==> if I have to create a Selenium automation framework from scratch, I would design it to be maintainable, scalable, reusable, and easy to execute locally as well as through Jenkins.

I would use Java, Selenium WebDriver, TestNG, Cucumber, Maven, and follow the Page Object Model design pattern.
1. Project structure

I would create a Maven project with a structure like:

selenium-automation-framework
│
├── src/test/java
│   ├── base
│   │   └── BaseTest.java
│   │
│   ├── pages
│   │   ├── LoginPage.java
│   │   ├── HomePage.java
│   │   └── CheckoutPage.java
│   │
│   ├── tests
│   │   └── LoginTest.java
│   │
│   ├── utils
│   │   ├── DriverFactory.java
│   │   ├── ConfigReader.java
│   │   ├── WaitUtils.java
│   │   └── ScreenshotUtils.java
│   │
│   └── listeners
│       └── TestListener.java
│
├── src/test/resources
│   ├── config.properties
│   ├── testdata
│   └── features
│
├── pom.xml
└── testng.xml

2. Driver management
	I would create a DriverFactory responsible for creating and managing WebDriver instances.
public class DriverFactory {

    private static ThreadLocal<WebDriver> driver = new ThreadLocal<>();

    public static void initDriver(String browser) {
        if (browser.equalsIgnoreCase("chrome")) {
            driver.set(new ChromeDriver());
        } else if (browser.equalsIgnoreCase("firefox")) {
            driver.set(new FirefoxDriver());
        }
    }

    public static WebDriver getDriver() {
        return driver.get();
    }

    public static void quitDriver() {
        if (driver.get() != null) {
            driver.get().quit();
            driver.remove();
        }
    }
}
I prefer ThreadLocal because if I later enable parallel execution in TestNG, each test thread gets its own WebDriver instance.

3. Page Object Model
	I would keep locators and page-specific actions inside page classes.
public class LoginPage {

    private WebDriver driver;

    private By username = By.id("user-name");
    private By password = By.id("password");
    private By loginButton = By.id("login-button");

    public LoginPage(WebDriver driver) {
        this.driver = driver;
    }

    public void login(String user, String pass) {
        driver.findElement(username).sendKeys(user);
        driver.findElement(password).sendKeys(pass);
        driver.findElement(loginButton).click();
    }
}
This provides separation between test logic and UI implementation. If a locator changes, I only need to update the page class.

4. Configuration management

I would keep environment-specific information outside the Java code, for example:

browser=chrome
environment=qa
url=https://www.saucedemo.com
timeout=10

A ConfigReader would read these properties.

I would also allow values to be overridden from Maven/Jenkins, for example:

mvn test -Dbrowser=chrome -Denv=qa

5. Synchronization

I would create a reusable WaitUtils instead of using Thread.sleep().

For example:

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(10));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(loginButton));

Depending on the application, I would use visibility, presence, clickable, URL, text, or custom waits.

6. Test execution

For TestNG, I would have:

@BeforeSuite
@BeforeTest
@BeforeMethod
@Test
@AfterMethod
@AfterSuite

I would keep common setup and teardown in the base layer.
@BeforeMethod
public void setUp() {
    DriverFactory.initDriver("chrome");
}

@AfterMethod
public void tearDown() {
    DriverFactory.quitDriver();
}

7. Cucumber integration

If the project requires BDD, I would separate:

features
   ↓
Step Definitions
   ↓
Page Objects
   ↓
WebDriver

For example:

Scenario: Successful login
  Given user is on the login page
  When user enters valid credentials
  And clicks on login
  Then user should see the home page

The step definitions would call methods from the Page Objects rather than directly interacting with Selenium.

8. Failure handling and reporting

I would implement a TestNG listener to:

Capture screenshots only when a test fails
Capture failure information
Generate execution reports
Log important execution steps

For Cucumber, I would also configure Cucumber reporting.

9. Test data

I would separate test data from test logic.

Depending on the requirement, I could use:

JSON
Excel
CSV
Properties
Database
Cucumber Scenario Outline/DataTable

For example, for multiple login combinations, I can use a Cucumber Scenario Outline.

10. Maven and dependencies

I would manage Selenium, TestNG, Cucumber, REST Assured and other libraries through pom.xml.

Maven would also help with:

Dependency management
Test execution
Surefire configuration
Environment parameters
CI execution
11. Parallel execution

Once the framework is stable, I would configure TestNG parallel execution.

For example:

<suite name="RegressionSuite" parallel="tests" thread-count="3">

Because WebDriver is maintained using ThreadLocal, parallel tests don't share the same browser instance.

12. CI/CD integration

Finally, I would integrate the framework with Jenkins.

A typical execution would be:

Developer pushes code
        ↓
Jenkins job
        ↓
Git checkout
        ↓
mvn clean test
        ↓
Selenium/TestNG/Cucumber execution
        ↓
Reports + screenshots
        ↓
Jenkins result
My overall framework design

I would keep the framework layered:

Test / Feature Layer
        ↓
Step Definition / TestNG Layer
        ↓
Page Object Layer
        ↓
Utility Layer
        ↓
Driver / Configuration Layer
        ↓
Selenium WebDriver

“The main goal is that the framework should be easy to maintain and extend. For example, if tomorrow I add a new page, browser, environment, or 100 more test cases, I shouldn't have to duplicate existing code. I would focus on reusability, proper synchronization, clean separation of responsibilities, parallel execution capability, reporting, and easy CI execution.”



#====================================================================================================
# Q : How do you handle flaky tests in Selenium?”

==> In my experience, flaky tests are usually caused by synchronization issues, unstable locators, dynamic data, environment problems, or browser/application timing differences.

First, I identify the root cause instead of directly adding retries. For synchronization issues, I use explicit waits such as visibilityOfElementLocated, elementToBeClickable, or custom waits rather than Thread.sleep(). For dynamic elements, I use stable attributes or relative XPath/CSS selectors.

In my Selenium TestNG framework, I also implemented a Retry Analyzer for specific transient failures, but I don't use retries to hide genuine application defects. I capture screenshots and logs for failures so that we can perform RCA.

For example, if a test works locally but intermittently fails in Jenkins because the application takes longer to load, I check the failure screenshot and logs, identify the synchronization point, and introduce an appropriate explicit or custom wait.

#====================================================================================================
# Q : Why did you choose Page Object Model for your framework?
==> I chose Page Object Model, or POM, because it provides a clear separation between the test logic and the UI implementation. With 3.5 years of experience in Selenium automation, I’ve found it very useful for maintaining large automation suites.

In POM, I create a separate Java class for each important application page. The class contains the locators and page-specific actions, while the test class focuses mainly on the business flow and validations.

For example, in a Swag Labs application, I would have LoginPage, ProductsPage, CartPage, and CheckoutPage. If the login button locator changes, I update it only in LoginPage rather than changing it across multiple test cases.

The main advantages I get are:

Maintainability – UI changes can be handled in one place.
Reusability – Common page actions like login or adding a product can be reused across multiple tests.
Readability – Test cases become easier to understand because they represent business actions rather than Selenium commands.
Less code duplication – Locators and common operations are centralized.
Scalability – It's easier to add new pages and test scenarios as the application grows.

For example, instead of writing Selenium code directly in the test:

loginPage.login("standard_user", "secret_sauce");

This makes the test read more like a business flow.

I also combine POM with explicit waits, reusable utilities, TestNG/Cucumber, configuration management, and a DriverFactory. So POM becomes one of the core design components of the overall framework, rather than the entire framework itself.

One important point I would mention in an interview is that POM doesn't mean putting all logic into page classes. I keep page-specific UI actions in page classes and keep test/business validations in the test or step-definition layer.”

Short interview version:

“I chose POM mainly for maintainability, reusability, readability, and scalability. It separates UI implementation from test logic, so when locators or page behavior change, I can update the page class without modifying multiple test cases.”



#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you organize your framework packages?
==> In my Selenium Java framework, I organize packages based on responsibility, so that each package has a clear purpose. This makes the framework easier to maintain and scale.

A typical structure I have used is:
src/test/java
│
├── base
│   └── BaseTest.java
│
├── pages
│   ├── LoginPage.java
│   ├── ProductsPage.java
│   ├── CartPage.java
│   └── CheckoutPage.java
│
├── tests
│   ├── LoginTest.java
│   ├── ProductTest.java
│   └── CheckoutTest.java
│
├── stepdefinitions
│   ├── LoginSteps.java
│   └── ProductSteps.java
│
├── runners
│   └── TestRunner.java
│
├── utils
│   ├── WaitUtils.java
│   ├── ScreenshotUtils.java
│   ├── ConfigReader.java
│   └── ExcelUtils.java
│
├── factory
│   └── DriverFactory.java
│
└── listeners
    └── TestListener.java
And under resources:

src/test/resources
│
├── features
│   ├── Login.feature
│   └── Checkout.feature
│
├── config
│   └── config.properties
│
└── testdata
    └── testdata.json

The responsibility of each package would be:

1. base – common setup and teardown, browser initialization, and common test configuration.
2. pages – Page Object classes containing locators and page-specific actions.
3. tests – TestNG test classes containing test scenarios and validations.
4. stepdefinitions – Cucumber step definitions that connect Gherkin steps with page methods.
5. runners – Cucumber/TestNG runner configuration.
6. utils – reusable utilities such as waits, screenshots, configuration readers, and test-data utilities.
7. factory – WebDriver creation and management. I use ThreadLocal<WebDriver> here when supporting parallel execution.
8. listeners – TestNG listeners for failure handling, screenshots, logging, and reporting.

For example, my Cucumber flow would be:

Feature File
     ↓
Step Definition
     ↓
Page Object
     ↓
Utility / Wait
     ↓
WebDriver

This package structure follows the separation of concerns principle. So if a locator changes, I generally modify the pages package; if browser management changes, I modify the factory package; and if reporting behavior changes, I modify the listeners package.

This keeps the framework clean, reusable, and scalable, especially when the number of test cases increases.”

If the interviewer asks for a very short answer:

“I organize packages based on responsibility—pages for POM, tests for TestNG tests, stepdefinitions and runners for Cucumber, factory for WebDriver management, utils for reusable utilities, base for common setup, and listeners for screenshots/reporting. This gives clear separation of concerns and makes the framework easier to maintain.”


#====================================================================================================
# Q : Where would you keep WebDriver initialization and termination logic?
==> “I would keep WebDriver initialization and termination logic in a dedicated DriverFactory class, rather than directly creating the driver inside every test class.

For example, my framework structure would be: factory
   						└── DriverFactory.java

In DriverFactory, I would handle browser creation, retrieving the driver, and quitting the driver.
public class DriverFactory {

    private static ThreadLocal<WebDriver> driver = new ThreadLocal<>();

    public static void initDriver(String browser) {
        if (browser.equalsIgnoreCase("chrome")) {
            driver.set(new ChromeDriver());
        } else if (browser.equalsIgnoreCase("firefox")) {
            driver.set(new FirefoxDriver());
        }
    }

    public static WebDriver getDriver() {
        return driver.get();
    }

    public static void quitDriver() {
        if (driver.get() != null) {
            driver.get().quit();
            driver.remove();
        }
    }
}
Then I would call this from my BaseTest using TestNG hooks:
public class BaseTest {

    @BeforeMethod
    public void setUp() {
        DriverFactory.initDriver("chrome");
        DriverFactory.getDriver().get("https://www.saucedemo.com");
    }

    @AfterMethod
    public void tearDown() {
        DriverFactory.quitDriver();
    }
}
The reason I separate these responsibilities is that DriverFactory manages WebDriver, while BaseTest manages the test lifecycle.

I use ThreadLocal<WebDriver> because if I enable parallel execution with TestNG, each test thread gets its own browser instance and tests don't interfere with each other.

So, in my framework:

DriverFactory → WebDriver creation/management
BaseTest → setup/teardown lifecycle
Test classes → actual test scenarios”

#====================================================================================================
# Q :  How would you manage browser configuration for Chrome, Firefox, and Edge?
==> I would manage browser configuration centrally using a DriverFactory, and I would make the browser configurable through a properties file or Maven/Jenkins parameter instead of hardcoding it.

For example, in my config.properties I can have:

browser=chrome
environment=qa

Then my DriverFactory would create the appropriate browser based on that value:
public class DriverFactory {

    private static ThreadLocal<WebDriver> driver = new ThreadLocal<>();

    public static void initDriver(String browser) {

        switch (browser.toLowerCase()) {

            case "chrome":
                ChromeOptions chromeOptions = new ChromeOptions();
                driver.set(new ChromeDriver(chromeOptions));
                break;

            case "firefox":
                FirefoxOptions firefoxOptions = new FirefoxOptions();
                driver.set(new FirefoxDriver(firefoxOptions));
                break;

            case "edge":
                EdgeOptions edgeOptions = new EdgeOptions();
                driver.set(new EdgeDriver(edgeOptions));
                break;

            default:
                throw new IllegalArgumentException(
                        "Unsupported browser: " + browser);
        }
    }

    public static WebDriver getDriver() {
        return driver.get();
    }

    public static void quitDriver() {
        if (driver.get() != null) {
            driver.get().quit();
            driver.remove();
        }
    }
}
I would then read the browser from configuration:

String browser = ConfigReader.getProperty("browser");
DriverFactory.initDriver(browser);

I would also support Maven or Jenkins parameters, so I don't have to modify the code for every execution:

mvn test -Dbrowser=chrome

or:

mvn test -Dbrowser=firefox

In Jenkins, I can expose browser as a build parameter and pass it to Maven.

For browser-specific settings, I would keep them inside the DriverFactory—for example, headless mode, window size, download preferences, arguments, and certificates—using ChromeOptions, FirefoxOptions, or EdgeOptions.

For example, in a CI environment I might run Chrome in headless mode:

ChromeOptions options = new ChromeOptions();
options.addArguments("--headless=new");
options.addArguments("--window-size=1920,1080");

For larger cross-browser execution, I would also consider Selenium Grid or a cloud execution platform, where the browser and version can be specified through capabilities.

So my approach is:

Configuration → DriverFactory → Browser Options → WebDriver → Test

This keeps browser handling centralized, avoids duplicate code, and makes the framework easy to run across Chrome, Firefox, and Edge.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you pass browser and environment values from Maven to your Selenium tests?
==> I would pass the browser and environment as Maven system properties using -D parameters. This avoids hardcoding values in the framework and makes it easy to execute the same tests against different browsers and environments from Jenkins or the command line.

For example, I can run:

mvn clean test -Dbrowser=chrome -Denv=qa

Or:

mvn clean test -Dbrowser=firefox -Denv=staging

In my framework, I would read these values using System.getProperty():

String browser = System.getProperty("browser", "chrome");
String environment = System.getProperty("env", "qa");

Here, chrome and qa are default values if the Maven parameters are not provided.

I would then pass the browser to my DriverFactory:

DriverFactory.initDriver(browser);

And based on the environment, I would select the appropriate application URL:

String url;

switch (environment.toLowerCase()) {
    case "qa":
        url = "https://qa.example.com";
        break;
    case "staging":
        url = "https://staging.example.com";
        break;
    default:
        throw new IllegalArgumentException(
                "Invalid environment: " + environment);
}

DriverFactory.getDriver().get(url);

I can also configure defaults in pom.xml:

<properties>
    <browser>chrome</browser>
    <env>qa</env>
</properties>

Then Maven command-line values can override those defaults.

In Jenkins, I would create browser and env as build parameters and execute something like:

mvn clean test -Dbrowser=${browser} -Denv=${env}

This gives us flexibility to run, for example:

Chrome + QA
Firefox + QA
Edge + Staging

without changing the Java code.

So my overall approach is:

Maven/Jenkins → System Properties → Config/DriverFactory → Selenium Tests.

This makes the framework environment-independent, configurable, and CI-friendly.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you manage test data in your framework?
==> I would manage test data separately from the automation code. My approach depends on the type and volume of data. The main objective is to avoid hardcoding test data inside the test scripts and make the data reusable and maintainable.

In my Selenium, Java, TestNG and Cucumber framework, I generally use a combination of properties files, JSON/Excel, Cucumber Scenario Outline, and API-generated data, depending on the requirement.

For configuration data, such as URLs, usernames, timeouts, and environment-specific values, I use a properties file:
browser=chrome
qa.url=https://qa.example.com
staging.url=https://staging.example.com

For small sets of test data, I can use JSON or properties files.

For data-driven testing in TestNG, I use @DataProvider:

@DataProvider(name = "loginData")
public Object[][] loginData() {
    return new Object[][] {
        {"user1", "password1"},
        {"user2", "password2"},
        {"user3", "password3"}
    };
}
@Test(dataProvider = "loginData")
public void loginTest(String username, String password) {
    loginPage.login(username, password);
}

For Cucumber, if I need to execute the same scenario with multiple combinations, I prefer a Scenario Outline:

Scenario Outline: Login with different users
  Given user is on the login page
  When user enters "<username>" and "<password>"
  Then login result should be displayed

Examples:
  | username | password |
  | user1    | pass1    |
  | user2    | pass2    |

For larger or frequently changing datasets, I can store the data in Excel, CSV, JSON, or a database and create reusable utility classes to read it.

Another approach I use for dynamic data is to create test data through APIs before UI execution. For example, if a test requires a new customer or order, instead of depending on static data, I can create it using REST Assured and then use the generated ID in the Selenium test.

I also avoid storing sensitive information such as real passwords directly in the repository. Those values should come from secure configuration, environment variables, or Jenkins credentials.

So my approach is:

Configuration → Properties
Data-driven tests → TestNG DataProvider
BDD scenarios → Cucumber Examples/DataTables
Large datasets → JSON/Excel/DB
Dynamic data → API/database setup

This keeps test data separate, reusable, maintainable, and independent from the automation code.”


#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you capture screenshots and browser logs when a test fails?

==> In my Selenium Java TestNG framework, I handle screenshots and browser logs using a TestNG listener, mainly through ITestListener. This allows me to automatically capture debugging information whenever a test fails, without adding screenshot code to every test case.
I would configure my listener like this:

public class TestListener implements ITestListener {

    @Override
    public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {
        WebDriver driver = DriverFactory.getDriver();

        // Capture screenshot
        TakesScreenshot screenshot = (TakesScreenshot) driver;
        File source = screenshot.getScreenshotAs(OutputType.FILE);

        File destination = new File(
                "screenshots/" + result.getName() + ".png");

        try {
            Files.copy(source.toPath(), destination.toPath());
        } catch (IOException e) {
            e.printStackTrace();
        }

        // Capture browser logs
        LogEntries logs = driver.manage()
                .logs()
                .get(LogType.BROWSER);

        for (LogEntry log : logs) {
            System.out.println(log);
        }
    }
}

Then I register the listener with TestNG:

@Listeners(TestListener.class)
public class LoginTest {
    // test cases
}

Or I can configure it through testng.xml.

For browser logs, I also configure logging preferences while creating the driver. For example, with Chrome:

LoggingPreferences logPrefs = new LoggingPreferences();
logPrefs.enable(LogType.BROWSER, Level.ALL);

ChromeOptions options = new ChromeOptions();
options.setCapability(
        ChromeOptions.LOGGING_PREFS, logPrefs);

Then, when the test fails, I capture:

Screenshot — to see the exact UI state.
Browser console logs — useful for JavaScript errors, network-related errors, etc.
Exception/stack trace — from TestNG.
Test name and timestamp — to uniquely identify the failure.
Current URL and page title — useful during RCA.

In a CI environment like Jenkins, I would configure the screenshots and log files as build artifacts, so the team can download and analyze them after the execution.

One important point is that I prefer to capture screenshots only for failed tests, rather than for every step, unless the project specifically requires step-level evidence. This reduces unnecessary storage and keeps the reports clean.

My overall failure flow would be:

Test Execution
      ↓
TestNG detects failure
      ↓
ITestListener.onTestFailure()
      ↓
Screenshot + Browser Logs + Exception
      ↓
Reports / Jenkins Artifacts

This makes debugging much easier because I can see both what the UI looked like at the time of failure and what errors the browser reported.”


#====================================================================================================
# Q :	How would you generate HTML/Extent reports after execution?
==> In my framework, I would use Extent Reports or another reporting library to generate an HTML report after the TestNG or Cucumber execution. I prefer integrating reporting through listeners/hooks, so the report is generated automatically without adding reporting code to every test case.

For a TestNG-based framework, I would create an ExtentManager to initialize the report and an ITestListener to update the report based on test execution.

For example, conceptually:

Test Execution
      ↓
TestNG Listener
      ↓
Extent Report
      ↓
HTML Report

I would maintain a single report instance and create an Extent test for each test case.

public class ExtentManager {

    private static ExtentReports extent;

    public static ExtentReports getReport() {
        if (extent == null) {
            ExtentSparkReporter spark =
                    new ExtentSparkReporter("reports/ExtentReport.html");

            extent = new ExtentReports();
            extent.attachReporter(spark);
        }
        return extent;
    }
}

Then in my TestNG listener:

public class TestListener implements ITestListener {

    private ExtentReports extent =
            ExtentManager.getReport();

    private ThreadLocal<ExtentTest> test = new ThreadLocal<>();

    @Override
    public void onTestStart(ITestResult result) {
        test.set(extent.createTest(result.getName()));
    }

    @Override
    public void onTestSuccess(ITestResult result) {
        test.get().pass("Test passed");
    }

    @Override
    public void onTestFailure(ITestResult result) {
        test.get().fail(result.getThrowable());
    }

    @Override
    public void onTestSkipped(ITestResult result) {
        test.get().skip("Test skipped");
    }

    @Override
    public void onFinish(ITestContext context) {
        extent.flush();
    }
}

The important part is extent.flush(), because it writes the execution results to the HTML report.

I would also integrate the failure screenshot into the report:

test.get().addScreenCaptureFromPath(
        "screenshots/LoginTest.png");

So if a test fails, the report can show the failure status, exception, screenshot, test name, execution details, and other logs.

For Cucumber, I would configure the Cucumber runner/plugin to generate an HTML report, and if the project requires a customized Extent report, I would integrate an Extent Cucumber adapter/plugin.

In Jenkins, after execution I would archive the reports directory as a build artifact so the team can open the HTML report from the Jenkins job.

My reporting flow would be:

Selenium + TestNG/Cucumber
          ↓
     Listener / Hooks
          ↓
    Pass / Fail / Skip
          ↓
Screenshot + Exception + Logs
          ↓
     Extent HTML Report
          ↓
         Jenkins

“The main benefit is that the report gives the team a single place to see execution summary, passed/failed/skipped tests, failure reasons, screenshots, and logs, which makes defect analysis and regression reporting much easier.”

#====================================================================================================
# Q : How would you make your framework reusable and maintainable?
==> With 3.5 years of experience, when I design an automation framework, I focus on reusability, maintainability, scalability, and minimum code duplication.

I would achieve this in several ways:

1. Use Page Object Model

I keep locators and page-specific actions inside Page Object classes. If a locator changes, I update it in one place instead of modifying multiple test cases.

2. Create reusable utilities

Common operations should not be duplicated across tests. I would create utilities such as:
utils/
├── WaitUtils.java
├── ScreenshotUtils.java
├── ConfigReader.java
├── ExcelUtils.java
└── JsonUtils.java

For example, instead of writing explicit wait logic repeatedly:

WaitUtils.waitForElementToBeClickable(driver, loginButton);

3. Centralize WebDriver management

I use a DriverFactory for browser initialization and termination. If I need Chrome, Firefox, Edge, or parallel execution, I don't have to change every test class.

4. Externalize configuration

I don't hardcode browser, URL, timeout, or environment values.
mvn test -Dbrowser=chrome -Denv=qa

This allows the same framework to run against different environments and browsers.

5. Separate test data from test logic

I use TestNG DataProvider, Cucumber Scenario Outline, JSON/Excel, or database/API-generated data depending on the requirement. This prevents test data from being tightly coupled with the test scripts.

6. Follow proper package structure

I separate responsibilities into packages such as:

pages
tests
stepdefinitions
utils
factory
listeners
base

This follows the separation of concerns principle.

7. Use meaningful reusable methods

Instead of creating very large methods, I create small methods with a single responsibility.

For example:

loginPage.enterUsername(username);
loginPage.enterPassword(password);
loginPage.clickLogin();

rather than putting the entire login flow into every test.

8. Centralize waits and synchronization

I avoid Thread.sleep() and create reusable explicit/custom wait methods. This makes the framework more stable and avoids duplicate synchronization code.

9. Use listeners for common reporting activities

Screenshot capture, failure logging, and reporting should be handled centrally through TestNG listeners rather than being implemented inside every test.

10. Keep dependencies and versions centralized

I use Maven pom.xml to manage Selenium, TestNG, Cucumber, REST Assured and other dependencies. I avoid unnecessary duplicate libraries and keep versions controlled.

11. Support parallel execution

If regression execution needs to be faster, I design WebDriver management using ThreadLocal so the framework can support TestNG parallel execution safely.

12. Follow coding standards and review

I use meaningful class/method names, avoid duplicate code, keep methods focused, and perform code reviews before merging changes.

For example, if tomorrow I have 500 test cases and the login button locator changes, I should ideally update only the LoginPage rather than searching through hundreds of test cases. That's a good indication that the framework is maintainable.

So my overall approach is:

POM
  +
Reusable Utilities
  +
Driver Factory
  +
External Configuration
  +
Separate Test Data
  +
Listeners / Reporting
  +
Clean Package Structure
  +
Parallel Execution

“The key principle I follow is write reusable components once and use them across multiple tests. That reduces maintenance effort, improves consistency, and makes the framework easier to scale as the application and regression suite grow.

#====================================================================================================
# Q :  If 50 new fields are added to the application, how would you update your Page Objects efficiently?

==> If 50 new fields are added to the application, I would avoid modifying individual test cases one by one. Since I’m using Page Object Model, I would update the relevant Page Object classes centrally.

First, I would understand whether the 50 fields belong to one page or multiple pages. Then I would identify which fields are actually required for automation. I don't automatically create objects for every field if they are not used by any test scenario.

For the fields that need automation, I would add the locators and reusable methods to the appropriate Page Object.
public class CustomerPage {

    private WebDriver driver;

    private By firstName = By.id("firstName");
    private By lastName = By.id("lastName");
    private By email = By.id("email");
    private By phone = By.id("phone");

    public CustomerPage(WebDriver driver) {
        this.driver = driver;
    }

    public void enterFirstName(String value) {
        driver.findElement(firstName).sendKeys(value);
    }

    public void enterLastName(String value) {
        driver.findElement(lastName).sendKeys(value);
    }

    public void enterEmail(String value) {
        driver.findElement(email).sendKeys(value);
    }
}
But if there are 50 similar fields, I would avoid creating 50 nearly identical methods if the application structure allows a better abstraction. For example, I could create a reusable method:

public void enterField(By locator, String value) {
    driver.findElement(locator).clear();
    driver.findElement(locator).sendKeys(value);
}

Then I can maintain the locators centrally and use:

enterField(firstName, "Uzma");
enterField(lastName, "Khan");
enterField(email, "test@example.com");

If the fields are highly dynamic, I could maintain their locators in an object repository/configuration file or create a mapping based on stable field identifiers, rather than hardcoding dynamic XPath throughout the framework.

I would also use data-driven testing if all 50 fields need to be populated. For example, test data could come from JSON, Excel, or a database, while the Page Object handles the UI interaction.

My process would be:
Identify new fields
      ↓
Identify stable locators
      ↓
Update relevant Page Object
      ↓
Create reusable field/action methods
      ↓
Externalize test data
      ↓
Update only affected tests
      ↓
Run regression

The key point I would mention in an interview is: POM doesn't mean creating a separate method for every possible UI operation blindly. I use centralized locators + reusable methods + data-driven design so that adding a large number of fields doesn't create unnecessary code duplication.

If the 50 fields are completely different controls—for example text boxes, dropdowns, checkboxes, date pickers, and custom components—I would create reusable methods for each control type and keep the page-specific locators inside the Page Object.”

#================================================================================================
#  Real-Time Debugging Scenarios
#================================================================================================
# Q : Your Selenium suite takes 4 hours to execute. The team wants it reduced to 1 hour. What would you analyze first?

==> First, I would analyze the execution time breakdown—whether the 4 hours are caused by the number of tests, sequential execution, browser startup, waits, API/database calls, environment performance, or flaky/retried tests.

I would check the following:

1. Execution report
  a. Identify how many tests are running and their individual execution times.
  b. Find the top 10 slowest test cases.
  c. Check whether some tests are unnecessarily repeated.

2. Parallel execution — my first major optimization
  a. If tests are currently running sequentially, I would introduce parallel execution using TestNG.
  b. For example, if 4 hours is mostly independent UI tests, running them across 4–6 browser instances/threads can potentially bring execution close to 1 hour.
  c. I would ensure the framework is thread-safe, especially WebDriver, test data, and reporting.

3. Analyze Selenium waits
  a. Look for excessive Thread.sleep().
  b. Replace fixed sleeps with explicit waits such as WebDriverWait.
  c. Avoid unnecessarily high implicit waits because they can multiply execution time.

4. Browser and WebDriver overhead
  a. Check how often browsers are created and closed.
  b. Avoid unnecessary browser launches for every small test where the test design allows better reuse.
  c. Run headless where appropriate, especially in Jenkins.

5. Test case optimization
  a. Remove duplicate tests.
  b. Identify tests that don't provide enough value for every regression run.
  c. Separate smoke, sanity, regression, and full-suite execution.

6. Application/API/database bottlenecks
  a. Check whether tests spend significant time waiting for APIs, database operations, downloads, or application loaders.
  b. If setup data is created through UI, I would consider using APIs/database utilities where appropriate.

7. Retries and flaky tests
  a. Check whether failed tests are being automatically retried multiple times.
  b. A test that takes 2 minutes but executes 3 times can significantly increase the suite duration.

8. Jenkins/environment performance
  a. Compare local vs Jenkins execution.
  b. Check CPU, memory, network latency, browser version, and Jenkins agent capacity.
Example calculation

Suppose I have 240 tests, each averaging 1 minute:

Sequential:

240 × 1 min = 240 min = 4 hours

If I safely execute 4 tests in parallel:

240 / 4 × 1 min ≈ 60 min

So my first target would be parallel execution, but only after verifying that the tests are independent and the framework is thread-safe.

What I would say in an interview

“My first step would be profiling rather than blindly optimizing. I would analyze the execution report to identify whether the 4 hours are coming from sequential execution, slow tests, excessive waits, retries, browser startup, or environment bottlenecks. If the suite is mostly sequential, my primary optimization would be TestNG parallel execution with thread-safe WebDriver management. Along with that, I would remove unnecessary sleeps, optimize waits, reduce duplicate tests, control retries, and separate smoke/regression suites. I would then measure the execution time after each change and target approximately 1 hour without compromising test coverage or reliability.”

Key point: Don't say “I will simply increase the number of threads.” An experienced answer is measure → identify bottleneck → optimize → measure again.


#====================================================================================================
# Q : Your tests are stable locally but 20% fail in Jenkins. How would you investigate?
==> As a 3.5-year Automation & Development Engineer, I would treat this as an environment-specific flakiness issue. I would not immediately modify the test code.

Interview Answer

First, I would compare the local and Jenkins environments and identify whether the failures are consistent or random. I would analyze the Jenkins logs, screenshots, browser logs, and execution reports to find the common failure pattern.

I would investigate in this order:

1. Identify the failure pattern
Check whether the same 20% tests fail every time or different tests fail on every run.
Look at the exact exception:
  1. TimeoutException
  2. ElementClickInterceptedException
  3. NoSuchElementException
  4. StaleElementReferenceException
  5. Assertion failures
  6. Check the failure timestamps and execution sequence.
2. Compare Local vs Jenkins environment

I would compare
A browser/driver mismatch or different browser version can cause Jenkins-only failures.

3. Check headless execution

If Jenkins runs Chrome in headless mode, I would check:

1. Window size
2. Element visibility
3. Responsive layout
4. Popup/overlay behavior
5. Download behavior

For example, I would explicitly configure:
ChromeOptions options = new ChromeOptions();
options.addArguments("--headless=new");
options.addArguments("--window-size=1920,1080");
4. Investigate synchronization issues

Jenkins machines can be slower than local machines.

I would look for:

Thread.sleep(5000);

and replace it with condition-based waits:

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(15));

wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(loginButton))
    .click();

I would also check for AJAX calls, loaders, overlays, and dynamically rendered elements.

5. Check parallel execution

If Jenkins executes tests in parallel, I would specifically investigate thread-safety.

A common problem is sharing one WebDriver:

static WebDriver driver;

Instead, I would use a thread-local driver:

private static ThreadLocal<WebDriver> driver = new ThreadLocal<>();

Each parallel test should have its own browser instance and isolated test data.

6. Check test-data conflicts

For example, two parallel tests may try to:
 1. Create the same user
 2. Update the same record
 3. Use the same file
 4. Modify the same database data

I would make the test data independent or generate unique data for each execution.

7. Capture diagnostics on failure
I would configure Jenkins/TestNG listeners to capture:
 1. Screenshot
 2. Browser console logs
 3. Selenium/WebDriver logs
 4. Page source
 5. Exception stack trace
 6. Current URL

This helps determine whether the problem is Selenium, the application, or Jenkins.

8. Check Jenkins agent resources
I would check:
 1. CPU utilization
 2. Memory
 3. Disk space
 4. Network latency
 5. Number of parallel jobs
 6. Other processes running on the Jenkins agent

If 10 browsers are running on an underpowered agent, tests can become extremely slow and flaky.

9. Reproduce the Jenkins conditions locally
I would try to run the same tests locally using:

 1. Same browser version
 2. Same headless mode
 3. Same Maven command
 4. Same environment
 5. Same test data
 6. Same parallel thread count

This helps isolate whether the problem is code-related or infrastructure-related.

Strong interview conclusion

“If 20% of my tests fail only in Jenkins, I would first collect the failure pattern and compare the Jenkins and local environments. Then I would investigate synchronization, headless/browser differences, parallel execution and thread safety, test-data conflicts, and Jenkins resource constraints. I would enable screenshots and logs for every failure. Once I identify the root cause, I would fix the underlying issue rather than adding retries. Finally, I would rerun the suite multiple times in Jenkins to verify that the failure rate has actually reduced.”

Important: I would use Retry Analyzer only as a diagnostic/safety mechanism, not as the solution. Otherwise, Jenkins may show a green build while hiding flaky automation.

#====================================================================================================
# Q : The same test fails with different errors on different executions. How would you perform RCA?
==>  As a 3.5-year Automation & Development Engineer, I would treat this as a flaky test and perform RCA systematically rather than fixing each error separately.

Interview Answer

If the same test fails with different errors on different executions, my first suspicion would be a timing, synchronization, test-data, environment, or parallel-execution issue. I would first collect evidence from multiple failed runs and identify the common point of failure.

1. Run the test multiple times
	I would execute the same test independently, for example 10–20 times, and record:
  1. Exception type
  2. Failed step
  3. Execution time
  4. Browser/environment
  5. Screenshot
  6. Page source
  7. Current URL
  8. Browser console logs

For example :
Run	Failure
1	TimeoutException
2	ElementClickInterceptedException
3	StaleElementReferenceException
4	Passed
5	NoSuchElementException

Different exceptions can still have one underlying root cause—for example, the page is not fully ready when the test interacts with it.

2. Identify the common failure point
	I would compare the stack traces and test steps.
	If all failures occur around:

	Login → Search → Click Product

	I would investigate the Product page loading/search result rather than treating each Selenium exception independently.

3. Check synchronization
	This would be one of my first checks.
	I would look for:

Thread.sleep(3000);

or insufficient waits.
Instead, I would wait for the actual application condition:

WebDriverWait wait = new WebDriverWait(driver, Duration.ofSeconds(15));

WebElement element = wait.until(ExpectedConditions.elementToBeClickable(productButton));

element.click();

If there is a loader: wait.until(ExpectedConditions.invisibilityOf(loader));

4. Check for dynamic elements
   If the DOM is changing, I would investigate:
  1. Dynamic IDs
  2. AJAX requests
  3. React/Angular re-rendering
  4. Stale elements
  5. Overlays
  6. Loading spinners

For example, StaleElementReferenceException followed by TimeoutException may indicate that the page is still re-rendering.

5. Check test-data dependency
	I would verify whether the test depends on:
  1. Previously created data
  2. Existing user/account
  3. Database state
  4. Execution order
  5. Another test's result

A test should ideally be independent and repeatable.

For example:

Test A creates user
        ↓
Test B expects that user
        ↓
Parallel execution
        ↓
Test B sometimes fails

I would remove that dependency or create the required data during setup.

6. Check parallel execution and thread safety
	If the suite runs in parallel, I would investigate shared:
  1. WebDriver
  2. Test data
  3. Static variables
  4. Files
  5. Database records
  6. Environment configuration

For example, sharing a single static driver between parallel tests can cause unpredictable failures.
I would use a thread-safe driver management approach such as ThreadLocal<WebDriver>.

7. Check environment differences
	Since the failures may occur only in Jenkins, I would compare:
  1. Java version
  2. Selenium version
  3. Chrome/browser version
  4. Driver version
  5. OS
  6. Screen resolution
  7. Headless/headed mode
  8. Network
  9. CPU/memory
  10. Application environment

8. Use failure evidence
	I would configure a TestNG listener so that whenever a test fails, it captures:

Screenshot
    +
Page Source
    +
Current URL
    +
Exception
    +
Browser Console Logs
    +
Test Data

Then I can correlate the failures instead of guessing.
